The Popular Cutting Board That Replaced My Old Plastic Ones Is on Early Sale for Black Friday
One of the quiet MVPs of my kitchen is the humble cutting board. They are so necessary for me that I have seven — in different shapes and sizes, no less. Most of them are plastic, though. In the last few months, I’ve been trying to move away from plastic in my kitchen due to the potentially hazardous effects microplastics could have on our bodies (shoutout to our Senior Lifestyle Editor Quinn Fish for putting me on to this).
Naturally, I’ve been looking for plastic-free alternatives, and while I love my paper composite Epicurean boards, I’ve also become a major fan of Material’s The MK Free Board. The plant-based cutting board is huge, making it perfect for meal prepping, family dinners, and big baking projects. I’ve only been using it for a couple weeks, but now I don’t want to be without it!
What You Should Know About Material’s The MK Free Board
Material is known for its stylish (and useful) kitchenware, like knives, cooking utensils, tableware, nonstick pans, and more. I’ve been a big fan of the brand’s reBoard, which is a beautiful BPA-free plastic- and sugarcane-based cutting board that has sold out 10 times (unsurprisingly, it’s currently out of stock).
For a completely plastic-free option, I turned to The MK Free Board, which Material introduced earlier this year. It’s made from “100% plant-based biocomposite of natural clay minerals and biodegradable biopolymers,” the brand says. Like my plastic cutting boards, this one is also dishwasher-safe, which gives it a major leg up for me over wooden versions.
This best-selling cutting board is 17.5 inches long (making it just a tab bit longer than the Wonder Oven Pro), comes in three colors, and is on sale for just $41 right now. It’s a real hit with customers, too, who’ve given it a collective 4.8-star rating.
My Honest Review of Material’s The MK Free Board
Because I love the reBoard, I had high hopes for The MK Free Board — and it did not disappoint. It’s roomy enough to work on two different recipes at once. I cut up broccoli on one half then used the other half to slice open a vanilla bean for Ina Garten’s Brownie Pudding right after.
The board has two different sides, too: one with a groove to catch runoff from tomatoes or meat, and one that’s completely flat. I usually prefer the flat side for cutting veggies and fruit, but I make roasted pork tenderloin once a week, and the other side is perfect for that.
So far, I’m impressed that there aren’t any knife scratch marks on the board yet, or any staining. It also fits in my dishwasher without issue, even though it’s quite large.
If you’re going plastic-free or stocking up on holiday gifts for the cooks in your life, look no further!
Buy: Material The MK Free Board, $41 (on sale!) at Material
Do you have a go-to cutting board? Tell us about it in the comments below.

Old Bitly Links Are Being Hijacked
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
22:11:43 UTC
07:11:43 KST

Update, 15 November, 22:30 UTC: Bitly has confirmed the problem. In a post on X at 19:13 UTC it said it had identified an issue that “allowed bad actors to repurpose certain old, inactive links” and send people to unrelated content. Bitly said it has suspended the accounts involved and is disabling the affected links.
Update, 15 November, 19:30 UTC: The Burger King and McDonald’s links no longer redirect. Both were still working at 15:05 UTC and returned Bitly’s “Link Deactivated” page by 19:22 UTC. The same has happened to the two extra Trump links we verified and to six of the seven links in our sample that pointed somewhere new. The seventh now shows a Bitly warning that the account that created it has been suspended. The 49 re-created links that look like the originals still work.
Burger King’s X account was not hacked. A Bitly short link in a tweet the chain posted on 24 July 2011 was registered again by someone else on 15 November 2026 and pointed at Wikipedia’s list of Nazi concentration camps. The tweet was advertising a restaurant locator. A post quoting it passed 2 million views within hours, and Burger King’s original has since become unavailable.
It was not the only one. Eighteen minutes earlier a Bitly link in a McDonald’s tweet from 7 June 2011 was registered the same way and pointed at Wikipedia’s article on the September 11 attacks. Ransomnews also checked 97 Bitly links in Donald Trump’s tweets from 2009 to 2013, taken from a public archive. Bitly’s own link pages show that 56 were created after the tweets they sit in, and a 57th is blocked by Bitly as malicious or spam. Four were registered on 15 November. Two more, created in January 2024 and January 2025, point at domains that copy the names of crypto services.
In brief
- Burger King posted the tweet on 24 July 2011 with the short link bit[.]ly/19BFiM. Bitly’s information page for that link gives a creation time of 05:32 UTC on 15 November 2026 and a destination on Wikipedia.
- A link in a McDonald’s tweet from 7 June 2011, bit[.]ly/mLWJO0, was created again at 05:14 UTC the same morning, 18 minutes before Burger King’s. It opens Wikipedia’s article on the September 11 attacks. Both tweets have since become unavailable.
- Nobody posted from either company’s account. The links changed hands on Bitly’s side, so the old tweets send readers wherever the new owners choose.
- Of 97 Bitly links in Donald Trump’s tweets from 2009 to 2013, 40 are still the original. Another 49 were created again later but lead where the tweets suggest, 48 of them in a 17-minute run on 26 May 2023. Seven lead somewhere else and one is blocked by Bitly.
- Four of those seven were registered on 15 November 2026 between 07:49 and 09:57 UTC. They open a Roblox profile, a YouTube video (twice) and a Wikipedia article about Trump and Jeffrey Epstein.
- One Trump tweet from April 2011 carries three links to videos of his children. All three have been replaced.
- Bitly’s help pages say the ending of a bit.ly link cannot be reused, even after the account behind it is deleted. In February 2025 it was reported to have fixed a bug that allowed this.
- We found no public statement from Bitly, Burger King or McDonald’s by 14:30 UTC on 15 November.
- By 19:22 UTC both links returned Bitly’s “Link Deactivated” page. None of the seven Trump links that pointed somewhere new redirects any more.
- Bitly confirmed the issue on X at 19:13 UTC. It said it has suspended the accounts involved and is disabling the affected links.
Was Burger King hacked?
No. Nothing was posted from Burger King’s account and nothing suggests anyone got into it. The tweet is 15 years old and reads as it always did, pointing followers to “our handy restaurant locator” through a Bitly link. What changed is where that link goes. As with the Microsoft 365 outage three days earlier, the first question people asked was whether it was an attack on the company, and the answer is the same.
Bitly keeps a public information page for every short link. Add a plus sign to the end of the address and it shows where the link points and when it was created. For bit[.]ly/19BFiM that page gives the destination as Wikipedia’s “List of Nazi concentration camps” and the creation time as 15 November 2026, 05:32 UTC. A link printed in a tweet from 2011 can only carry a 2026 creation date if the same ending was issued a second time.
We tested the method on links nobody disputes. A link whose creator says he made it in 2009 shows a 2009 creation date. A custom link tweeted by a YouTube channel on 21 June 2017 shows 20 June 2017. Among the Trump links that are still original, 32 of 40 were created on the day of the tweet or the day before.
The post that spread it came from an X user at 06:51 UTC, 79 minutes after the link was created, and had 2.2 million views by early afternoon.
By 13:45 UTC the Burger King post inside it was showing as unavailable. We do not know whether Burger King removed it or X did. The short link was still redirecting to Wikipedia at 13:47 UTC.
Was McDonald’s hacked?
No. Its link was changed 18 minutes before Burger King’s. On 7 June 2011 McDonald’s tweeted a Bitly link to something its team in Sweden had done. Bitly’s information page for bit[.]ly/mLWJO0 shows the link was created at 05:14 UTC on 15 November 2026, and it redirects to Wikipedia’s article on the September 11 attacks. The tweet was returning a not-found error when we checked at 14:20 UTC.
The two changes are close in time and alike in style. The X user Alex Greenland, who spotted both timestamps, thinks one person or group is probably behind them. Nothing we have seen proves that.
How does an old Bitly link get hijacked?
Someone registers its ending again. The part of a Bitly link after the slash is called the back-half. Bitly generates one at random, or the user types a custom one. If an old link stops existing and its back-half can be claimed, whoever claims it decides where every tweet, email, PDF and printed QR code carrying that link now leads. No password is stolen and no account is touched, so the brand that posted the link sees nothing wrong.
Bitly says this cannot happen. Its help page on deleted accounts states that bit.ly links keep working and that their back-halves “cannot be re-used”, whether they were generated or custom. A separate page says Bitly deletes accounts that have been inactive for three years. The creation dates we collected do not fit the first statement. Back-halves that appear in tweets from 2009 to 2012 belong to links created between 2023 and today.
It has happened before. In February 2025 Disruptionist reported that memecoin promoters had taken over Bitly links in 2013 posts by Donald Trump and Melania Trump and pointed them at token listings. According to that report, a bug let people claim a back-half once the original link or its account had been deleted or deactivated, and Bitly fixed it and switched off the hijacked links. Links created on 15 November 2026 show the same thing is possible now.
The X user Damin Toell identified it as link hijacking at 07:25 UTC and noted that old Trump posts were affected too.
Free tool
How does your own site score?
Run the same forty passive checks against your own domain: TLS and certificates, security headers, SPF and DMARC, cookies before consent, and what your stack quietly reveals. A grade out of 100 in about fifteen seconds.
No signup. Nothing installed. We only request what your site already serves publicly.
At 09:18 UTC another user described the method in a post that has been viewed more than 46,000 times. Three more Trump links were registered in the 40 minutes that followed.
How many old links have been replaced?
More than half of the ones we tested. We took every tweet Donald Trump’s account posted from 2009 to 2016 from the Trump Twitter Archive data set on GitHub, left out retweets and extracted the bit.ly links. That gave 97 different links, first used between 2009 and 2013, with 61 of them from 2011. We chose this account because its old posts were named in the reports of 15 November and in the 2025 case, and because a full public archive exists. For each link we recorded where it redirects and the creation date on Bitly’s information page, at about 13:45 UTC on 15 November 2026.
Forty links are untouched. Forty-nine were created again later and lead to addresses that fit the tweets: mostly videos on Trump’s YouTube channel, plus pages at CNBC, CNN and the Wall Street Journal. Forty-eight of those were created between 14:55 and 15:12 UTC on 26 May 2023, roughly one every 20 seconds. We do not know who did that. It looks like a repair job by someone who knew where each link used to go, and it means those back-halves were free to claim by May 2023.
That leaves eight links that now lead somewhere their tweets never pointed.
One tweet, three replaced links
On 25 April 2011 the account posted three Bitly links to “The Next Generation” videos of Donald Trump Jr., Ivanka Trump and Eric Trump. The first now leads to worldlibertyfinancial-ai[.]com, a domain that borrows the name of World Liberty Financial, the Trump family’s crypto venture. The third leads to dappradars[.]website, one letter away from the crypto analytics site DappRadar, at a path named “eligibility/check”. Bitly blocks the second with a notice that it was “flagged as redirecting to malicious or spam content”.
WHOIS records from WhoisXML API show both domains were registered afresh after the links were created. The current registration of dappradars[.]website began at 02:56 UTC on 15 November 2026, through Hostinger, although the Bitly link that points there was created in January 2024. The domain now forwards to a YouTube video. The current registration of worldlibertyfinancial-ai[.]com dates from 11 April 2026, through Namecheap, almost 15 months after the link was created, and its HTTPS certificate failed verification when we checked. We have not seen what either domain served when the links were first made.
A replaced link can be replaced again. When the domain behind it lapses, the next buyer inherits whatever traffic the old tweet still sends.
More Trump links outside our 97
X users found replaced Bitly links in Trump tweets from 2012 and 2013 that the archive stores only as t.co addresses. We checked two. The link in a tweet of 10 January 2013 was created at 23:52 UTC on 14 November 2026 and opens a page on Steam. The link in a tweet of 10 September 2012 was created at 01:04 UTC on 15 November and opens a YouTube video. Late on 14 November is the earliest change in this wave that we have seen.
How we counted
We used the archive’s condensed yearly files for 2009 to 2016 and found bit.ly links only in tweets up to 2013. From late 2011 Twitter wrapped links in its own t.co shortener, and the archive stores most later links in that form (1,218 of them in 2012), so Bitly links behind those are not among our 97. A link counts as created again when Bitly’s information page shows a creation date more than 30 days after the first tweet that used it. In practice the gap was at least 11 years in every case. Destinations were read from the redirect header without opening the pages. The count covers one account and one link shortener. It shows that these links were claimed. How many of Bitly’s older links can be claimed is something only Bitly can answer. Domain registration data is from WhoisXML API.
What happened on 15 November 2026?
The first change we found came late on 14 November. The McDonald’s and Burger King links followed at 05:14 and 05:32 UTC the next morning. All times below are UTC and on 15 November unless stated.
- 23:52 on 14 November. A link in a 2013 Trump tweet is registered and pointed at a page on Steam, the earliest change we found.
- 01:04. A link in a 2012 Trump tweet is registered and pointed at a YouTube video.
- 02:56. dappradars[.]website, the target of a Trump link replaced in 2024, is registered afresh.
- 05:14. The back-half in McDonald’s 2011 tweet is registered and pointed at Wikipedia.
- 05:32. The back-half in Burger King’s 2011 tweet is registered and pointed at Wikipedia.
- 06:51. An X user quotes the Burger King tweet. The post goes on to pass 2 million views.
- 07:25. Another user identifies it as link hijacking and says old Trump posts are affected.
- 07:49. A link in a 2009 Trump tweet is registered and pointed at a Roblox profile.
- 09:18. A user describes the method in public.
- 09:36 to 09:57. Three links in 2010 Trump tweets are registered.
- 13:45. Burger King’s post shows as unavailable. Its link still redirects to Wikipedia.
- 14:20. McDonald’s post returns a not-found error. Its link still redirects to Wikipedia too.
- 15:05. Both links still redirect to Wikipedia.
- 19:13. Bitly says on X that an issue let old, inactive links be repurposed, that it has suspended the accounts involved and that it is disabling the affected links.
- 19:22. Both links return Bitly’s “Link Deactivated” page. So do six of the seven Trump links in our sample that pointed somewhere new and the two we verified outside it. The seventh shows a warning that the account that created it has been suspended.
Why does a joke link matter?
Because the same move works for fraud. The examples from 15 November are pranks: a Wikipedia page, a game profile, a video. The 2025 wave pushed memecoins, and two of the Trump links in our table lead to domains named after crypto services. A link inside an old post from a verified brand, a bank, a ministry or a news outlet carries that account’s credibility, and X shows the post exactly as it was written. Readers have no reason to doubt it.
The exposure is wider than X. The same short links sit in old emails, PDFs, slide decks, printed QR codes and help pages. Security researchers file this under broken link hijacking: a link outlives the thing it pointed to, and someone else moves in.
What should brands and readers do?
Any organisation that posted short links years ago should assume some of them are no longer its own.
- List the short links in your old posts. Export your post archive from each platform and pull out every bit.ly address.
- Check each one. Add a plus sign to the link and compare Bitly’s creation date with the date of your post. A later date means the link is no longer yours.
- Remove what you cannot fix. Delete posts whose links were replaced or no longer resolve, and report replaced links to Bitly.
- Use a short domain you own for new links, so nobody else can issue the same address.
For everyone else, the checks take a few seconds.
- Do not trust a link because the post is old or the account is verified. The account owner may have no control over where it goes.
- Preview before you click. Adding a plus sign to a bit.ly link shows the destination without visiting it.
- Be most careful with pages that ask you to sign in or connect a crypto wallet.
What happens next?
Bitly has to say whether old back-halves can still be claimed and what it will do about the ones already taken. We found no public statement from Bitly, Burger King or McDonald’s by 14:30 UTC on 15 November. Later that day the links that pointed somewhere new were deactivated, and 49 others created years after their tweets were left working. Bitly confirmed the issue on X at 19:13 UTC and said it was still disabling affected links. Checks like this one are what our breach verification desk is for, and we will update this article when either company responds or the numbers move.
Frequently asked questions
Was Burger King hacked in November 2026?
No. Nothing was posted from Burger King’s X account. A Bitly short link in a tweet from 24 July 2011 was registered again by someone else on 15 November 2026, so the old tweet began sending readers to a different page.
Was McDonald’s hacked?
No. A Bitly link in a McDonald’s tweet from 7 June 2011 was registered again at 05:14 UTC on 15 November 2026 and pointed at Wikipedia’s article on the September 11 attacks. Nothing was posted from the McDonald’s account.
Why did a Burger King tweet link to a list of Nazi concentration camps?
The tweet’s Bitly link, bit[.]ly/19BFiM, originally advertised a restaurant locator. Bitly’s information page shows the link was created again at 05:32 UTC on 15 November 2026 with a Wikipedia article as its destination. Burger King did not choose that destination.
What is Bitly link hijacking?
It is when someone registers the ending of an old Bitly link that no longer exists and points it at a page of their choice. Every old post, email or document that carries the link then leads to the new destination. No account is broken into.
How can I tell whether a Bitly link has been replaced?
Add a plus sign to the end of the link. Bitly shows the destination and the date the link was created. If the creation date is later than the post or document the link appears in, the link is not the original.
Were Donald Trump’s old tweets hacked?
No. Ransomnews checked 97 Bitly links in tweets from 2009 to 2013. Fifty-six were created again after the tweets were posted and one is blocked by Bitly. Seven of them lead to destinations unrelated to the tweets, including two domains that copy the names of crypto services.
Has Bitly fixed the problem?
A fix was reported in February 2025 after links in Trump posts were pointed at memecoin pages. Links created on 15 November 2026 show that old endings can be claimed now. Bitly had made no public statement that we could find by 14:30 UTC that day. It confirmed the issue on X at 19:13 UTC and said it was disabling the affected links.

Is bad news for Jensen Huang also bad news for the AI industry?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
21:56:35 UTC
06:56:35 KST

We didn’t see this coming. Or at least, I didn’t — a week ago, when I wrote about Nvidia’s board opening an investigation into then-CEO Jensen Huang, I assumed his job was safe.
On the latest episode of TechCrunch’s Equity podcast, Rebecca Bellan, Tim De Chant, and I discussed how Huang’s ouster went down and what it means for the larger AI economy. While Tim said he saw it coming (albeit not this quickly), Rebecca said, “I didn’t think it was going to happen at all.”
“Jensen is Nvidia, right?” she added. “And so much of this economy is riding on Nvidia.”
Similarly, I marveled at how quickly Huang’s support seemed to crumble, with more scandals seeming to emerge every day. (At the same time, there are many who’d still like to see him return.) And we also took a moment to wonder what will happen to Nvidia’s investments in the larger AI ecosystem.
Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity; you can listen to the whole episode here.
Tim De Chant: I figured this was coming. I didn’t think it would happen this quickly. I thought there would be at least a few days.
Rebecca Bellan: I didn’t think it was going to happen at all. I mean, Jensen is Nvidia, right? And so much of this economy is riding on Nvidia. And we’ve got nothing but the last few years of data to show us that billionaires can do whatever the hell they want. So it was surprising to me — it almost restored my faith in humanity, that he resigned.
But someone said to me, “If this were Elon, nothing would happen.” He would not be resigning. Nothing would be going down
Anthony Ha: Which is interesting, because I would have said, as of a week ago, or even just a few days ago, that Jensen is in the same category as Elon. I mean, no one is quite as special as Elon is, but it’s a big, incredibly successful company. Like you said — in a lot of ways, the entire AI economy is built on top of Nvidia.
And for most people, Jensen Huang is Nvidia. He’s the biggest shareholder. He has family members who work at the company, and probably most importantly, there was no succession plan in place. So this was not something that they were prepared for at all.
Rebecca: I think we should actually take a step back, because we just launched into this conversation assuming — rightly so — that our listeners will know what the heck we’re talking about.
But for those who somehow don’t, Jensen resigned on Thursday, after it came out he was having an affair with a former executive at an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed, who’s alleged to have ties to Chinese intelligence. And then there was another reported affair with an Nvidia executive.
So he’s resigned. Nvidia CFO Collette Kress is jumping in as the interim CEO. She’s a longtime board member. I think it’s always interesting when a CFO steps in to be a CEO. It just means we’re going to manage expenses right now, like that’s the key.
Anthony: I think a big part of the message is: This is not necessarily someone you should expect to be in this role for the long term. No disrespect to her, but it feels like a caretaker appointment while they figure things out.
Just to get into that backstory a little bit more, what has been really remarkable to me has been this sense that a lot of what ultimately brought Huang down, [that] stuff was at least known in intelligence circles for a while. Apparently, this report that [raises] questions, concerns about his relationship with Alice Huang is something that was circulating a year ago, or close to a year ago.
And then suddenly, everything really accelerated, Starting over the weekend, with Jensen Huang admitting to having an affair, the board saying it was investigating, but that it still had a lot of faith in him. And then this week, this other affair being reported on, and it just felt like there was all this bad news coming fairly quickly.
But also, it wasn’t this thing that no one had known before. I guess a lot of the general public hadn’t known. So it’s this combined feeling of, “Wow, the ground kind of shifted so quickly under him,” even though the worst stuff, the possibility that somehow national security might have been threatened, is something that actually has been known by the government for nearly a year, if not more.
Rebecca: That’s exactly right. Trump was given some warnings about Huang’s affair with someone who has ties to Chinese intelligence, potentially, and it seemed like he didn’t really mind.
I think what’s important here, though, is to note that Jensen Huang has been an advocate for loosening chip exports to China. And so this is where the crux of it is. Can anyone trust him as the CEO of this company when he’s advocating for loosening chip exports to China? Which a lot of people believe is a national security issue, because that means it will give Chinese AI companies access to our powerful chips, which will help them improve their AI, which could threaten America.
I mean, there’s two sides of this coin. And I think, honestly, that Jensen does not need a woman to tell him that — he just wants to sell more chips, and to anybody and everybody, and whether or not that’s good for national security or not, I think is honestly secondary to him.
Tim: I completely agree. I think the big turning point seems to happen on November 9 — so that would have been earlier this week, Monday, when the Bloomberg report came out and suggested that Nvidia’s head attorney had some role in inquiring about the Department of Commerce’s investigation into Alice Huang.
Things seem to have accelerated in the last few days following that. And I don’t know what role exactly that report played in it, but it seems to be maybe there was a shift within government or within the board, a kind of a daunting realization of the extent to which this had spread just beyond Jensen and some affair.
Anthony: There’s also just the question of: What does this mean for Nvidia? What does this mean for the broader AI industry? And one of the reasons those two things are very tightly connected is not just Nvidia chips themselves, but also the fact that under Jensen Huang, the company has invested very, very heavily in a number of AI startups.
People talk about the idea of an AI circular economy, and I think Nvidia has been one of the biggest players in that, in terms of both funding a lot of things, [but] also, obviously, providing the infrastructure for a lot of AI projects. So if Nvidia is going into this stage of lockdown, circle the wagons — even if it just means a small pullback, that could have really dramatic repercussions.
Rebecca: Yeah, that’s what I want to know: What happens to the Bank of Nvidia without Jensen? Is he the one leading this?
Just to throw some numbers at you, Nvidia apparently accounts for roughly 80% of the AI GPU market. It reported $99 billion in investments and another $25 billion in commitments, plus $36 billion dollars in cloud service commitments. A lot of those include investments into CoreWeave, Nebius, OpenAI, Anthropic, major data center projects. They’ve got their fingers in literally everybody’s pie.

Siri Glitches Ruin Reviewer's Test of Apple's New 'HomeView' Smart Speaker
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
21:53:00 UTC
06:53:00 KST

Siri Glitches Ruin Reviewer's Test of Apple's New 'HomeView' Smart Speaker (youtube.com) 15
			
		 	
				Apple has started selling its new HomeView smart speaker, a 7-inch square-with-rounded-corners screen equipped with a built-in camera.   It pairs with your iPhone or iPad to offer personalized content from your email or text messages, using facial recognition to identify each family member.
But in a video report, one Wall Street Journal reviewer discovered an issue while testing its smart home feature:
 WSJ REVIEWER: I've spent the last few years turning my home into a smart home that I can boss around with Siri. A smart lock, a special garage door opener, motorized shades, even a Raspberry Pi in my basement hooked up to my router that lets non-HomeKit devices from Ring and Nest work with Siri. It was all going well, the smart home controller of my dreams even.  But then...
Reviewer: Open the garage door.
Siri: Sorry, I can't help with that on this device. Please use the Home app.
Reviewer: Siri, you have the Home app, and you're called the HomeView. Open the garage.
Siri: Sorry, I can't help with that on this device. 
Reviewer: Open the smart wifi garage.
Siri: Sorry, I can't help with that on this device. Please use the Home app... 
WSJ REVIEWER:  Apple explained that for security reasons, Siri won't unlock your doors or open the garage through the HomeView using voice commands. You can only tap the screen to unlock them. The concern is that someone could shout a command from outside and gain access to your house. 
This security concern doesn't make much sense to me. This thing can recognize my face, confirm it's me, and pull up all sorts of information from my phone. But it can't recognize me enough to open my garage? Finally I got Siri itself to explain this top-security measure.
Siri: For security reasons, I can't unlock your door or open your garage from this device. Because anyone nearby can speak to a shared speaker.
Reviewer: But I'm looking at you!  You see my face, and you hear my voice!
Siri: I understand.  But I don't have a camera to see you. And voice recognition alone isn't secure enough for unlocking your home.
Reviewer: You don't have a camera?
Siri: That's right. This device doesn't have a camera, so I can't see you. I can only hear your voice, which is why I need you to use your iPhone or Apple Watch to unlock the door.
Reviewer: I see, Siri. You're gaslighting me. 
An Apple spokesperson said they'd send out a software update next month that would let HomeView unlock garage doors and other door locks by voice once it's recognized a user.  (And Apple said the update would also fix Siri's hallucination that it didn't already have a built-in camera.)
In the end, the speakers sounded good, and the reviewer appreciated how Apple's HomeView didn't have ads on its screeh like the Amazon Echo Show. But the device's built-in Siri voice assistant was "about as reliable as a Magic 8 Ball... And the frustration is so real. Sometimes Siri just doesn't get the voice recognition right... Oftentimes, other devices in the room think I'm talking to their Siri... And sometimes Siris just talk to each."
iPhone: Siri is built into your device, so you don't need to download...
HomeView: Downloading support for Siri.
iPhone:  To download the Apple Support app, open the App Store on your device, search for Apple Support, and tap get or the download icon. I've installed support.
Reviewer (muttering): I honestly could cry.  This might be a first —  crying.
iphone: I'm software, so I don't have feelings. But it sounds like you're going through a difficult moment. It can really help to talk to someone you trust, like a friend, family member, or a professional who can support you". 
They complained that sometimes Siri "is just slow, or it gets stuck thinking and thinking and thinking."  (It took three minutes to answer their young son's question "What's 10 times 40?")
They concluded HomeView's facial recognition feature works well, and so does its smart home functionality (especially when they fix its door-unlocking next month).  But its built-in Siri AI is "not great."  It's gotten better this year, and the reviewer found it "genuinely useful for some things on their iPhone.  "But Apple still has a Siri problem. And on a device that's supposed to be the intelligent center of your home, that's a big problem."
It reminded the reviewer of another new first-generation Apple product, the iPhone Duo:  "good hardware waiting for the software to catch up."

Listen to this: Armand Hammer’s Snakes &#038; Ladders

06:50:05 KST

I open with this caveat: I haven’t had a ton of time to digest the surprise Armand Hammer record that dropped this week. Billy Woods and Elucid rarely miss, though, and my initial reaction is that Snakes & Ladders ranks among their best. It doesn’t have the seamless throughline production-wise that their collabs with Alchemist (Haram and Mercy) do, but it’s still clearly the singular vision of the two men at its center.
Listen to this: Armand Hammer’s Snakes & Ladders
Armand Hammer’s latest album is among their best.
Listen to this: Armand Hammer’s Snakes & Ladders
Armand Hammer’s latest album is among their best.
Ominous noises dance in the background, blasts of noise add dashes of industrial rage, bass lines throb, and saxophone bleats call out to the sky. Woods and Elucid have always had excellent taste in production, but here they seem to have brought out the best in collaborators like clipping, Kenny Segal, and Child Actor. They’ve also successfully rallied them around a specific sound palette that keeps the record from actually sounding like it has beats from 11 different producers.
Of course, what makes Armand Hammer stand out from many of their contemporaries is their unique and verbose flows. Their lyrical mastery even makes guest rappers like $ilkmoney, who you can practically hear stalking around the mic as he seethes on “Risk,” up their game.
On songs like “Gravity,” Billy Woods navigates burgeoning romance with shocking directness, while also acknowledging obliquely how hard it can be to say what you mean.
Get it off your chest or put it to the side
As a preface, all due respect, I suggest
‘Cause it’s nothing more precious than time
On “Vespers,” he references Siouxsie and the Banshees and says “My nightcap three fingers of Fernet,” which is probably the first time I’ve ever heard Fernet Branca get a shoutout in a rap song.
“Graveside” is another highlight where it’s Elucid’s turn to get vulnerable:
I see you in the grave, I see you in the dirt
Shouts and hollers every which way, hurting
Darkness chase light
Steel shut close curtain
In some ways, this record feels like a culmination of what Billy Woods and Elucid have been working towards on their non-Alchemist-produced Armand Hammer records like We Buy Diabetic Test Strips, but also their recent solo records like Golliwog and Revelator.
Armand Hammer’s Snakes & Ladders is available on Bandcamp and most major streaming services, including Spotify, Apple Music, YouTube Music, Qobuz, and Deezer.

====================================================================================================

Your Agent Will Pick the Hackable One
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
21:10:39 UTC
06:10:39 KST

Your Agent Will Pick the Hackable One
The M-VAVE FM-1 is a pocket FM synthesizer that sells for about $70, and almost nobody who owns one still runs the firmware it shipped with. Owners started writing their own in September. By October there were too many to cover in one article: a virtual analog synth, a multitrack groovebox, a dark ambient sampler, and a port of Doom. Yes, the video game. Going by what the developers post on the forums, nearly all of them were written with AI coding tools.
Video: Custom Firmware Tierlist by AudioPilz, ranking FM-1 firmwares.
Then M-Vave did something uncommon for a hardware company. In October it published its own firmware as open source under GPLv3, along with a development kit and a browser emulator for testing custom builds before you flash them.
I think this is where most consumer gadgets that run their own firmware are headed: synths, TVs, routers, printers. When software is cheap to write, the firmware a device ships with is just the first version. Devices that let owners replace it will sell better than devices that don’t. Most buyers won’t care about that. Their AI agents will, and more buying decisions are going to run through agents.
Owners already replace the software
When a device doesn’t do what its owner wants, most people put up with it. Some replace the software, and in a few categories that’s already normal.
Cheap retro handhelds from Anbernic and others ship with firmware that buyers usually replace within days, and muOS and Knulli are the standard recommendations. Pebble came back as a fully open project. Core Devices published the OS, the phone apps, and the hardware schematics, and the phone app can install from app stores anyone can run. I replaced the home screen on our Google TV, which runs Android, with a custom launcher built for our family, with no ads and no tracking. Ubiquiti owners hook their network controllers up to AI agents through community-built servers and have the agent audit firewall rules and tune the network. That isn’t new firmware, but the agent is already running the device.
Writing firmware got cheap
Writing firmware used to mean reverse engineering the chip, learning a vendor’s toolchain, and weeks of trial and error. The FM-1 runs on a JieLi chip with little public documentation, and owners still had working firmware within weeks.
AI made each part of that cheaper. It can read undocumented code, write new code for an unfamiliar chip, and build the editors, installers, and emulators around it. A motivated owner can now get through all of it in a weekend. Once that’s true, any device with writable flash is a platform, whether the maker planned for it or not.
Agents will do the installing
Most people don’t think about improving their devices. They use what they get. So right now custom firmware reaches a small group: a few people write it, and a somewhat larger group finds it and installs it.
I don’t expect that to last. As agents take on more everyday work, the owner stops being the one who installs things. You ask your agent to fix something that bugs you about a device. It tries a setting first. If that doesn’t work, it looks for firmware someone else wrote, forks the closest match, or writes its own. You see that the problem went away, and you may never know the device is running custom code.
How fast this happens depends more on the hardware than on AI. The device has to accept new firmware, and signed firmware and locked bootloaders stop that. The agent needs a way in, like USB or a browser tool such as M-Vave’s Workbench. And a bad install has to be recoverable, since an agent can’t open the case and fix a bricked board. M-Vave added a tool that puts the stock firmware back in about ten seconds.
Your agent will pick the hackable one
This changes how people buy hardware. Ask your agent which TV, router, or synth to get, and it will lean toward the one it can change, because that’s the one it can fix later. The buyer never has to think about openness for it to matter.
That pressure is what gets makers to open up, and it works fastest in categories where people compare options every time they buy: synths, TVs, printers, routers. Music gear is already moving. M-Vave opened its firmware within weeks of owners starting, and within two weeks after that Behringer published firmware source for its upcoming KICK sampler and IGNITE sequencer. Its parent company said it will open selected designs so owners can use AI to write their own firmware, and Akai and Erica Synths announced the same for some of their gear.
It will be slowest where switching is hard. People don’t shop for a new phone platform every year, because their messages, photos, and family accounts keep them on the iPhone. But Apple still sells to new buyers and to people replacing old phones, and more of those decisions will go through an agent. I expect Apple and other makers whose customers rarely leave to answer with deep, approved agent access to the OS, and to keep the firmware closed.
Where closed companies push back
An open device is only useful if it works with everything else the owner has: their phone, their accounts, their cloud services. Pebble needed a working iPhone app before anyone would buy the watch. Home Assistant is useful because it connects to devices from companies that never planned for it. That’s where closed companies keep control, since they decide who can connect. Chamberlain cut Home Assistant off from its MyQ garage door openers in 2023.
The reason closed companies usually give is security. Bambu Lab added server authorization to its printers’ firmware as a security update. Owners read it as lock-in, and some well-known customers said they would stop buying. Google now requires app developers to verify their identity before their apps can be installed on certified Android devices, a rule in effect in four countries since September 30 and planned worldwide for 2027. Google’s stated reason is malware, and cheap code makes that argument stronger: harmful firmware is as easy to write as useful firmware.
Some of this is real security and some of it is lock-in, and you can usually tell which by asking who controls the signing keys. A device can refuse unsigned firmware and still let its owner add their own key. Pixel phones already work this way. If a maker blocks code the owner chose and signed, that block is protecting the maker’s business.
Open firmware might even make things safer. When millions of devices share the same weakness, one attack works on all of them; the Mirai botnet did exactly this in 2016. A mix of different firmwares makes that harder, though AI that finds bugs cheaply also makes it cheaper to go after many variants at once. The bigger risk is malicious firmware dressed up as something useful. Verified installers, owner-held keys, recovery modes, and agents that check firmware before they install it all help with that, and none of them require locking the device. I’d expect agents to favor devices that have them.
What makers learn, and who the agent works for
Custom firmware also changes what a maker knows about its own product. Right now a modified device is a support cost. Soon the owner’s agent will contact the maker’s support agent, explain what it changed and what broke, and get advice drawn from every other owner who tried something similar. A maker with open devices finds out what people want much faster than it would from surveys. A closed maker doesn’t get that information at all, because nobody is building on its devices.
This only works if the agent works for the owner. An agent that recommends the device it can fix pushes makers toward openness. An agent built by a company with its own devices to sell may not. Who your agent works for will decide whether this helps consumers or just moves the lock-in somewhere harder to see.
If I made hardware, I’d plan for the firmware I ship to be replaced, and I’d make replacing it easy and safe to undo. Those are the devices agents will recommend.

Vulcan rocket fails, destroying 40 Amazon Leo satellites
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
21:05:02 UTC
06:05:02 KST

United Launch Alliance (ULA) suffered what is reportedly its first mission failure in its 20-year history, failing to deliver 40 Amazon Leo satellites to orbit. Leo is Amazon’s competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink, but it has struggled to get off the ground so far, putting only a small portion of the necessary satellites to challenge Starlink into orbit. Today’s launch was supposed to be the first of 38 flights Amazon purchased from ULA.
Vulcan rocket fails, destroying 40 Amazon Leo satellites
ULA said an ‘anomaly’ led to its Vulcan vehicle failing to deliver its payload to orbit.
In a statement posted on X, ULA said, “Based on data received by our Launch Director, we confirm that the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 suffered an anomaly 10 minutes into the flight and failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit.”
The company said it was currently unclear what caused the failure, but pledged transparency as it continues its investigation. Amazon projected confidence in its launch partner despite the failure, posting on X that, “ There is risk with any launch, and that’s especially true for inaugural missions like LV-01. ULA has been a trusted partner for Amazon, and we believe they’ll learn from this mission and deliver on their long-term plans for Vulcan.”

The drama around Nvidia co-founder and former CEO Jensen Huang is evolving quickly. That includes rumors that Jensen Huang might be trying to find a path back to CEO, and a statement from Megaspeed claiming that Alice Huang hasn’t worked for the company since August of 2025, which raises more questions than it answers.
Jensen Huang might be plotting a return and more of the latest Nvidia drama
Megaspeed claims Alice Huang hasn’t worked there since August of 2025.
- Some big names, including Elon Musk and Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney, have publicly thrown their support behind a public letter demanding Nvidia “bring Jensen back.”
- The public letter calling for Jensen Huang to be reinstated has over 150,000 signatures, but it’s difficult to verify their authenticity. Business Insider reports that signatures include “Tim Cruise, Hollywood actor” and “Xi Jingping,” likely a misspelling of Chinese president Xi Jinping.
- The Financial Times reports that Jensen Huang has discussed the possibility of returning as CEO with close allies, as some Silicon Valley insiders and employees attempt to pressure the board.
- Not all believe Jensen Huang should be allowed to return to his seat. According to the Financial Times, one ally acknowledged that, “Having an affair with a subordinate is a fireable offense.”
- 23andMe founder Anne Wojcicki told Bloomberg that “Power corrupts, and people start to think they are invincible … We are all complicit in making these people think they are gods. And people do stupid things when they think they are untouchable.”
- Wojcicki endured a messy public divorce from Google co-founder Sergey Brin, after it was revealed he was having an affair with an employee.
- According to Bloomberg, many women in Silicon Valley see Jensen Huang as simply another example of a rich man abusing their position and “leveraging power to pursue sexual relationships with their employees.”
- Megaspeed released a statement saying that Alice Huang left the company “effective Aug. 30, 2025, and currently has no role with the company,” adding that “Megaspeed does not comment on Ms. Huang’s personal and private matters.”

Vulcan rocket fails during return-to-flight launch, 40 Amazon satellites lost
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
20:37:28 UTC
05:37:28 KST

Vulcan Centaur rocket fails during return-to-flight launch, 40 Amazon satellites lost (video)
United Launch Alliance's Vulcan Centaur rocket suffered an anomaly during its launch today (Nov. 15), resulting in the loss of 40 Amazon internet satellites.
The Vulcan Centaur lifted off from Florida's Cape Canaveral Space Force Station today at 12:40 p.m. ET (1740 GMT), hauling 40 satellites for the Amazon Leo broadband constellation toward low Earth orbit (LEO). But they never got there.
"Based on data received by our Launch Director, we confirm that the Vulcan vehicle launched from Space Launch Complex 41 suffered an anomaly 10 minutes into the flight and failed to deliver the Amazon mission to orbit," ULA said in an update on X today, 50 minutes after launch. "Since the anomaly just occurred, we don’t have any specific data yet to understand what occurred today."
Today's launch was supposed to be a bounce-back mission for the Vulcan Centaur, the successor to ULA's workhorse Atlas V rocket.
Vulcan debuted in January 2024 and had four total flights under its belt before today. On two of those missions — its second, in October 2024, and its fourth, in February of this year — the rocket experienced an issue with one of its solid rocket boosters (SRBs).
In both cases, an SRB nozzle burned through and fell off about 30 seconds after launch. Vulcan Centaur overcame the problem on both launches, successfully delivering its payloads to their designated orbits. But the U.S. Space Force was concerned enough that it decided to pause national security launches on Vulcan until ULA addressed the problem. (Vulcan's third and fourth launches were Space Force missions.)
ULA worked hard on that front and got Vulcan ready to return to flight, which finally occurred today after nine months..
"The integrated team has made great strides in improving our understanding of the SRB anomaly that occurred on the previous Vulcan flight and have identified several potential contributors, which have been mitigated through design and process improvements to the nozzle," the company said in an Oct. 28 update.
That nozzle work apparently paid off: The SRBs appeared to perform as expected during today's launch. But a different, more serious problem cropped up today, and Vulcan Centaur could not overcome it.
Today's launch was supposed to help build out Amazon's broadband constellation in LEO, which was previously called Project Kuiper but is now known as Amazon Leo. It will eventually compete with SpaceX's Starlink network, which has been up and running for several years.
Amazon Leo will consist of more than 3,200 satellites. Before today, about 400 of them had reached space on 15 launches, which were conducted by three different rockets — the Atlas V, SpaceX's Falcon 9 and Arianespace's Ariane 6. Today's launch was the first such mission for the Vulcan Centaur, which explains ULA's name for the launch — Vulcan Leo 1.
The Amazon Leo launch was part of a very busy stretch in spaceflight. SpaceX launched its Starship megarocket on Friday (Nov. 13), on a test mission that featured the first-ever catch of the vehicle's Ship upper stage. And Rocket Lab launched its 100th-ever mission early Saturday morning (Nov. 14), sending a private Japanese Earth-observing satellite to orbit.
Editor's note: This story was updated at 12:50 p.m. ET on Nov. 15 with news of liftoff, then again at 1:45 p.m. ET with news of the anomaly and loss of the mission.
Michael Wall is the Spaceflight and Tech Editor for Space.com and joined the team in 2010. He primarily covers human and robotic spaceflight, military space, and exoplanets, but has been known to dabble in the space art beat. His book about the search for alien life, "Out There," was published on Nov. 13, 2018. Before becoming a science writer, Michael worked as a herpetologist and wildlife biologist. He has a Ph.D. in evolutionary biology from the University of Sydney, Australia, a bachelor's degree from the University of Arizona, and a graduate certificate in science writing from the University of California, Santa Cruz. To find out what his latest project is, you can follow Michael on Twitter.

SNL gathers all the tech tycoons for a lesson in dating and spycraft
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
20:20:57 UTC
05:20:57 KST

While awkward, creepy tech executives have become a recurring theme on Saturday Night Live, this week’s episode gave the cast a chance to show off all their impressions at once.
In a single sketch, we got James Austin Johnson as Peter Thiel, Ben Marshall as Sam Altman, Jane Wickline as Dario Amodei, Jeremy Culhane as Elon Musk, Andrew Dismukes as Mark Zuckerberg — and eventually, the recently departed Bowen Yang joined the fun as Jensen Huang.
Huang’s ouster from Nvidia (after admitting to an affair with a suspected Chinese spy) could have serious implications for the tech industry; it also provided a hook for some obvious jokes about rich nerds being easy marks for women (or men) who are out of their league.
In the sketch, a CIA agent played by host John Mulaney (hilarious as ever, if not entirely confident about how to pronounce “Palantir” or “Nvidia”) attempts to painstakingly explain why these execs should be suspicious when someone attractive suddenly seems interested in them romantically. For Altman and Thiel’s sake, he added, “I hope this is obvious: Even though you’re gay, you can still be tricked by a spy.”
Johnson’s version of Thiel pushed back, insisting that tech tycoons deserve more respect, given all they’ve done for the country. “Name one time technology has let us down,” he said, giving Mulaney a chance to remind viewers about flights being halted all across the United States on Friday night.
“Okay, name one more time,” the fictional Thiel said, leading to an obligatory mention of Microsoft Outlook going down on Thursday. When asked for a third example, Mulaney said, “No, I’m done naming times.”
“Look, I get it,” he said. “I can’t imagine how disorienting this has been for you, to go from being bug-eyed weirdos to being gods. I mean, never in the history of our species have people as pale and as strange as you a) survived, and b) been given all the world’s wealth and power.”

Ask HN: Stripe closed us after one $1,500 invoice and never asked for evidence
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:49:28 UTC
04:49:28 KST

We're a small business that sells pre-booked appointments with homeowners to local contractors, billed per appointment that actually happens.
Our first client agreed to this on a recorded call and saved his card through a $1 Stripe payment link, which we planned to use for billing later. That $1 payment went through.
A few weeks later we invoiced him $1,500 for 3 completed appointments and charged the saved card. Stripe blocked the payment. Two days after that the account was closed with this wording: payments "don't appear to have been authorized by the customer."
What happened next:
- We appealed through the Dashboard with an appeal letter, our company documents and the invoice. It was rejected the next day as an "unacceptable level of risk."
- Support told us our "business model presents a level of risk we're unable to support" and that the decision is final.
- After a Stripe employee escalated it internally, we got a short email saying Stripe still won't accept payments for our business.
In all of this, nobody asked us for the call recording, our messages with the client, or a confirmation from the client himself. As far as we know, he never disputed anything, and he has told us he is paying by bank transfer outside Stripe.
I'm not saying our setup is perfect. It's a new account, the first real charge was large compared to the $1 test, and billing a saved card later is probably what looked wrong. If there's something specific we should fix, I'll fix it.
What I'm asking:
1. Is there anyone at Stripe who can look at this with the evidence, including getting the client on a call?
2. For people who've been through this: how would you structure per-appointment billing so it doesn't look unauthorized (an invoice with a link each time, an email confirmation before each charge, something else)?
I can share the account details, the call recording and the messages with anyone from Stripe who wants to look. Thanks for reading.

Parents pay $75k for Alpha's AI school. Half of its students missed math target
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:39:46 UTC
04:39:46 KST

Alpha School, the network of AI-driven private schools charging parents up to $75,000 a year, has won backers from Silicon Valley to the Trump administration. Its own data show most of its students aren’t reaching the model’s central promise.
Only 42% of Alpha students met the network’s goal of learning twice as fast in both math and reading, according to internal data first reported by The New York Times. The findings land as Alpha pushes into public schools, where districts are adopting its software in hopes of fitting more learning into less time.
What Alpha promises parents
Alpha has no traditional teachers. It employs what it calls “guides,” whose job, according to the company’s marketing materials, is to “make every student feel valued and motivated.” Guides don’t plan lessons or grade homework, Fortune reported in September.
The core of the pitch is that students will “learn twice as much as their peers in two hours of academics a day” using TimeBack, an app Alpha markets as an AI tutor. Cofounder MacKenzie Price says the app personalizes and accelerates learning for each student, freeing the rest of the school day for workshops on skills such as public speaking and relationship building.
The network has grown fast. A spokesperson told Fortune in September that Alpha had more than 1,200 students. The Times put the figure at about 1,500 across roughly 50 campuses, in line with the expansion plans Alpha announced in August.
That pitch has drawn AI optimists. “I definitely think using AI well is a skill in the future, if not the skill, so it’s kind of a disservice not to have that in school,” Sarah Cone, a venture capitalist whose 8-year-old daughter attends Alpha’s New York campus, told Fortune in September.
In September 2025, U.S. Education Secretary Linda McMahon toured Alpha’s Austin campus and called its model “the most exciting thing I’ve seen in education in a long time.” LinkedIn cofounder Reid Hoffman and hedge fund manager Bill Ackman have both publicly backed Price and Alpha on X. Even the X account of Elizabeth Holmes, the Theranos founder serving more than 11 years in prison for fraud, praised Price.
Where students are falling short
Math is the bigger gap. About half of K-8 students at Alpha’s private campuses missed the goal of learning math twice as fast, and roughly one in five missed their reading targets, according to the Times‘ review of the network’s data.
Students who started behind fared worse. About a fifth of Alpha’s private school students scored at or below the 50th percentile nationally in math last fall, and only 37% of that group met the network’s 2x growth goal by winter, the Times found.
The two-hour premise is also in question. Guides and parents told Wired that students worked late into the night to meet the school’s goals. One parent of a 13-year-old reading at an 11th-grade level said reaching those goals came with “stress and anxiety.”
Former employees told 404 Media that Alpha’s AI generated faulty lesson plans, which internal documents said sometimes did “more harm than good,” and that the company scraped other online courses without permission.
Alpha’s model is reaching public schools
More than 2,600 public school students in Texas and South Carolina now use Alpha’s platform, according to company documents reviewed by the Times. That is a tougher test: public schools can’t remove struggling students, the Times noted, and they lack the prize incentives Alpha uses at its private campuses.
The Houston Independent School District, the largest in Texas, is piloting the software at two schools. Superintendent Mike Miles told the Times that students spent more time on Alpha’s apps than planned, cutting into other activities. He still wants AI in the district’s toolkit, calling it an important tool for students to be “successful in a different world and workplace.”
Research points to a learning penalty
Alpha’s expansion comes as schools grapple with falling math and reading scores and growing evidence that how students use AI matters as much as whether they use it.
A study of 26,811 secondary students in central China found that homework scores rose 18% after students adopted generative AI tools, while closed-book exam scores fell 20%. The OECD’s latest PISA results found that daily AI users scored lower in science than nonusers, while moderate users performed about the same.
Professors have told Fortune that students who lean on AI summaries struggle with sustained reading. “Today, if you assign that amount of reading, they often don’t know what to do,” Timothy O’Malley, a theology professor at the University of Notre Dame, told Fortune‘s Preston Fore in June.
“It’s not even an inability to critically think,” Jessica Hooten Wilson, a professor of great books and humanities at Pepperdine University, told Fortune. “It’s an inability to read sentences.”

Protomaps: Self-hosting map tiles has gotten cheap and easy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:26:22 UTC
04:26:22 KST

Protomaps: Self-hosting map tiles has gotten cheap and easy
PMTiles is a tile format which works via HTTP Range requests and can be deployed as a single file to a storage bucket.
I came across Protomaps in research for my latest project, Mapboard.1 For most non-commercial projects, commercial APIs become prohibitively expensive if you ever go viral (Mapbox, for instance, charges $5 per 1k map loads once you’ve exhausted the free tier).
PMTiles, part of the Protomaps ecosystem, is a tile format that ships in a single file, accessible via Range requests, and is thus extremely portable and easy to deploy. For CDNs that support caching Range requests of large files, it requires no effort besides down- and uploading it to storage. For others, like Cloudflare, you can utilize an edge function to translate between the traditional x/y/z URL path and Range requests.
Getting some Maps
If all you want is the world map, getting it is as simple as going to the build page and downloading the latest daily build.
However, more fun can be had with the CLI. For example, you can extract data for only a bounding box or specific zoom levels, to get a significantly smaller file.
Deploying to Bunny.net
One great CDN for deploying this is Bunny.net (affiliate link). They support HTTP Range requests, don’t collect personal data by default,2 are headquartered in the EU, and offer low-cost access to a global network of CDN and edge storage servers.
Pricing varies based on region and replication factor of the edge storage. But generally you pay $0.01/GB ($0.005/GB on the smaller Volume network) for bandwidth to Europe and North America, and $0.01/GB/month (no replication) to $0.055/GB/month (maximum replication) for storage. A high degree of replication mostly matters for latency in case of a cache miss (as the data is further replicated when it’s cached at the CDN PoPs).
In practical terms, storing the 139GB world map with replication to most continents (5x) will run you about $5/month in storage fees. Loading a map of Europe on a laptop and zooming in on a city causes about 5MB of data to be transmitted, which would be $0.00005 — further navigations and reloads will be less expensive as more of the map gets cached locally. At $0.05/1k map loads that could be 100x cheaper than Mapbox. However, the actual bandwidth of course varies (less for people that bounce immediately, possibly tens of MB for heavy users). For Mapbox you only pay a flat fee per visit.
Uploading the file
It’s a good idea to create a storage zone with S3 compatibility enabled, as uploading via the HTTP API timed out after 15 minutes for me.
Then, with rclone installed, you can download and stream the file to the Bunny storage zone. Preferably in tmux or from a temporary VPS, if your connection isn’t great.
rclone config create bunnycdn s3 \
  provider=Other \
  access_key_id="<zone-name>" \
  secret_access_key="<storage-key>" \
  endpoint="https://<primary-region>-s3.storage.bunnycdn.com"
rclone copyurl "<build-url>" "bunnycdn:<zone-name>/<filename>.pmtiles" \
  --s3-chunk-size 256M \
  --progress
Configuring the pull zone
Theoretically, at this point you just need to create a pull zone, select the storage zone as an origin, and you’re done. Practically, it would be sensible to change some settings.
Allowed CORS origins can be configured under Security > General > Allowed referrers, so that other websites cannot embed your tiles.
You’ll also want to verify under Caching that “Optimize for large object delivery” is enabled, as this makes it possible to cache Range requests.
You might also want to dissuade people from downloading the whole file and leaving you with a large CDN bill. The most practical way I’ve found for this is limiting the download speed to 10kB/s after 10MB transmitted in a single request (using the “Download speed limits” and “Limit after” settings under “Network limits”). Regular usage should never reach this amount. You may further want to introduce a requests/second limit (do note that legitimate usage can also quickly reach high numbers here) or a monthly bandwidth limit for the pull zone.
Embedding the Map
With the tiles deployed, the only thing left to do is to embed them in your website via MapLibre GL, for example. To do this, you’ll need to install the pmtiles npm package and add the protocol and a style to MapLibre as detailed in the official installation guide.
Congratulations, you now host your own cost-effective and highly available map of the world!

Stay updated on new blog posts and projects by me; sent less than once per week.
written by
Hi, I’m Bruce, a Computer Science student at ETH Zurich. I build mobile apps, websites and other products, and I write about the interesting things I run into along the way. More about me →

Congress wants to tighten export rules on AI chips after Nvidia spy scandal
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:15:43 UTC
04:15:43 KST

Unsurprisingly, following the scandal involving now-former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and a suspected Chinese spy, Congress is looking to further restrict exports of AI chips. Both Democrats and Republicans have voiced support for various measures and bills that would tighten rules and have demanded that Huang and other Nvidia leaders testify before Congress.
Congress wants to tighten export rules on AI chips after Nvidia spy scandal
Following revelations about Jensen Huang’s relationship with a suspected Chinese spy Congress wants to crack down.
Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer has already said that Huang and the Trump administration need to answer to Congress. Senate Banking Committee Chairman Tim Scott has also sent letters to both Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick calling on them to publicly testify to the committee.
Bills such as the Remote Access Security Act, first introduced in the House in April 2025 but stalled in the Senate, are receiving renewed interest. While that specific bill might not become law, House Speaker Mike Johnson has said there is “growing consensus in Congress on limiting chip exports to China,” according to Reuters. Johnson has also said that he personally supports efforts to restrict China’s access to powerful AI chips from companies like Nvidia.
