Sopa de Lima
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

Sopa de Lima
Just one bite of this citrusy chicken soup will instantly transport you to Mexico.
Serves4
Makesabout 5 cups
Prep35 minutes
Cook1 hour 15 minutes
Why You’ll Love My Sopa de Lima
- This soup is full of bold flavors. Yet it manages to still feel light and can easily be served on any warm day.
- So much crunch! The tortilla strips are easy to make and give it the perfect texture. The leftovers are also great as a snack or salad topper.
The Yucatán Peninsula is a magical region in Southeast Mexico that’s rich in Mayan history and surrounded by beautiful waterfronts and jungles. In the towns, you will find colonial haciendas that are often lined with citrus trees and a very particular one called lima agria. From the outside, it resembles a large lime, though when you try it, it tastes more like a very sweet key lime. This citrus fruit is used in marinades, ceviches, aguas frescas, salsas, and, of course, this classic sopa de lima. The traditional way to make it is with ocellated turkey (a tropical relative of the typical turkey that can only be found in this region), though I prefer using chicken thighs, which gives the soup a rich flavor and has the added bonus of cooking up much faster.
Key Ingredients in Sopa de Lima
- Skin-on chicken thighs: Cook them in low-sodium chicken stock to make an even more flavorful stock in less time.
- Tomatoes: Blackening and peeling Roma tomatoes gives them another layer of flavor while removing any unpleasant texture you get with the skin of the tomato.
- Garlic: Pan-roasting garlic with the peel on helps protect it from getting bitter and lets it develop sweetness and smokiness. Peel and chop after blackened.
- Citrus juices: Mixing mandarin orange and lime juices creates a well-balanced combination that mimics the one from a lima agria found in the Yucatán Peninsula.
- Tortilla strips: Slice into strips and fry until crunchy. As you eat, add a few at a time to create the ultimate crunch. Save extras for snacking or as salad toppers.
- Sumac: While it is not a traditional ingredient, I find that its addition brightens and gives the soup a slight tartness that you find in the Yucatán.
How to Make Sopa de Lima
- Start making the soup. Place white onion, chicken broth, bone-in chicken thighs, unpeeled garlic cloves, kosher salt, black peppercorns, bay leaves, whole cloves, and dried oregano in a large pot. Simmer until chicken is cooked through. Remove the chicken and shred the meat once cool enough to handle. Strain the broth, discarding the contents.
- Finish the soup. Char tomatoes and unpeeled garlic cloves in a cast-iron skillet; once cool, peel and finely chop. Cook chopped white onion, diced green bell pepper, and chopped tomatoes and garlic in a pot until tender. Stir in shredded chicken, strained broth, and sumac, simmer, then stir in orange and limes juices.
- Fry the tortilla strips. Heat vegetable oil in a large saucepan over medium-high heat until 365ºF. Working in batches, add the tortilla strips and fry until crisp and golden brown. Using a slotted spoon or spider, transfer to a paper towel-lined plate.
- Make the optional Yucatecan salsa. Toss sliced red and white onion and kosher salt in a bowl. Let sit for 5 minutes, then stir in sliced habanero pepper, orange juice, lime juice, white vinegar, and dried oregano.
- Serve. Fill each bowl with a couple ladles of soup, then top with tortilla strips, a slice of lime, and the optional salsa.
Helpful Swaps
It is hard to replicate the exact flavor profile of lima agria from the Yucatán Peninsula. I find that a combination of lime and mandarin orange juice works great. Though you can also try using other citruses such as lime and grapefruit or even orange juice. You are looking for some acid that is slightly balanced with sweetness.
Storage and Make-Ahead Tips
- Make ahead: The broth can be made one day ahead, strained, and refrigerated in an airtight container. Place shredded chicken in an airtight container and refrigerate. The fried tortilla strips can be made up to one day ahead and stored in an airtight container at room temperature. The optional Yucatecan salsa can be made two days before and stored in a glass container in the refrigerator.
- Storage: Leftover soup can be refrigerated in an airtight container for up to four days or frozen for up to three months. Leftover fried tortilla strips can be stored at room temperature in an airtight container for up to four days. And the optional Yucatecan salsa will last seven days under refrigeration.
What to Serve with Sopa de Lima
Sopa de Lima Recipe
Just one bite of this citrusy chicken soup will instantly transport you to Mexico.
Prep time 35 minutes
Cook time 1 hour 15 minutes
Makes about 5 cups
Serves 4
Ingredients
For the soup:
- 1 medium white onion, divided
- 2 (32-ounce) cartons low-sodium chicken broth (8 cups)
- 1 1/2 pounds skin-on, bone-in chicken thighs
- 9 cloves garlic, unpeeled, divided
- 2 teaspoons kosher salt, plus more as needed
- 15 whole black peppercorns
- 2 dried bay leaves
- 2 whole cloves
- 1/2 teaspoon dried oregano, preferably Mexican
- 1 pound roma tomatoes
- 2 tablespoons vegetable oil
- 1 medium green bell pepper, diced
- 1/2 teaspoon ground sumac
- 2 tablespoons regular or mandarin orange juice
- 2 tablespoons freshly squeezed lime juice (from 1 medium lime)
For the tortilla strips and serving:
- 2 cups vegetable oil
- 8 yellow or white corn tortillas, cut into 1/2-inch strips
- Thinly sliced lime wheels
For the Yucatecan salsa (optional):
- 1/4 medium red onion, thinly sliced (about 3/4 cup)
- 1/4 medium white onion, thinly sliced (about 3/4 cup)
- 1 teaspoon kosher salt
- 1 habanero pepper, seeded and thinly sliced
- 2 tablespoons regular or mandarin orange juice
- 2 tablespoons freshly squeezed lime juice (from 1 medium lime)
- 1 tablespoon distilled white vinegar
- 1 small pinch dried oregano, preferably Mexican
Instructions
Make the soup:
- Halve 1 medium white onion (do not peel). Place one onion half (reserve the remaining for the final soup), 2 (32-ounce) cartons low-sodium chicken broth, 1 1/2 pounds bone-in chicken thighs, 5 of the unpeeled garlic cloves, 2 teaspoons kosher salt, 15 whole black peppercorns, 2 dried bay leaves, 2 whole cloves, and 1/2 teaspoon dried oregano in a large pot or Dutch oven.
- Bring to a simmer over medium-high heat. Simmer until the chicken is cooked through, about 30 minutes. (This is a good time to fry the tortilla strips and make the salsa.)
- Transfer the chicken to a plate. When cool enough to handle, remove and discard the skin and bones. Shred the meat. Pour the broth through a strainer set over a large heatproof bowl. Discard the contents of the strainer. Reserve the pot.
- Heat a large cast-iron skillet over medium-high heat. Add 1 pound roma tomatoes and remaining 4 unpeeled garlic cloves and cook, turning occasionally, until charred all over, 4 to 5 minutes total. Transfer to a cutting board.
- Peel and finely chop the reserved onion half. Once cool enough to handle, peel the tomatoes as best you can and garlic and discard the skins. Finely chop the garlic and tomatoes.
- Heat 2 tablespoons vegetable oil in the reserved pot over medium heat. Add the finely chopped white onion and cook until translucent, about 4 minutes. Stir in the tomatoes, garlic, and 1 diced medium green bell pepper. Cook until the pepper is tender, about 10 minutes.
- Stir in the chicken, broth, and 1/2 teaspoon ground sumac. Return to a simmer, then simmer for 5 minutes to let the flavors meld. Stir in 2 tablespoons orange juice and 2 tablespoons lime juice. Taste and season with kosher salt as needed.
- To serve, top the soup with the tortilla strips, lime wheels, and salsa if using.
Fry the tortilla strips:
- Heat 2 cups vegetable oil in a large saucepan over medium-high heat until 365ºF. Working in 3 batches, add the tortilla strips and fry, stirring occasionally, until crisp and golden brown, 2 to 3 minutes. Using a slotted spoon or spider, transfer to a paper towel-lined plate.
Make the Yucatecan salsa (optional):
- Toss 1/4 thinly sliced medium red onion, 1/4 thinly sliced medium white onion, and 1 teaspoon kosher salt together in a medium bowl. Let sit for 5 minutes.
- Stir in 1 thinly sliced habanero pepper, 2 tablespoons orange juice, 2 tablespoons lime juice, 1 tablespoon distilled white vinegar, and 1 small pinch dried oregano.
Recipe Notes
Storage: Leftover soup can be refrigerated in an airtight container for up to 4 days or frozen for up to 3 months

====================================================================================================

Eclipse Mosquitto – An open source MQTT broker
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
18:36:40 UTC
03:36:40 KST

Mosquitto is an open source implementation of a server for version 5.0, 3.1.1,
and 3.1 of the MQTT protocol. It also includes a C and C++ client library,
the mosquitto_pub mosquitto_rr, and mosquitto_sub utilities for
publishing and subscribing, and the mosquitto_ctrl, mosquitto_signal, and
mosquitto_passwd applications for helping administer the broker.
See the following links for more information on MQTT:
- Community page: http://mqtt.org/
- MQTT v3.1.1 standard: https://docs.oasis-open.org/mqtt/mqtt/v3.1.1/mqtt-v3.1.1.html
- MQTT v5.0 standard: https://docs.oasis-open.org/mqtt/mqtt/v5.0/mqtt-v5.0.html
Mosquitto project information is available at the following locations:
- Main homepage: https://mosquitto.org/
- Find existing bugs or submit a new bug: https://github.com/eclipse-mosquitto/mosquitto/issues
- Source code repository: https://github.com/eclipse-mosquitto/mosquitto
There is also a public test server available at https://test.mosquitto.org/
See https://mosquitto.org/download/ for details on installing binaries for various platforms.
If you have installed a binary package the broker should have been started automatically. If not, it can be started with a very basic configuration:
mosquitto
Then use mosquitto_sub to subscribe to a topic:
mosquitto_sub -t 'test/topic' -v
And to publish a message:
mosquitto_pub -t 'test/topic' -m 'hello world'
Note that starting the broker like this allows anonymous/unauthenticated access but only from the local computer, so it's only really useful for initial testing.
If you want to have clients from another computer connect, you will need to
provide a configuration file. If you have installed from a binary package, you
will probably already have a configuration file at somewhere like
/etc/mosquitto/mosquitto.conf. If you've compiled from source, you can write
your config file then run as mosquitto -c /path/to/mosquitto.conf.
To start your config file you define a listener and you will need to think about what authentication you require. It is not advised to run your broker with anonymous access when it is publicly available.
For details on how to do this, look at the authentication methods available and the dynamic security plugin.
Documentation for the broker, clients and client library API can be found in
the man pages, which are available online at https://mosquitto.org/man/. There
are also pages with an introduction to the features of MQTT, the
mosquitto_passwd utility for dealing with username/passwords, and a
description of the configuration file options available for the broker.
Detailed client library API documentation can be found at https://mosquitto.org/api/
To build from source the recommended route for end users is to download the archive from https://mosquitto.org/download/.
On Windows and Mac, use cmake to build. On other platforms, just run make
to build. For Windows, see also README-windows.md.
If you are building from the git repository then the documentation will not
already be built. Use make binary to skip building the man pages, or install
docbook-xsl on Debian/Ubuntu systems.
- cJSON - required
- c-ares (libc-ares-dev on Debian based systems) - optional, enable with
WITH_SRV=yes
- libedit - for mosquitto_ctrl interactive shell - optional, disable with
WITH_EDITLINE=no
- libmicrohttpd - for broker http api support - optional, disable with
WITH_HTTP_API=no
- openssl (libssl-dev on Debian based systems) - optional, disable with
WITH_TLS=no
- pthreads - for client library thread support. This is required to support the
mosquitto_loop_start() and mosquitto_loop_stop() functions. If compiled
without pthread support, the library isn't guaranteed to be thread safe.
- sqlite3 - for persistence support in the broker - optional, disable with
WITH_SQLITE=no
- uthash / utlist - bundled versions of these headers are provided, disable
their use with WITH_BUNDLED_DEPS=no
- xsltproc (xsltproc and docbook-xsl on Debian based systems) - only needed
when building from git sources - disable with WITH_DOCS=no
Equivalent options for enabling/disabling features are available when using the CMake build. It is also possible to enable/disable building of specific plugins in the CMake build.
You can download and install mosquitto using the vcpkg dependency manager:
git clone https://github.com/Microsoft/vcpkg.git
cd vcpkg
./bootstrap-vcpkg.sh
./vcpkg integrate install
./vcpkg install mosquitto
The mosquitto port in vcpkg is kept up to date by Microsoft team members and community contributors. If the version is out of date, please create an issue or pull request on the vcpkg repository.
Mosquitto was written by Roger Light roger@atchoo.org. There have been substantial contributions by other people in the community both in terms of code and other help.

====================================================================================================

Microsoft 365 outage leads to chlorine gas poisoning of 15 hotel guests
Germany
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
19:36:10 CET
03:36:10 KST

Diesem Problem waren wir alle ausgesetzt: Am Freitag gab es eine Störung, die den US-Softwarekonzern Microsoft lahmgelegt hat. Unter anderem das E-Mail-Programm Outlook.
Betroffen war auch das Phantasialand. Durch die Störung kamen keine Mails an, wie das Unternehmen dem WDR am Samstag mitteilte. So bekam niemand mit, dass das Wartungssicherheitssystem der Saunalandschaft längst per Mail Alarm geschlagen hat. Denn das automatischen Dosiersystem hatte zu viel Chlor in den Pool gepumpt.
Reizungen der Atemwege
Dadurch wurden Gäste im Hotel Ling Bao des Phantasialands in Brühl verletzt. Sie haben über gesundheitliche Probleme geklagt, nachdem sie den Pool besucht hatten. Der wurde zeitweise gesperrt, konnte inzwischen aber wieder freigegeben werden, wie die Feuerwehr am Samstag dem WDR mitteilte.
Wegen der Beschwerden der Hotel-Besucher wurden Rettungskräfte und die Feuerwehr gerufen. 13 Gäste klagten über Reizungen der Atemwege, letztlich stieg die Zahl der Betroffenen auf insgesamt 15.
Zunächst hieß es, dass fünf Gäste ins Krankenhaus gebracht wurden, später wurde die Zahl auf vier korrigiert, eine Person davon blieb wegen einer mittelschweren Verletzung zur Beobachtung im Krankenhaus.
Überhöhter Chlorgehalt
Im Hotel ergaben Tests der Einsatzkräfte einen Defekt des automatischen Dosiersystems für den Pool. Dies hatte zu einem deutlich überhöhten Chlorgehalt geführt. Das Wasser wurde daher ausgetauscht.
Unsere Quellen:
- Polizei Rhein-Erft-Kreis
- Feuerwehr Rhein-Erft-Kreis
- Pressemeldung Feuerwehr Brühl
- Birgit Reckersdrees, Direktion Marketing und Sales Phantasialand
Sendung: WDR2, WDR-Aktuell, 14.11.2026, 18 Uhr 
Sendung: WDR Fernsehen, WDR aktuell, 15.11.2026, 12.45 Uhr

GTA 6's Upcoming Launch Celebrated by Wendy's, YouTube, the City of Miami, and 20% of America's Workforce
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
18:34:00 UTC
03:34:00 KST

GTA 6's Upcoming Launch Celebrated by Wendy's, YouTube, the City of Miami, and 20% of America's Workforce (gamingbible.com) 26
			
		 	
				Fast food restaurant Wendy's drew an image of their redheaded mascot in the style of GTA, posting it on social media with the message "Our ride-or-dies are gonna love what we have in the works," and the date 11/19/26.
That's how widespread the hype has become for Thursday's release of Grand Theft Auto VI.
The BBC reports that fictional brands from the game are now actually appearing on billboards across Miami, the city that inspired the game's "Vice City" setting. And  GamingBible.com reports Rockstar Games has announced Grand Theft Auto VI: The Festival, "a real-life music festival to be held in Miami, Florida...  on November 21, 2026.  Featuring two stages, the festival will be headlined by some incredible artists that, to state the obvious, feature on the game's soundtrack...."
YouTube said pre-launch videos about the game have already racked up a whopping 40 billion views (with 900 billion views for videos about all GTA games).  And now YouTube is partnering with Rockstar Games to offer several special features on launch day:
Fans can drop themselves directly into Vice City using the GTAVI: Visit Vice City Shorts Effect. The feature includes multiple iconic backgrounds, making it easy for creators and fans to build their own launch-week videos... 
Listeners can find the official GTAVI soundtrack (featuring Morgan Wallen's "Last Thing You Need") and the complete GTA series anthology playlist directly on the YouTube Music homepage throughout launch week...
Starting November 19 at 12 AM ET, fans worldwide can tune in to youtube.com/youtube for a 24 hour stream packed with live gameplay, creator podcasts, and original content featuring top creators... [On launch day] fans can jump straight into the global broadcast by clicking the custom GTAVI-themed YouTube logo (Yoodle) on the YouTube homepage  
At one one study suggests nearly one in a five U.S. workers plan to skip work on Thursday, writes
the New York Post:
Roughly 18% of workers plan to take Thursday off to park themselves on the couch and wreak havoc across the revamped Vice City, according to data from BambooHR, which analyzed information from 3.4 million US-based employees... Half of the joystick jockeys planning to take off will simply call in sick instead of requesting time off, BambooHR reported.  The hordes of workers playing hooky could cost the US economy $1 billion in lost productivity, International Business News reported.  
Forbes reports the game has $1.18 billion in pre-orders from 12 million buyers, according to market research firm Sensor Tower, puting it ahead of Grand Theft Auto V, which had 7 million pre-orders. 
The BBC points out that "Already, both PlayStation and Xbox, the two platforms the game is initially releasing on, have said the game has become the most pre-ordered title in their digital stores' history.  This may be partly down to its huge size (212GB), meaning many are pre-ordering to "pre-load" the game, ready to play it immediately at launch rather than waiting to download it. 
But that's a problem for other games, reports GamingBible.com:
With most current-gen consoles having somewhere between 512 GB and 1 TB of free storage before expansion upgrades, it means if you're a gamer with lots of games installed, you're going to have to make some sacrifices and delete any older games you're not currently playing.  As reported by Push Square, it seems that Electronic Arts is also getting the message, as it allegedly is showing messages inside games asking players not to uninstall in anticipation of the GTA VI launch. 
GamingBible.com notes one final concern for hardcore gamers.  "Rockstar Games recently confirmed the game targets 30fps, even on high-end consoles like the PlayStation Pro.  While it probably won't affect the game's total sales, many gamers are appalled."

Flaming Hydra’s new Kickstarter campaign rethinks e-book ownership and digital lending
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
18:29:14 UTC
03:29:14 KST

Here’s a big promise: A new Kickstarter campaign aims to usher in the “return of the human internet.”
That’s the tagline of a crowdfunding campaign for The Phoenix Project, an initiative from the online culture and commentary magazine Flaming Hydra. If you back The Phoenix Project, you’re supporting three things — a one-off print magazine designed to celebrate “the values and fun of the early internet,” a new e-book service for libraries, and a newsletter subscription/archiving program that’s also designed for libraries.
To Flaming Hydra’s lead editor Maria Bustillos, these disparate projects are “basically all one thing,” tied together by the goal of “protecting freedom of information” and fulfilling the “promise of the early internet, where you’re making information available to as many people as possible, while still protecting the right of artists and authors to make a living.”
Bustillos told me that these issues have been highlighted on Flaming Hydra itself, for example when Tal Levin wrote about finding countless AI-generated clones of his book on Amazon. By “creating an ecosystem that is based in people working together, who are offering their stuff in a context that is controlled and doesn’t allow” others to create and sell AI-generated clones, Bustillos said Flaming Hydra is “part of the human project.”
“AI is not this unstoppable force,” she added. “If people just set up publishing pipelines and networks and organizations that are dedicated to the human internet, people who want to read stuff can go to them, and it’s actually completely isolated from mechanistic interference.”
The Phoenix Project fits into this vision by making the work of human writers more accessible to libraries.
Back in 2020, Bustillos wrote about the e-book systems dominating libraries and schools. In one example, instead of just buying a book, a school had to pay $27 per student per year for continued access to copies of “The Diary of Anne Frank.”
This is symptomatic of a broader system that treats true ownership of e-books and other digital media as a thing of the past. Instead, we “license” these e-books from their publishers, and they can be altered or removed at any time.
“E-books are books,” Bustillos said. “They’re not some other thing. They are books, and people should be able to own them, and libraries should be able to own them, and media should be able to be owned. All digital media should be able to be owned.”
In her view, ownership boils down to a simple question: “If you are at the mercy of somebody who can shut the book off, you do not own it.”
So even beyond the question of ongoing payments, there have also been examples of e-books being altered or removed from readers’ devices long after their purchase. Bustillos acknowledged that for individual pieces of media, this distinction may not seem like a big deal, “but if it’s all the textbooks for everybody, if it’s all the books,” then it’s a real problem.
Flaming Hydra and The Phoenix Project seek to solve this by letting libraries truly purchase and own e-books. They can then lend those e-books to their patrons through a new, open source “library in a box” system called Lenny for Libraries.
The Phoenix Project is starting by offering five new e-books from Flaming Hydra writers, including collections of writing from The Awl, and of essays by Tom Scocca and Bustillos herself. That might sound like a relatively small start, but Bustillos said she’s talking to library consortiums and author groups, and the goal here is to “change the conversation” around e-books and digital archiving and lending. So The Phoenix Project could be paving the way for other initiatives that follow a similar model.
Similarly, The Phoenix Foundry Archive is designed to make paywalled newsletter content available to libraries. Bustillos noted that “so much of the best journalism, criticism, essays” is now being published in paywalled newsletters that are inaccessible to libraries (as well as the Internet Archive).
With Phoenix, newsletter writers (starting with those who use Ghost, with support for additional newsletter platforms coming in the future) will be able to offer subscriptions to libraries, similar to the way libraries can subscribe to newspapers and magazines. Once it’s set up, Phoenix will automatically convert whichever newsletter posts the writer wants into EPUB documents, which are then sent to libraries for digital lending.
Flaming Hydra has been working on all this even before launching the Kickstarter, and Bustillos said she was grateful for the financial support from backers like Anand Raganathan, Brewster Kahle, and Paul Ford. But by turning to crowdfunding, Bustillos said Flaming Hydra can better protect the work, ensuring that it remains sustainable and independent.
“We don’t have to be at the mercy of anybody, and it also protects editorial independence,” she said. “Those things go hand in hand.”
Flaming Hydra seeks to raise $30,000 for The Phoenix Project, with the campaign ending on November 27.

'Birdbrain' Is a Misnomer. Crows Make Decisions Faster Than We Can
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
17:53:31 UTC
02:53:31 KST

Supported by
‘Birdbrain’ Is a Misnomer. Crows Make Decisions Faster Than We Can
In a recent experiment, crows could process and respond to visual cues at twice the speed as humans and monkeys.
Lemmy and Iggy, two carrion crows, are no strangers to a psychology lab. As study participants, they have helped scientists prove that birds can use tools to forage, learn numbers and count objects, and provided other insights into the ways birds think.
Now, they’ve also revealed that birds can make staggeringly quick decisions, besting their human and nonhuman primate counterparts with ease. In new findings published in Science, the crows could process and respond to visual cues at twice the speed as humans and monkeys, quantifying the neural speed of avian decision-making for the first time.
The discovery might be the final blow for the use of “birdbrains” as a pejorative.
“We wouldn’t think of birds making these decisions faster than monkeys, or faster than us ‘awesome’ human beings,” said Mike Colombo, a neuroscientist and comparative psychologist at the University of Otago in New Zealand who was not involved in the research. “But they do. They’re just incredibly fast.”
Questions of bird intelligence have long been debated by researchers. Their small and superficially simple brains led scholars in the 19th and much of the 20th century to conclude they were not cognitively competent creatures. Even early research into birdsong, which demonstrated that some birds are capable of complex speech-like patterns and impressive vocal mimicry, was not enough to turn the tide. Their elaborate melodies were considered specialized adaptations that didn’t reflect their cognitive abilities.
Although passionate birders may find those conclusions obviously mistaken, the scientific community has been slower to catch on. “It’s taken the last 20 years to realize that actually their brain architecture is quite fascinating,” said Dr. Colombo.
Recent research, for example, has found that pigeons are capable of distinguishing between cancerous and noncancerous cells, numerical values, and words and non-words. Corvids (the group that includes crows, ravens, rooks and scrub jays) can even recognize themselves, solve abstract problems, plan for the future and show theory of mind — an ability to understand what other birds might be thinking and act accordingly.

In a recent experiment, crows could process and respond to visual cues at twice the speed as humans and monkeys.
Lemmy and Iggy, two carrion crows, are no strangers to a psychology lab. As study participants, they have helped scientists prove that birds can use tools to forage, learn numbers and count objects, and provided other insights into the ways birds think.
Now, they’ve also revealed that birds can make staggeringly quick decisions, besting their human and nonhuman primate counterparts with ease. In new findings published in Science, the crows could process and respond to visual cues at twice the speed as humans and monkeys, quantifying the neural speed of avian decision-making for the first time.
The discovery might be the final blow for the use of “birdbrains” as a pejorative.
“We wouldn’t think of birds making these decisions faster than monkeys, or faster than us ‘awesome’ human beings,” said Mike Colombo, a neuroscientist and comparative psychologist at the University of Otago in New Zealand who was not involved in the research. “But they do. They’re just incredibly fast.”
Questions of bird intelligence have long been debated by researchers. Their small and superficially simple brains led scholars in the 19th and much of the 20th century to conclude they were not cognitively competent creatures. Even early research into birdsong, which demonstrated that some birds are capable of complex speech-like patterns and impressive vocal mimicry, was not enough to turn the tide. Their elaborate melodies were considered specialized adaptations that didn’t reflect their cognitive abilities.
Although passionate birders may find those conclusions obviously mistaken, the scientific community has been slower to catch on. “It’s taken the last 20 years to realize that actually their brain architecture is quite fascinating,” said Dr. Colombo.
Recent research, for example, has found that pigeons are capable of distinguishing between cancerous and noncancerous cells, numerical values, and words and non-words. Corvids (the group that includes crows, ravens, rooks and scrub jays) can even recognize themselves, solve abstract problems, plan for the future and show theory of mind — an ability to understand what other birds might be thinking and act accordingly.

Huawei Set To Challenge Tesla Powerwall with New, 20 kW Home Battery

Huawei Set To Challenge Tesla Powerwall with New, 20 kW Home Battery (electrek.co) 24
			
		 	
				"Huawei believes its new Luterra S2 is more than just a home backup battery," reports Electrek:
This high-tech Huawei home battery pack hopes to hold back Tesla's latest Powerwall with silicon carbide technology, AI-powered energy management baked in, and enough modular storage to keep even large homes bright and warm when the rest of the block goes dark...  The Chinese tech firm is calling it an "all-in-one residential energy system" that combines a hybrid inverter, modular battery storage, and AI-powered energy management in a single, simple, and stackable package that's available in single-phase versions that put out between 3 and 10 kW and three-phase versions that can push up to 20 kW.  The Luterra S2 is the first system of its kind to use Huawei's proprietary full-silicon carbide (SiC) architecture, which promises improved efficiency, power density, and ease of use. It's so easy, in fact, that Huawei claims it can be installed and commissioned in just minutes. 
Huawei's basic Luterra S2 configuration starts with "just" 5 kWh of battery energy storage and 3 kW of output. That's enough to keep essential household appliances, lighting, and electronics running during a power outage, but you'll want to keep going if you're preparing for more than a day or two without power.  If that sounds like you, Luterra's got you covered. The modular system can be expanded with modular 7 kWh LFP battery packs and can keep scaling up to a massive 336 kWh of total storage across interconnected systems, with inverter output peaking at an impressive 60 kW using three 20 kW units in parallel. Keeping all that power cool and quiet is a Huawei-developed passive cooling system that does without external fans, keeping operating noise levels below a claimed 29 dB (about the same as my Mac mini).
