Clice v1.0 Released: Welcome to Modern C++
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
17:22:32 UTC
02:22:32 KST

clice recently hit 1,000 commits, a special milestone for the project. Since July 19, 2026, clice has been shipping nightly builds and going through roughly four months of alpha testing, with a huge number of bugs fixed thanks to real-world feedback from early users. Over the same stretch, I built a rigorous testing system for clice and ran extensive tests across many real open-source projects, tracking down and fixing the issues they uncovered. What does that mean? Every core feature is now stable, and you rarely run into problems in day-to-day use. So I think the time is right to ship the first stable release. The project looks very different today from where it stood two years ago in Design and Implementation of a New C++ Language Server, and this is probably the first time I’m promoting it widely, so please let me introduce it to you once again! This is the project I have poured the past two and a half years into, the work I am proudest of, the one that has excited me the most and left me sad and anxious more times than I can count, and the most ambitious thing I have taken on in my short life.
What is clice?
In short, clice is a C/C++ language server written in modern C++, but it’s more than just a language server. It is still built on LLVM/Clang, but unlike clangd and ccls, which are also Clang-based, clice was redesigned from the ground up to fix architectural problems that have gone unsolved in those servers for years. It is written entirely in C++23 and serves as an example of modern C++ best practices in its own right, with all the major modern C++ features coming together remarkably well:
- Written entirely with C++20 coroutines, using our own libuv-based structured-concurrency async library for intricate asynchronous logic. It is a complex concurrent program running an event loop on the main thread while coordinating both multiple processes (IPC) and multiple threads.
- Written entirely with C++20 named modules and C++23 import std, and fully self-hosted: module wrappers for third-party libraries are generated automatically by clice modularize, and clice runs smoothly on its own codebase to provide module-aware language services.
- Uses static reflection built on templates and C++20 concepts for all serialization and deserialization across formats, including TOML, JSON, bincode, blob, and command-line arguments, with Rust-macro-like declarative annotations and zero boilerplate.
- Data-oriented code design throughout, with almost no virtual functions outside the callback interfaces Clang requires. Makes heavy use of modern C++ APIs like std::format, std::expected, std::ranges, and std::views, steering clear of outdated legacy C++98/11 APIs.
- A completely hermetic, reproducible build powered by Bazel, compiled with the unified xclang toolchain for a consistent cross-platform cross-compilation workflow. Compiled binaries are heavily optimized (PGO + LTO), and build artifacts depend on nothing beyond system libraries.
People are always debating whether modern C++ features like modules are actually ready for real work. clice’s answer is: yes, of course, completely usable! They hold up for complex software like clice, with hundreds of thousands of lines of code. Maybe this is what the modern C++ we have been hoping for looks like. That said, the surrounding ecosystem really is inconvenient enough right now that getting this whole workflow running end to end took a huge amount of effort on my part to track issues and adapt everything.
Language Server
Here is an overview of clice’s main features:
- Multi-process compilation: clice compiles code in a process pool modeled after modern web browsers, whereas clangd uses multithreading. Why multiple processes? Because C++ is simply too complex, and on certain corner cases the compiler can easily crash or leak memory. That is especially true for incomplete code in the middle of editing: a huge downstream user base tests compilers on complete code, while very few people test them on half-written code, where the compiler mostly falls back on best-effort heuristics. In clangd, a compiler crash takes down the whole server and forces a restart. In clice, compilation happens in isolated child processes: losing one worker does not affect the rest of the server, so go-to-definition, highlighting, and other services keep working. It also makes it much easier to manage memory usage and schedule compilation priorities, cutting down on unnecessary resource consumption.
- Modules and shared caching: clice has full support for C++20 named modules. All you need is a compile_commands.json containing all module files, and clice scans dependencies on its own and lazily builds required modules on demand, regardless of your build system or compiler, with full support for module completion, highlighting, go-to-definition, hover, and more. Built PCHs and PCMs are also cached on local disk for reuse across sessions. By comparison, clangd offers only very limited module support and no persistent cache.
- Compilation context: clice has full support for non-self-contained files, and more generally for the concept of a compilation context. You can explicitly pick the active context of a file: a specific compile command for a source file, or a specific inclusion site for a header. When you open a non-self-contained file, clice detects its context automatically, so it works out of the box. More broadly, it also supports grouping compile commands into build configurations, letting you switch between, say, debug and release.
- Better template support: clice implements a DFS pseudo instantiator that infers what many dependent names resolve to without needing concrete template arguments for a full instantiation. When a template has been instantiated once, clice also draws on that instantiation to power language features, such as completing on auto parameters of a lambda passed to a ranges call. On top of that, clice treats a template as a duck-typed interface and each instantiation as one of its implementations, much like a virtual function and its overrides: Go to Implementation on a dependent call inside a template lists the functions it actually calls across every instantiation, and Find References and Call Hierarchy work the same way.
- Out-of-the-box experience: Most projects need nothing more than a compile_commands.json. clice ships with built-in environment probing for 50+ toolchains, covering nvcc, MSYS2, Cygwin, zigcc, and more, automatically handling mismatches between your actual compiler and Clang. You don’t have to manually add piles of compiler flags to a config file the way you do with clangd.
Beyond these main features, clice also fixes a long list of smaller clangd issues that have gone untouched for years across semantic highlighting, folding, hover, and more. If you’re interested, give it a try and see what surprises you find. Overall, clice surpasses clangd in ease of use, feature support, and performance. You can install the clice extension in VS Code and other editors and start using it right now.
Command Line
Besides running as a language server, clice can also be used from the CLI to access its language features directly. Currently supported subcommands include:
- clice index: Builds a persistent index for the entire project ahead of time and supports resuming interrupted runs, so your editor starts warm as soon as you open the project.
- clice lint: Built-in clang-tidy runner that checks translation units concurrently across a process pool and merges duplicate diagnostics from headers; pass --index to reuse the same parse pass to build the index along the way.
- clice format: A wrapper around clang-format that automatically finds files to format from your config file or CDB and formats them efficiently in parallel.
- clice command: Shows the effective compile command for a file after rule rewrites and toolchain probing, or converts compile_commands.json into resolved -cc1 commands for other Clang tools.
- clice query: Reads the on-disk index directly without starting a server and outputs symbol definitions, references, call graphs, inheritance hierarchies, and file dependencies as JSON.
- clice refactor: Rewrites source files directly from the persistent index without recompiling, supporting project-wide symbol and macro renaming as well as batch renames in one pass via --spec.
- clice analyze: Outputs project-structure JSON from the index to guide refactoring; for example, analyze modules analyzes module dependencies and cycles across directories and lists code patterns that stand in the way of modularization.
- clice modularize: Automatically wraps third-party libraries used by a project as C++20 modules without modifying any source files; following the split analyze proposes, it can also rewrite the project’s own code into module units, turning headers into partitions and #include into import. clice used it to modularize itself.
The story behind it
That wraps up the feature tour. Next, I want to talk about the story behind the project, some of its design decisions, and my own thoughts and struggles along the way, especially now, in the age of AI.
Why not improve clangd?
First, why start a new project instead of improving clangd? That is the question I get asked most often. There are several reasons. I discussed some of them in my earliest post, and today those problems are even easier to see firsthand:
- Maintenance and staffing: At root, clangd has been effectively unmaintained since around 2024. That sounds surprising. How could a tool used by hundreds of thousands of developers have no one maintaining it? I stumbled onto this fact in 2024 when I was getting ready to submit a PR to clangd for better code completion inside templates. From what I understand, it ties back to a shift in Google’s C++ strategy. Before that, clangd was maintained mainly by several Google engineers, and around 2024 Google started scaling back its investment in C++ (Clang); clangd was deemed good enough for Google’s internal needs, and those engineers were reassigned elsewhere. Since then, clangd has lacked any real long-term maintainers. Only Nathan Ridge has been answering questions unpaid in his spare time, purely out of passion, and one person only has so much bandwidth, making it nearly impossible to handle large PRs. That is why clangd’s C++20 modules PR dragged on for nearly a year, and even today it still isn’t really usable on large projects, with plenty of flaws and nobody working on them. Earlier this year, in 2026, Clang’s lead maintainer Aaron Ballman recognized the problem and posted a call for help on the community forum, saying clangd is very close to unmaintained, with a heavy backlog of PRs and issues, and hoping to find new community maintainers; otherwise clangd could eventually be removed from the LLVM project.
- Scale of the code changes: As mentioned at the start, clice introduces many new architectural ideas, such as multi-process compilation. Implementing those inside clangd would come close to rewriting clangd. Nobody is going to review and accept changes of that size. Self-hosting with modules would also require sweeping changes to LLVM’s build system, which I will go into in detail when I introduce xclang later.
- LLVM repository constraints: clice relies on a lot of third-party libraries for better performance and user experience, such as libuv, simdjson, and tomlplusplus. To keep builds simple, LLVM requires the repository to remain self-contained, so pulling in that many third-party libraries is out of the question. You would either have to rewrite and port them into LLVM or put up with worse ergonomics and lower performance. On top of that, a language server is not a standalone project: it also includes extensions and adapters for different editors. Keeping them in the same repo as the server makes maintenance much easier, and LLVM would be just as unlikely to accept that editor code (spanning Rust, Lua, TypeScript, and so on). For long-term maintainability and our core goals, a separate repository was unavoidable.
So in mid-2024, I started looking into how to design and write a new language server. At the end of 2024, I published Design and Implementation of a New C++ Language Server, setting out clice’s core goals and kicking off development in earnest.
How did we get here?
Next, let’s talk about how clice was built. How did it get to its current state? Over the past two and a half years, at a rough guess, I have put nearly 3,000 hours into this project. Even that number doesn’t really tell the story, because clice became part of my life. It was always on my mind, like eating or sleeping: there wasn’t a moment when I wasn’t turning over some problem related to it. Development broke down into roughly four stages:
Mid-2024 to early 2025: I spent this period studying Clang and clangd’s codebase and internals. Anyone who has looked through my older blog posts will know I am a longtime template metaprogramming enthusiast, familiar with C++ and its implementation details down to language-lawyer territory. During this phase I settled on clice’s core goals and overall design, and put together an initial demo. I was a third-year undergrad at the time: I would wake up and start coding right away, skip lunch, walk out to the McDonald’s outside the campus gate when I got hungry, and put in 8 to 10 hours a day.
Early 2025 to early 2026: I originally expected to ship a usable version of clice fairly quickly during this stage. Instead, progress went much slower than I expected, and it remained a long way from production-ready. Looking back, there were a few main reasons:
- Above all, time. I had assumed I would keep putting in 8 to 10 hours a day like before. My undergraduate major was chemistry, though, and everything I knew about programming was self-taught. Even though I had plenty of C++ experience and had participated in GSoC, I lacked specific “domain knowledge.” To find a job, I spent most of 2025 interning and picking up a new field (unrelated to C++ frontends), while also dealing with university chores and everything involved in graduating. The hours I had left for clice shrank drastically compared to my earlier assumptions, so progress naturally slowed down.
- Getting the project genuinely production-ready also took far more than the core architecture. It meant working through countless corner cases of C++ syntax alongside subtle cross-platform differences: URI parsing, file paths and file uniqueness, OS-level quirks, and so on. The workload turned out to be much bigger than I had imagined. I also have a bit of an obsessive streak and dislike band-aid solutions that barely get by, so to build things the way I wanted I ended up reinventing quite a few wheels, covering serialization, IPC/LSP, async, and more. Had I chosen Rust at the start, development might have taken considerably less time, since those pieces already existed in the Rust ecosystem and had been thoroughly proven in other language servers. Still, writing a C++ language server in Rust felt odd, and it would have ruled out self-hosting, so I didn’t go that route.
- Lack of experience scheduling and managing complex software. Even with so much work on the table, several early contributors were already interested in the project and willing to put real time into helping write code. Unfortunately, I had very little teamwork experience back then (being completely self-taught and only three years into programming altogether), so I failed to turn that interest into effective contributions. That sank in much deeper once I started working in industry: 3 or 4 of my coworkers can split up responsibilities, stand up a project from scratch in 2 to 3 months, and ship it to production, whereas I had poor instincts for project planning and mostly just wrote whatever was on my mind at the moment. Open-source volunteer bandwidth can’t be compared to a full-time job, of course, yet the truth is I didn’t do a great job of building a community or pushing the project forward.
Early 2026 to July 2026: The turning point came when coding agents led by Claude Opus 4.6 started catching on. I began using agents to help with the mountain of tedious detail work and painful cross-platform debugging, and found they were remarkably good at it. During this stretch I did a sweeping cleanup of the existing codebase and established the prototype of clice as it stands today. As agents grew stronger, I handed them increasingly complex tasks and kept moving the project forward rapidly. It was an exciting period: I could finally stop burning hours on tedious details and focus on architecture and feature work. At the same time, it hit me that coding agents were only going to keep getting stronger. If humans stop writing code altogether in the future, what point does a language server even have? That pushed me to start designing and building clice’s CLI features.
July 2026 to present: By July I felt the timing was right to put out early test builds and iterate based on real user feedback. Core language server features were already in place and fully usable day to day, though still short of everything I had originally envisioned, so I threw myself into building out the rest of that vision, tuning performance, and fixing bugs. In October, I started planning clice’s modular self-hosting. That was a remarkably complicated undertaking, because C++ module support today is a long, painful story:
- CMake supports modules, yet if implementation units importing a module use different compile flags, CMake forks a separate compilation of the module even when those flags don’t affect PCM compatibility. CMake also has no built-in compilation cache, which matters a lot for CI; normally we reach for ccache to solve that, except ccache doesn’t support modules.
- clice has a demanding platform matrix: six combinations across Windows/Linux/macOS × ARM64/x64, plus ASan builds, prebuilt LLVM/Clang binaries to speed up CI, C++23 import std, and build artifacts that depend only on system libraries rather than extra shared libraries so everything works out of the box for users.
I surveyed the options extensively, and the answer was that an out-of-the-box setup for this simply did not exist in open source, and most open-source C++ module projects don’t have build requirements nearly this involved. After thinking it through carefully, I built xclang, inspired by zigcc, to give every target platform a unified cross-compilation experience, and paired it with Bazel (which includes built-in build caching) to solve the build problem for good. Going one step further, I wrote clice modularize to automatically generate module wrappers for every third-party library we depend on, completing clice’s self-hosted modularization! That probably makes clice one of the most complex open-source C++ projects using modules today.
Alongside all of this, I ran extensive fuzz testing across many open-source projects and fixed a huge number of bugs, bringing clice to genuine production readiness!
What about agents?
As I mentioned earlier, I designed a CLI interface for clice so that it would still hold some value in the agent era. An obvious idea is replacing grep with clice query: it is more accurate and uses fewer tokens. That is true, except exact symbol lookup requires indexing the whole project first, which takes a very long time. grep is imprecise, sure, yet 2 or 3 grep calls are usually enough to find the target, and the extra text grep pulls back isn’t necessarily a bad thing for helping an agent understand the codebase. Put plainly, most of this CLI isn’t actually all that useful. The part that really earns its keep is probably clice modularize: if you ask an agent to modularize a project on its own, it spins up a swarm of subagents and writes custom scripts to pull off the migration, which is both slow and expensive. Giving the agent clice modularize to assist the migration works much better. LLMs are trained first and foremost for generality, and what they do best is writing and combining scripts to generalize across problems. As things stand, only highly specialized tools still carry real value; otherwise you are usually better off letting the LLM compose its own scripts.
As agents keep getting stronger, especially with models led by Claude Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2, the cost of building complex software keeps falling, and 10x or even 100x the productivity of the past is well within reach. Over on X, you can see plenty of people asking Claude in a single sentence to rewrite the TypeScript compiler in another programming language and getting better performance out of it. Surprising at first glance, yet completely reasonable when you think about it. Software written in the past was bounded by human time, cost, and skill, so codebases inevitably accumulated lots of suboptimal code paths. When humans optimize performance, we go after the main bottlenecks, whereas an agent can tirelessly optimize every path until the small wins add up. Refactoring also often produces a better architecture than existing code; rewriting or refactoring software was just too expensive to justify before. In the foreseeable future, then, most code may be written entirely by agents, humans may stop writing code and even reading it (which is already happening now), and language servers may have no reason to exist at all. Agents might even invent programming languages better suited to themselves: compilation and interpretation themselves have often become the bottleneck in the agent loop, and agents already have the ability to optimize that underlying infrastructure; they just haven’t started yet. Ideally, every stage of the agent loop should be something the agent itself can improve and speed up, compilers and toolchains included.
To be continued
That is a sad reality, and in an era like this it means the effort I poured into clice over the past two and a half years is unlikely to have the lasting meaning I first imagined. Every time I dwell on it, I slip into a dark mood, bordering on nihilism. I can’t help asking myself: thousands of hours and a lot of money, and what was it all for?
Because I taught myself programming off the beaten path without a CS degree, I got used early on to the cycle of finding my own goals, defining problems, solving them, and then looking for the next goal. Interest alone only sustains me for a little while; sticking with something for the long haul takes some deeper “meaning” or “vision” behind it, ideally one I defined for myself. And now that meaning is fading away. So over the past few months, I started pushing myself relentlessly. The more those thoughts crept in, the harder I used agents to drive the project forward, trying to finish it before it lost its meaning altogether.
Imagine an alternate timeline where coding agents never took off. clice wouldn’t be nearly as complete as it is today, of course, yet I would have slowly gained experience, learned how to collaborate and plan work, built a community around it, and watched clice move forward slowly and steadily. Except the SOTA models have already been trained. Models like Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2 are real, and whether AI is a bubble or not, they aren’t going away. Software becoming cheap is already an undeniable fact.
We have to face reality, so what mindset should we bring to all of this? My thinking has shifted again recently, though honestly neither my mental state nor my daily life is in a great place right now. Pouring yourself too deeply into one thing naturally takes a heavy toll when it doesn’t turn out the way you hoped. Still, if you zoom out over a longer stretch of time, what does it really amount to? Even if I had done nothing at all for these two and a half years, so what? There is plenty of life ahead. I know that in principle, yet letting go is hard, especially when this project has been part of my life for over two years. As I mentioned in an earlier post, graduating from college never really felt real to me, because the rhythm of my life barely changed afterward: apart from my job, it was just this project. Something has to change eventually, though. I can treat clice as my graduation project: my real graduation project, not one from the chemistry major I never really liked. And that’s enough. I don’t need to force a grander meaning onto it. This graduation project has met every single goal I set two years ago, and exceeded them by a wide margin. It stands on its own as the best illustration of modern C++, neatly bringing together everything I studied about C++ in college. If on top of that it can be helpful to people just learning to program or curious about modern C++, that is plenty.
So this post is a kind of ritual for me. I need to step out of my old life and look for other things worth doing, maybe things outside code. I will keep maintaining clice, just not at the intensity I used to; practically everything worth building is already built anyway, so from here on it will mostly be incremental optimizations and bug fixes based on user feedback. In times like these, it is hard to say what the right path is, or what you ought to be doing. Nobody knows what will happen next week, let alone next year, or how far AI will actually go. I think it is time to rest for a while so I don’t burn out, and have the energy to face whatever problems come next. I still have a strong drive to keep creating good work. It just might not be about programming anymore, and figuring that out is what I need to learn and think through in this next stage.
That’s all for this post. Thanks for reading. I hope you’ll give clice a try, and feel free to report any problems!

====================================================================================================

Clice v1.0 Released: Welcome to Modern C++
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
17:22:32 UTC
02:22:32 KST

clice recently hit 1,000 commits, a special milestone for the project. Since July 19, 2026, clice has been shipping nightly builds and going through roughly four months of alpha testing, with a huge number of bugs fixed thanks to real-world feedback from early users. Over the same stretch, I built a rigorous testing system for clice and ran extensive tests across many real open-source projects, tracking down and fixing the issues they uncovered. What does that mean? Every core feature is now stable, and you rarely run into problems in day-to-day use. So I think the time is right to ship the first stable release. The project looks very different today from where it stood two years ago in Design and Implementation of a New C++ Language Server, and this is probably the first time I’m promoting it widely, so please let me introduce it to you once again! This is the project I have poured the past two and a half years into, the work I am proudest of, the one that has excited me the most and left me sad and anxious more times than I can count, and the most ambitious thing I have taken on in my short life.
What is clice?
In short, clice is a C/C++ language server written in modern C++, but it’s more than just a language server. It is still built on LLVM/Clang, but unlike clangd and ccls, which are also Clang-based, clice was redesigned from the ground up to fix architectural problems that have gone unsolved in those servers for years. It is written entirely in C++23 and serves as an example of modern C++ best practices in its own right, with all the major modern C++ features coming together remarkably well:
- Written entirely with C++20 coroutines, using our own libuv-based structured-concurrency async library for intricate asynchronous logic. It is a complex concurrent program running an event loop on the main thread while coordinating both multiple processes (IPC) and multiple threads.
- Written entirely with C++20 named modules and C++23 import std, and fully self-hosted: module wrappers for third-party libraries are generated automatically by clice modularize, and clice runs smoothly on its own codebase to provide module-aware language services.
- Uses static reflection built on templates and C++20 concepts for all serialization and deserialization across formats, including TOML, JSON, bincode, blob, and command-line arguments, with Rust-macro-like declarative annotations and zero boilerplate.
- Data-oriented code design throughout, with almost no virtual functions outside the callback interfaces Clang requires. Makes heavy use of modern C++ APIs like std::format, std::expected, std::ranges, and std::views, steering clear of outdated legacy C++98/11 APIs.
- A completely hermetic, reproducible build powered by Bazel, compiled with the unified xclang toolchain for a consistent cross-platform cross-compilation workflow. Compiled binaries are heavily optimized (PGO + LTO), and build artifacts depend on nothing beyond system libraries.
People are always debating whether modern C++ features like modules are actually ready for real work. clice’s answer is: yes, of course, completely usable! They hold up for complex software like clice, with hundreds of thousands of lines of code. Maybe this is what the modern C++ we have been hoping for looks like. That said, the surrounding ecosystem really is inconvenient enough right now that getting this whole workflow running end to end took a huge amount of effort on my part to track issues and adapt everything.
Language Server
Here is an overview of clice’s main features:
- Multi-process compilation: clice compiles code in a process pool modeled after modern web browsers, whereas clangd uses multithreading. Why multiple processes? Because C++ is simply too complex, and on certain corner cases the compiler can easily crash or leak memory. That is especially true for incomplete code in the middle of editing: a huge downstream user base tests compilers on complete code, while very few people test them on half-written code, where the compiler mostly falls back on best-effort heuristics. In clangd, a compiler crash takes down the whole server and forces a restart. In clice, compilation happens in isolated child processes: losing one worker does not affect the rest of the server, so go-to-definition, highlighting, and other services keep working. It also makes it much easier to manage memory usage and schedule compilation priorities, cutting down on unnecessary resource consumption.
- Modules and shared caching: clice has full support for C++20 named modules. All you need is a compile_commands.json containing all module files, and clice scans dependencies on its own and lazily builds required modules on demand, regardless of your build system or compiler, with full support for module completion, highlighting, go-to-definition, hover, and more. Built PCHs and PCMs are also cached on local disk for reuse across sessions. By comparison, clangd offers only very limited module support and no persistent cache.
- Compilation context: clice has full support for non-self-contained files, and more generally for the concept of a compilation context. You can explicitly pick the active context of a file: a specific compile command for a source file, or a specific inclusion site for a header. When you open a non-self-contained file, clice detects its context automatically, so it works out of the box. More broadly, it also supports grouping compile commands into build configurations, letting you switch between, say, debug and release.
- Better template support: clice implements a DFS pseudo instantiator that infers what many dependent names resolve to without needing concrete template arguments for a full instantiation. When a template has been instantiated once, clice also draws on that instantiation to power language features, such as completing on auto parameters of a lambda passed to a ranges call. On top of that, clice treats a template as a duck-typed interface and each instantiation as one of its implementations, much like a virtual function and its overrides: Go to Implementation on a dependent call inside a template lists the functions it actually calls across every instantiation, and Find References and Call Hierarchy work the same way.
- Out-of-the-box experience: Most projects need nothing more than a compile_commands.json. clice ships with built-in environment probing for 50+ toolchains, covering nvcc, MSYS2, Cygwin, zigcc, and more, automatically handling mismatches between your actual compiler and Clang. You don’t have to manually add piles of compiler flags to a config file the way you do with clangd.
Beyond these main features, clice also fixes a long list of smaller clangd issues that have gone untouched for years across semantic highlighting, folding, hover, and more. If you’re interested, give it a try and see what surprises you find. Overall, clice surpasses clangd in ease of use, feature support, and performance. You can install the clice extension in VS Code and other editors and start using it right now.
Command Line
Besides running as a language server, clice can also be used from the CLI to access its language features directly. Currently supported subcommands include:
- clice index: Builds a persistent index for the entire project ahead of time and supports resuming interrupted runs, so your editor starts warm as soon as you open the project.
- clice lint: Built-in clang-tidy runner that checks translation units concurrently across a process pool and merges duplicate diagnostics from headers; pass --index to reuse the same parse pass to build the index along the way.
- clice format: A wrapper around clang-format that automatically finds files to format from your config file or CDB and formats them efficiently in parallel.
- clice command: Shows the effective compile command for a file after rule rewrites and toolchain probing, or converts compile_commands.json into resolved -cc1 commands for other Clang tools.
- clice query: Reads the on-disk index directly without starting a server and outputs symbol definitions, references, call graphs, inheritance hierarchies, and file dependencies as JSON.
- clice refactor: Rewrites source files directly from the persistent index without recompiling, supporting project-wide symbol and macro renaming as well as batch renames in one pass via --spec.
- clice analyze: Outputs project-structure JSON from the index to guide refactoring; for example, analyze modules analyzes module dependencies and cycles across directories and lists code patterns that stand in the way of modularization.
- clice modularize: Automatically wraps third-party libraries used by a project as C++20 modules without modifying any source files; following the split analyze proposes, it can also rewrite the project’s own code into module units, turning headers into partitions and #include into import. clice used it to modularize itself.
The story behind it
That wraps up the feature tour. Next, I want to talk about the story behind the project, some of its design decisions, and my own thoughts and struggles along the way, especially now, in the age of AI.
Why not improve clangd?
First, why start a new project instead of improving clangd? That is the question I get asked most often. There are several reasons. I discussed some of them in my earliest post, and today those problems are even easier to see firsthand:
- Maintenance and staffing: At root, clangd has been effectively unmaintained since around 2024. That sounds surprising. How could a tool used by hundreds of thousands of developers have no one maintaining it? I stumbled onto this fact in 2024 when I was getting ready to submit a PR to clangd for better code completion inside templates. From what I understand, it ties back to a shift in Google’s C++ strategy. Before that, clangd was maintained mainly by several Google engineers, and around 2024 Google started scaling back its investment in C++ (Clang); clangd was deemed good enough for Google’s internal needs, and those engineers were reassigned elsewhere. Since then, clangd has lacked any real long-term maintainers. Only Nathan Ridge has been answering questions unpaid in his spare time, purely out of passion, and one person only has so much bandwidth, making it nearly impossible to handle large PRs. That is why clangd’s C++20 modules PR dragged on for nearly a year, and even today it still isn’t really usable on large projects, with plenty of flaws and nobody working on them. Earlier this year, in 2026, Clang’s lead maintainer Aaron Ballman recognized the problem and posted a call for help on the community forum, saying clangd is very close to unmaintained, with a heavy backlog of PRs and issues, and hoping to find new community maintainers; otherwise clangd could eventually be removed from the LLVM project.
- Scale of the code changes: As mentioned at the start, clice introduces many new architectural ideas, such as multi-process compilation. Implementing those inside clangd would come close to rewriting clangd. Nobody is going to review and accept changes of that size. Self-hosting with modules would also require sweeping changes to LLVM’s build system, which I will go into in detail when I introduce xclang later.
- LLVM repository constraints: clice relies on a lot of third-party libraries for better performance and user experience, such as libuv, simdjson, and tomlplusplus. To keep builds simple, LLVM requires the repository to remain self-contained, so pulling in that many third-party libraries is out of the question. You would either have to rewrite and port them into LLVM or put up with worse ergonomics and lower performance. On top of that, a language server is not a standalone project: it also includes extensions and adapters for different editors. Keeping them in the same repo as the server makes maintenance much easier, and LLVM would be just as unlikely to accept that editor code (spanning Rust, Lua, TypeScript, and so on). For long-term maintainability and our core goals, a separate repository was unavoidable.
So in mid-2024, I started looking into how to design and write a new language server. At the end of 2024, I published Design and Implementation of a New C++ Language Server, setting out clice’s core goals and kicking off development in earnest.
How did we get here?
Next, let’s talk about how clice was built. How did it get to its current state? Over the past two and a half years, at a rough guess, I have put nearly 3,000 hours into this project. Even that number doesn’t really tell the story, because clice became part of my life. It was always on my mind, like eating or sleeping: there wasn’t a moment when I wasn’t turning over some problem related to it. Development broke down into roughly four stages:
Mid-2024 to early 2025: I spent this period studying Clang and clangd’s codebase and internals. Anyone who has looked through my older blog posts will know I am a longtime template metaprogramming enthusiast, familiar with C++ and its implementation details down to language-lawyer territory. During this phase I settled on clice’s core goals and overall design, and put together an initial demo. I was a third-year undergrad at the time: I would wake up and start coding right away, skip lunch, walk out to the McDonald’s outside the campus gate when I got hungry, and put in 8 to 10 hours a day.
Early 2025 to early 2026: I originally expected to ship a usable version of clice fairly quickly during this stage. Instead, progress went much slower than I expected, and it remained a long way from production-ready. Looking back, there were a few main reasons:
- Above all, time. I had assumed I would keep putting in 8 to 10 hours a day like before. My undergraduate major was chemistry, though, and everything I knew about programming was self-taught. Even though I had plenty of C++ experience and had participated in GSoC, I lacked specific “domain knowledge.” To find a job, I spent most of 2025 interning and picking up a new field (unrelated to C++ frontends), while also dealing with university chores and everything involved in graduating. The hours I had left for clice shrank drastically compared to my earlier assumptions, so progress naturally slowed down.
- Getting the project genuinely production-ready also took far more than the core architecture. It meant working through countless corner cases of C++ syntax alongside subtle cross-platform differences: URI parsing, file paths and file uniqueness, OS-level quirks, and so on. The workload turned out to be much bigger than I had imagined. I also have a bit of an obsessive streak and dislike band-aid solutions that barely get by, so to build things the way I wanted I ended up reinventing quite a few wheels, covering serialization, IPC/LSP, async, and more. Had I chosen Rust at the start, development might have taken considerably less time, since those pieces already existed in the Rust ecosystem and had been thoroughly proven in other language servers. Still, writing a C++ language server in Rust felt odd, and it would have ruled out self-hosting, so I didn’t go that route.
- Lack of experience scheduling and managing complex software. Even with so much work on the table, several early contributors were already interested in the project and willing to put real time into helping write code. Unfortunately, I had very little teamwork experience back then (being completely self-taught and only three years into programming altogether), so I failed to turn that interest into effective contributions. That sank in much deeper once I started working in industry: 3 or 4 of my coworkers can split up responsibilities, stand up a project from scratch in 2 to 3 months, and ship it to production, whereas I had poor instincts for project planning and mostly just wrote whatever was on my mind at the moment. Open-source volunteer bandwidth can’t be compared to a full-time job, of course, yet the truth is I didn’t do a great job of building a community or pushing the project forward.
Early 2026 to July 2026: The turning point came when coding agents led by Claude Opus 4.6 started catching on. I began using agents to help with the mountain of tedious detail work and painful cross-platform debugging, and found they were remarkably good at it. During this stretch I did a sweeping cleanup of the existing codebase and established the prototype of clice as it stands today. As agents grew stronger, I handed them increasingly complex tasks and kept moving the project forward rapidly. It was an exciting period: I could finally stop burning hours on tedious details and focus on architecture and feature work. At the same time, it hit me that coding agents were only going to keep getting stronger. If humans stop writing code altogether in the future, what point does a language server even have? That pushed me to start designing and building clice’s CLI features.
July 2026 to present: By July I felt the timing was right to put out early test builds and iterate based on real user feedback. Core language server features were already in place and fully usable day to day, though still short of everything I had originally envisioned, so I threw myself into building out the rest of that vision, tuning performance, and fixing bugs. In October, I started planning clice’s modular self-hosting. That was a remarkably complicated undertaking, because C++ module support today is a long, painful story:
- CMake supports modules, yet if implementation units importing a module use different compile flags, CMake forks a separate compilation of the module even when those flags don’t affect PCM compatibility. CMake also has no built-in compilation cache, which matters a lot for CI; normally we reach for ccache to solve that, except ccache doesn’t support modules.
- clice has a demanding platform matrix: six combinations across Windows/Linux/macOS × ARM64/x64, plus ASan builds, prebuilt LLVM/Clang binaries to speed up CI, C++23 import std, and build artifacts that depend only on system libraries rather than extra shared libraries so everything works out of the box for users.
I surveyed the options extensively, and the answer was that an out-of-the-box setup for this simply did not exist in open source, and most open-source C++ module projects don’t have build requirements nearly this involved. After thinking it through carefully, I built xclang, inspired by zigcc, to give every target platform a unified cross-compilation experience, and paired it with Bazel (which includes built-in build caching) to solve the build problem for good. Going one step further, I wrote clice modularize to automatically generate module wrappers for every third-party library we depend on, completing clice’s self-hosted modularization! That probably makes clice one of the most complex open-source C++ projects using modules today.
Alongside all of this, I ran extensive fuzz testing across many open-source projects and fixed a huge number of bugs, bringing clice to genuine production readiness!
What about agents?
As I mentioned earlier, I designed a CLI interface for clice so that it would still hold some value in the agent era. An obvious idea is replacing grep with clice query: it is more accurate and uses fewer tokens. That is true, except exact symbol lookup requires indexing the whole project first, which takes a very long time. grep is imprecise, sure, yet 2 or 3 grep calls are usually enough to find the target, and the extra text grep pulls back isn’t necessarily a bad thing for helping an agent understand the codebase. Put plainly, most of this CLI isn’t actually all that useful. The part that really earns its keep is probably clice modularize: if you ask an agent to modularize a project on its own, it spins up a swarm of subagents and writes custom scripts to pull off the migration, which is both slow and expensive. Giving the agent clice modularize to assist the migration works much better. LLMs are trained first and foremost for generality, and what they do best is writing and combining scripts to generalize across problems. As things stand, only highly specialized tools still carry real value; otherwise you are usually better off letting the LLM compose its own scripts.
As agents keep getting stronger, especially with models led by Claude Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2, the cost of building complex software keeps falling, and 10x or even 100x the productivity of the past is well within reach. Over on X, you can see plenty of people asking Claude in a single sentence to rewrite the TypeScript compiler in another programming language and getting better performance out of it. Surprising at first glance, yet completely reasonable when you think about it. Software written in the past was bounded by human time, cost, and skill, so codebases inevitably accumulated lots of suboptimal code paths. When humans optimize performance, we go after the main bottlenecks, whereas an agent can tirelessly optimize every path until the small wins add up. Refactoring also often produces a better architecture than existing code; rewriting or refactoring software was just too expensive to justify before. In the foreseeable future, then, most code may be written entirely by agents, humans may stop writing code and even reading it (which is already happening now), and language servers may have no reason to exist at all. Agents might even invent programming languages better suited to themselves: compilation and interpretation themselves have often become the bottleneck in the agent loop, and agents already have the ability to optimize that underlying infrastructure; they just haven’t started yet. Ideally, every stage of the agent loop should be something the agent itself can improve and speed up, compilers and toolchains included.
To be continued
That is a sad reality, and in an era like this it means the effort I poured into clice over the past two and a half years is unlikely to have the lasting meaning I first imagined. Every time I dwell on it, I slip into a dark mood, bordering on nihilism. I can’t help asking myself: thousands of hours and a lot of money, and what was it all for?
Because I taught myself programming off the beaten path without a CS degree, I got used early on to the cycle of finding my own goals, defining problems, solving them, and then looking for the next goal. Interest alone only sustains me for a little while; sticking with something for the long haul takes some deeper “meaning” or “vision” behind it, ideally one I defined for myself. And now that meaning is fading away. So over the past few months, I started pushing myself relentlessly. The more those thoughts crept in, the harder I used agents to drive the project forward, trying to finish it before it lost its meaning altogether.
Imagine an alternate timeline where coding agents never took off. clice wouldn’t be nearly as complete as it is today, of course, yet I would have slowly gained experience, learned how to collaborate and plan work, built a community around it, and watched clice move forward slowly and steadily. Except the SOTA models have already been trained. Models like Opus 5.5 and Fable 5.2 are real, and whether AI is a bubble or not, they aren’t going away. Software becoming cheap is already an undeniable fact.
We have to face reality, so what mindset should we bring to all of this? My thinking has shifted again recently, though honestly neither my mental state nor my daily life is in a great place right now. Pouring yourself too deeply into one thing naturally takes a heavy toll when it doesn’t turn out the way you hoped. Still, if you zoom out over a longer stretch of time, what does it really amount to? Even if I had done nothing at all for these two and a half years, so what? There is plenty of life ahead. I know that in principle, yet letting go is hard, especially when this project has been part of my life for over two years. As I mentioned in an earlier post, graduating from college never really felt real to me, because the rhythm of my life barely changed afterward: apart from my job, it was just this project. Something has to change eventually, though. I can treat clice as my graduation project: my real graduation project, not one from the chemistry major I never really liked. And that’s enough. I don’t need to force a grander meaning onto it. This graduation project has met every single goal I set two years ago, and exceeded them by a wide margin. It stands on its own as the best illustration of modern C++, neatly bringing together everything I studied about C++ in college. If on top of that it can be helpful to people just learning to program or curious about modern C++, that is plenty.
So this post is a kind of ritual for me. I need to step out of my old life and look for other things worth doing, maybe things outside code. I will keep maintaining clice, just not at the intensity I used to; practically everything worth building is already built anyway, so from here on it will mostly be incremental optimizations and bug fixes based on user feedback. In times like these, it is hard to say what the right path is, or what you ought to be doing. Nobody knows what will happen next week, let alone next year, or how far AI will actually go. I think it is time to rest for a while so I don’t burn out, and have the energy to face whatever problems come next. I still have a strong drive to keep creating good work. It just might not be about programming anymore, and figuring that out is what I need to learn and think through in this next stage.
That’s all for this post. Thanks for reading. I hope you’ll give clice a try, and feel free to report any problems!

Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and now, more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility!
I am traveling through Portugal now that Web Summit has wrapped up, so please enjoy this shortened edition.
Web Summit leaned heavily into Chinese founders and companies this year. The opening remarks from Web Summit co-founder Paddy Cosgrave drove the point home when he said the superstars of the future are coming from China, not California. He also gave a strong endorsement of China and open source technology when he noted that Chinese companies are building more of our shared future than anyone else.
These remarks carried a different kind of weight just a few days later as the news spread throughout the conference that Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang had resigned from his leadership post after admitting to an affair with an alleged spy.
The former CEO, after all, has been one of the strongest proponents for open source in the United States. And anyone who has followed the emergence of autonomous vehicle tech knows how central Nvidia is to that ecosystem.
Nvidia is a major supplier of AI computing technology to the industry — although the degree of reliance varies. Some companies are using Nvidia’s automotive processors to run automated driving software, while others are using Nvidia GPUs to train autonomous driving models. And then there are the full-stack partnerships, which includes Mercedes-Benz, Zoox, and Wayve.
I spoke to Leo Wang, the CFO of Chinese autonomous vehicle company Pony.ai, this week. I asked him about the challenges China’s self-driving industry faces and what he thinks will happen if the fallout from Huang’s resignation results in even stricter export controls on Nvidia chips shipped to China.
Demand is already far greater than supply, Wang said, noting that it’s one of the pain points Pony.ai struggles with. The company’s strategy is twofold. Engineers are directed to be as efficient as possible when deploying its training models. That might mean using midtier compute resources instead of high-end ones, he said.
Pony.ai is also turning more to domestically produced chips to help close the gap.
“If you look at the whole ecosystem, especially in recent years, definitely domestic chips in China also get lots of attention and development,” he said, noting that these chips provide another option for AI training. “I think that gives us some flexibility.”
When asked what he thinks will happen if export restrictions on Nvidia chips tighten, Wang said Chinese companies have already been preparing.
“If you look at the whole situation, this has been developing for already a couple of years,” he said. “Everyone, I would say, is also practicing kind of like a Plan B for a few years, so at least if you think about that, I would say there are alternatives. Although cost-effectiveness-wise, probably it’s not the best choice yet. But still, it’s an alternative way that already exists and which is more mature than a few years ago.”
Deals!
May Mobility plans to use the Volkswagen all-electric ID. Buzz for its global ride-hailing platform. The company said it is targeting the deployment of more than 10,000 autonomous vehicles through 2031. This does not mean that VW’s MOIA group is ditching Mobileye, I have been told. Instead, VW told me, the company is offering an automated driving-ready base vehicle to other companies that develop their own self-driving systems. (This was just presented last month during Nvidia GTC in Berlin.)
Newmo, a Japanese robotaxi startup, raised a ¥20.8 billion (roughly $135 million) round led by KDDI.
ParkBee, a Dutch parking technology company, acquired Mobian’s city and parking operations.
Port, an urban logistics company, raised €14.5 million in a round led by the European Bank for Reconstruction and Development (EBRD) and Balnord.
Notable reads and other tidbits
Lucid reported Q3 revenue of $350 million, below Wall Street expectations, and it is still losing money. Although its Q3 net loss did narrow to $830.9 million. Notably, the EV maker increased its delayed draw term loan from its largest shareholder, Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund, to $1.75 billion.
The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration is investigating a Waymo software recall from a year ago that was issued after numerous reports that its robotaxis were illegally passing school buses. The agency said that a majority of these reported incidents occurred after the company deployed a software update intended to fix the problem. Sources familiar with Waymo tell me that there have not been school bus-related incidents since March. I have not been able to independently verify that with the Austin Independent School District.
The Tesla Cybertruck will live on through next summer, but production will ultimately end, according to Elon Musk, who defended the polarizing steel-clad truck.
Tesla is expanding its robotaxi service to San Antonio, Texas, as it seeks to compete with Waymo and position itself as more than just an automaker.
Uber is launching a new service that lets customers reserve and pay for parking in major U.S. cities, about nine months after the ride-hailing company acquired parking app SpotHero. Meanwhile, the company is notifying thousands of drivers that their personal and driver’s license information was stolen during an October data breach at one of the company’s tech providers.
United Airlines will restrict access to AI agents seeking to book flights and manage reservations on their users’ behalf.

Skip the Charcuterie Board: This Is the Secret to the Best 2-Ingredient Thanksgiving Appetizer
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
17:00:00 UTC
02:00:00 KST

Skip the Charcuterie Board: This Is the Secret to the Best 2-Ingredient Thanksgiving Appetizer
It’s the holiday season, which means it’s also peak winter entertaining season. (Bring on all the festive holiday parties!) Of course, no gathering is complete without something to nibble on. And while a classic charcuterie board is something that certainly no one is going to turn their nose up at, I’m here to argue that baked brie should be on the table too. It comes together with a few store-bought ingredients and hardly any effort at all. Here’s why it deserves a spot at your next get-together.
Get the recipe: Baked Brie in Puff Pastry
What Makes Baked Brie in Puff Pastry So Good
Making this crowd-pleasing and impressive app couldn’t be any easier. All you need to do is grab some puff pastry, wrap a wheel of brie in it, bake, and voila! It’s like an instant fondue — and who doesn’t love melted cheese with some bread, crackers, or fresh fruit to dip into it?
You can take it up a notch and add in some caramelized onions, jam, or nuts to take the flavors to the next level. It’s also a great make-ahead appetizer because you can do the wrapping beforehand, then just take it out of the fridge or freezer, brush with the egg wash, and bake when needed. It’s perfect for a holiday party or a cozy night in with a few friends. Trust us — everyone will love it, and no one needs to know how easy it was to make.
How to Make Baked Brie in Puff Pastry
- Wrap the brie. Roll out a sheet of puff pastry, wrap your wheel of cheese in it (add any toppings if desired), and fold it up like a package.
- Brush and bake. Brush the puff pastry with egg wash, then bake for 35 to 40 minutes until the puff pastry is golden-brown.
- Cool and serve. Let the baked brie cool for a few minutes before digging in and serving warm.
Tips for Making Baked Brie in Puff Pastry
- Get creative with the toppings. Baked brie is delicious as is, but adding some fig jam, caramelized onions, cranberry sauce, or roasted nuts and honey really take the flavors to the next level.
- Use a pie plate. Rather than cooking the baked Brie on a sheet pan, cook it in a pie plate for easier serving and to collect all that runny, melted cheese.
Get the recipe: Baked Brie in Puff Pastry

Apple’s smart glasses are among the worst-kept secrets in the tech world. They likely won’t ship right away, however, with Gurman saying only that “glasses shipments would begin before the end of the year.”
Apple’s first smart glasses are coming together
The company’s Siri AI-powered eyewear is set to be announced at WWDC next year.
Key to their success will be the newly revamped Siri AI, which is adding fine-grained control over apps. By adding those features now and letting developers add support on the iPhone first, it’s hoping that its smart glasses will be able to hit the ground running.
The prototypes, dubbed N50, are currently running a pared-down version of visionOS and a new processor based on its older smartwatch chips. They also have a MagSafe-like charger Gurman describes as “flat, rectangular” and “roughly the size of a Tic Tac,” which can be used even while still wearing the glasses.
In addition to Apple putting an emphasis on style, privacy, and audio quality, the company is also hoping that Wi-Fi connectivity will grant them a certain amount of freedom, despite relying on an iPhone to actually execute many tasks:
The glasses have Wi-Fi, allowing certain functions outside of third-party apps to continue working when the iPhone isn’t nearby. Users could theoretically keep listening to audio over Wi-Fi after walking away from their phones, for instance, though the eyewear won’t operate as a fully independent device. Apple is also emphasizing the sound quality, which employees have said is industry-leading, with support for phone calls, music playback and FaceTime audio communication.

Big Grey Has Big Problems
Posted on November 15, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 12 Comments
I’m sure you’ve all been patiently waiting for an update on Big Grey, so I won’t keep you in suspense any longer! Here’s his joyous expression on the way to the vet:
I ended up taking Big Grey to Acute Pet Urgent Care in Beavercreek, and I told them ahead of time that it’s safe to assume that anything that could possibly be wrong with this cat is probably, indeed, wrong with this cat.
And boy was I right! This guy has the works; fleas, earmites, malnutrition, upper respiratory infection, dental disease, heart murmur, and leukemia. And possibly asthma. Also it turns out that ol’ Grey here is an older gentleman, estimated to be around 7-10.
Shaved, flea medicated, injected with antibiotics, he got the full treatment. The vet also said he will more than likely need all of his teeth pulled, he has huge masses in his mouth that are full of pus. Needless to say, this guy has been through the wringer. But at least the vet gave him these stickers:
Big Grey is curled up in a corner still entirely petrified. He eats his food when I’m not looking and uses the litterbox, too, but I have genuinely not seen him move a single muscle or heard him make a single noise at all. He is just a gargoyle in the corner, shaved and sad.
So, yeah, things are still pretty rough but I can only hope he’s a least feeling a little better with all those mats off him, and hopefully the antibiotics are running their course through his immunocompromised system. I just want him to be happy, and he seems so very not happy right now. I just want to help him and love him and he’s completely terrified of me. Sigh.
Anyways, he still needs fixed, and eventually will need dental work. But for now he can just rest while the medicine soaks in. I won’t stop stepping up to the plate for this guy.
Also, shoutout to Acute Pet for handling Big Grey with so much care and compassion. Everyone there was very friendly and the doctor went over everything in immense detail and let me ask as many questions as I wanted. I didn’t feel rushed or confused about anything they were doing throughout the entire process, and it was clear that they really were rooting for this little guy. As we all are.

Bill Gates Has Earned Billions Investing in Climate-Polluting Landfills, Reports ProPublica

Bill Gates Has Earned Billions Investing in Climate-Polluting Landfills, Reports ProPublica (propublica.org) 47
			
		 	
				Environmental activist Bill Gates has "quietly accumulated a $29 billion stake in America's No. 2 landfill operator, Republic Services," reports ProPublica, in a new 5,000-word investigation. But inspectors from America's Environmental Protection Agency "have repeatedly found methane — a highly potent greenhouse gas — escaping from Republic landfills at levels above federal limits.  Republic has lobbied against regulations that could make the waste industry cleaner, including by keeping organic garbage out of landfills in the first place."
While Gates has invested at least $2 billion in green companies, he's "put more than double that amount into a very dirty, old-school industry," their article argues. And Gates has increased his stake in Republic "to 38% of the company, a position worth $29 billion."
While fossil fuels get most of the attention, scientists increasingly see landfills as a crucial climate issue. They're responsible for as much as a fifth of the country's emissions of methane, a highly potent greenhouse gas, studies show. Republic alone generates as much climate pollution as 3 million gasoline-burning cars each year, according to its own reports....
[Gates] has failed to make Republic live up to his own climate principles, a ProPublica investigation has found. Federal inspectors have repeatedly found methane escaping from Republic landfills at levels above federal limits. The company has refrained from adopting the ambitious emissions-reduction goal embraced by its main competitor... "It seems very likely that Bill Gates has the power to influence Republic's behavior to make it more climate-friendly, even if that costs the company profits," said Ann Lipton, a legal scholar who studies corporate governance at the University of Colorado, Boulder... Gates didn't respond to repeated requests to discuss his landfill investments, and ProPublica couldn't find any public comments he's made about them.  
But the bet has proven wildly lucrative. Since November 1999, when he emerged as the owner of 5% of Republic's shares — "It's 'Garbage In' for Gates," the New York Post headline read — the company's stock price has soared thirtyfold. And Gates has personally received $2.6 billion in dividends, a ProPublica analysis of securities filings shows... A rare clue to why Gates made his foray into landfills can be found in an interview he gave to Fortune [in 1999]... To fund his plan to give away virtually his entire fortune, he said he'd given his personal money manager, Michael Larson, a simple remit: Sell Microsoft shares, buy safe, boring assets... After that initial investment, Larson progressively increased Gates' stake in Republic over the years that followed. [On behalf of the Gates Foundation] Larson would also acquire a small chunk of Republic's main competitor, Waste Management.
 
Methane emissions weren't a major focus of climate activism until years later
The gas can trap 80 times more heat than carbon dioxide in the near term, and it's responsible for one-third of the global warming we experience today, according to the United Nations Environment Programme. But it also breaks down much more quickly, turning into CO2 and water vapor in about a decade — which means that cutting methane emissions now would reduce that warming within our lifetimes. This is especially urgent, scientists say, as the climate approaches tipping points that could doom rainforests, coral reefs and ice sheets.

====================================================================================================

What Picasso taught me about building AI Agents
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
16:16:26 UTC
01:16:26 KST

The great master Pablo Picasso is famously attributed with saying:
"It took me four years to paint like Raphael, but a lifetime to paint like a child."
His work, Le Taureau (The Bull), captures this beautifully. Across 11 iterations, Picasso progressively stripped away the details of a bull until only a few lines remained. Yet the bull was unmistakable.
In my journey of learning to build AI agents, I can relate. It has come full circle.
Like many engineers, I started with abstractions and sophisticated software systems. Frameworks, orchestration engines, multi-agent architectures, state machines, vector databases, memory systems, and elaborate planning mechanisms.
The more I experimented, the more I found myself stripping things away.
The models were getting better. More things worked with a single prompt. But that wasn't the whole story. I had been overcomplicating things.
This is now my workflow.
I open my terminal.
mkdir my_awesome_agent
cd my_awesome_agent
claude
and start a conversation.
"Claude, I want to build an agent that does XYZ. Help me initialize it. Create an AGENTS.md and let's figure out what else it needs."
I work through a few questions with Claude.
What is this agent supposed to accomplish?
What is the actual job? Not what framework to use, unless you're creating a framework. What does success look like? What decisions can the agent make independently? What should it never do without permission?
What skills does it need?
Skills are specialized instructions that can be reused and remixed. There are plenty of excellent examples on the internet. Borrow them, adapt them, combine them, or ask Claude to create new ones.
Often, a skill is simply a Markdown file with instructions and perhaps a few supporting resources.
What context does it need?
The agent will need context: what your business does, who your customers are, how your codebase works, or some domain-specific knowledge. These can all live as Markdown files inside the folder. Some context can be retrieved when needed. Some comes from the conversation itself. You don't automatically need a vector database or a sophisticated memory architecture.
What tools does it need?
To perform a job, an agent needs tools. Maybe it needs to search the web, read a database, call an API, execute a script, or interact with a browser.
Introducing a tool is as simple as enabling a plugin or adding an MCP connector.
I ask Claude to help identify the smallest set of tools required to accomplish the task. A tool could even be created dynamically, with the agent writing and invoking code. The agent could also find tools on the internet and install them.
How does the agent get invoked?
For an agent to be useful, it needs to be invoked as part of a workflow, whether by a cron job, a webhook event, or another program.
The simplicity is in the packaging. A folder with instructions is a clean deployable unit. It can be versioned, copied around, and embedded in other processes.
With Claude and the required tools and credentials available, it can be invoked headless using the -p flag:
cd my_awesome_agent
claude -p "Do awesome stuff"
Headless means there is no interactive chat. Claude receives the task, works in the agent's folder, prints the result, and exits. A script can pass in the task and collect the output. For unattended work, I configure the tool permissions in advance. Claude can also return structured JSON for another program to use.
Anthropic's documentation on headless execution covers the commands and configuration.
When the agent starts, it needs to know where to find its instructions, what input it has received, and whether it should resume previous work. The invocation mechanism can change without changing the fundamental purpose of the agent.
This setup can be deployed on a remote VM, with hooks triggering the agent and a tunnel providing access to a service that invokes it.
How does it know whether it has done a good job?
This is where things have drastically improved. I can now get things done with a single prompt, without elaborate instructions. Nowadays, I often just trust it to get on with the job. And I expect it to keep getting better.
When it gets something wrong, I go through it with Claude. What happened? What was missing? What should we change?
Claude saves local session transcripts under ~/.claude/projects/<project>/<session>.jsonl. Each transcript records the conversation, tool calls, and tool results. It provides a trace of how the task unfolded: what Claude was asked to do, what it tried, what came back from the tools, and where errors appeared.
Claude can then be pointed at the transcript and asked to analyze the run. Where did it go off track? Did it miss an instruction, lack context, or misinterpret a tool result? What evidence in the trace supports that explanation? That gives us something concrete to examine before deciding what to change.
If the lesson is reusable, we save it in the instructions, context, examples, or tests.
Over time, the agent improves. Not necessarily because its underlying model has changed, but because its working context and operating instructions have become better.
How do we know it is improving?
To be rigorous about the implementation, you need benchmarks: representative tasks with clear criteria for success. Running the same tasks lets you compare your agent across harnesses such as Gemini CLI, Codex, or pi, or across models such as Opus, Astra, Sol, and DeepSeek. It also tells you whether changes to your own setup are helping.
These tests are called evals. This is probably the hardest part of all of this. I will share my experiences with evals in a later post. In the meantime, these writeups explain them in much more depth:
- Hugo Bowne-Anderson: Does Your AI Agent Actually Work? A Guide to Evals
- Hamel Husain and Shreya Shankar: AI Evals: Everything You Need to Know
Orchestration
A task can be broken into smaller tasks and delegated to specialized subagents. In this setup, each subagent has its own folder under the main agent, containing its instructions, context, and tools.
The root agent acts as an orchestrator, or chief of staff. It decides which subagent to invoke, gives it a task, and brings the results together. For example, a chief-of-staff agent could coordinate all kinds of activity across a company, delegating work to specialist agents.
Making this concrete
I am using this setup to publish this website, including the article you are reading. For this project, I am using Codex.
The agent works in the website's project folder. AGENTS.md points it to my editorial guidance and publishing instructions. Those files capture my preferences and the steps for updating the site. Here is a shortened view of the actual folder:
claramap/
├── AGENTS.md
├── docs/
│   ├── editorial-memory.md
│   └── publishing.md
├── content/posts/
│   └── what-picasso-taught-me-about-building-ai-agents.md
├── public/assets/blog/
└── scripts/
    ├── build.mjs
    └── check-live.mjs
The agent is connected to Cloudflare through MCP, so it can deploy the site as part of the workflow.
For this article, I asked it to add the orchestration section and create the chief-of-staff diagram. It edited the Markdown, generated the illustration, built the site, deployed to Cloudflare, and checked the live page.
The publishing instructions also cover Google Analytics and discovery files. The build automatically updates the site's analytics tracking code for its current pages, regenerates llms.txt and llms-full.txt, and updates the RSS feed.
I review the result and correct it in the same conversation. Change the wording. Move a diagram. Make an image bigger. Reusable feedback goes back into the editorial memory, so future runs have better instructions to work with.
The Essence
This is the foundation. Everything else builds on this.
No need for OpenClaw, Hermes, Muse, LangChain, Pydantic, or any other agent framework. Solve the basic case first, then add machinery later. You can find leads, create content, publish, send emails, answer questions, fill out forms, or write code. Pick a job. Give it what it needs. Let it work.
Like Picasso's bull, my approach became simpler as I learned what to leave out. The progression looks something like this.

The Atheist Bible
A Secular Humanist view on life, the Universe, and everything
CC-BY 2006-2026 Fabian M. Suchanek
This book describes a worldview without gods and the supernatural. Unlike a number of other books, it does not merely argue that God does not exist or that religion is harmful. Rather, it offers an atheist perspective on life, the Universe, morality, truth, and the meaning of life. To this end, it draws inspiration from Secular Humanism, a philosophy in the Enlightenment tradition that values every human being equally and emphasizes ethical behavior, science, democracy, human rights, and freedom of religion. The book also explores the history, functioning, tenets, diversity, benefits, and liabilities of the major world religions. Thus, it aims to be a comprehensive handbook that atheists can use to support, enrich, and scrutinize their perspective.
With more than 700 pages, 300 illustrations, 500 inspirational quotes, 1700 references, and 1500 cross-references, the Atheist Bible is one of the largest works on atheism, and, quite possibly, the largest monograph on the topic. The book is licensed by the author, Fabian M. Suchanek, under a Creative Commons Attribution license. See the legal conditions at the end of the book.
