'Duffy Lied': Controllers Refute Transportation Secretary's Ground Stop Story
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
15:45:42 UTC
00:45:42 KST

SUBSCRIBE TO OUR FREE NEWSLETTER
Daily news & progressive opinion—funded by the people, not the corporations—delivered straight to your inbox.
To donate by check, phone, or other method, see our More Ways to Give page.
"I’ve spoken with 60 air traffic controllers tonight about what Sean Duffy said at the White House today," said one journalist. "None of them are buying what he said."
Air traffic controllers are rejecting US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy's explanation for Friday's nationwide flight ground stop, raising questions about the Trump administration's account of a communications failure that disrupted air travel across the United States, affecting an estimated 1.5 million travelers.
Washington Examiner homeland security reporter Anna Giaritelli told CNN's Laura Coates during a Friday night interview that she'd spoken with 60 air traffic controllers about the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) ground stop, which was just the third to ever occur.
"None of them are buying what he said," Giaritelli said. "The controllers were saying, 'We don’t buy that this is actually what’s happening, the reason that Sean Duffy is saying this has happened.'"
Duffy lied.
[image or embed]
— Ron Filipkowski (@ronfilipkowski.bsky.social) November 14, 2026 at 3:24 AM
At a White House briefing Friday evening, Duffy blamed the disruption on America's aging air traffic control infrastructure, saying overloaded switches and routers had produced inconsistent radar and communications data.
“Our switches, our routers actually got overloaded with data,” he said. “And because of that, we shut the system down.”
But Giaritelli said the controllers she contacted questioned the plausibility of the administration's account, particularly the timing of the alleged equipment changes.
“These types of switches... that the FAA is doing, they don't happen during the day,” she told Coates. “They don't happen during busy times like a Friday afternoon. They're doing these midweek, middle of the night, when there is no air traffic going on."
Giaritelli's comments came as the Trump administration struggled to explain how a failure in the nation's air traffic control network could have escalated into a shutdown affecting airports nationwide. The FAA lifted the ground stop at approximately 8:15 pm Eastern on Friday, but the disruption continued Saturday.
Duffy offered a new detail on Saturday, saying that a system overload occurred when contractor L3Harris Technologies Inc. was replacing a section of the air traffic control system’s old copper wire with new fiber wire.
“Our contractor didn’t meet the moment or the equipment didn’t meet the moment,” the secretary said, adding that officials were investigating whether the contractor's "incompetence" or a faulty router was to blame.
Duffy emphatically denied that the FAA's recent nationwide rollout of an $875 million artificial intelligence system called Strategic Management of Airspace, Routes, and Trajectories (SMART) had anything to do with Friday's disruption, claiming that the system "is not even plugged in."
The FAA said earlier this week that it "will now use SMART in the airspace of all 22 [air traffic control] centers across the country to reduce delays, reduce fuel consumption, and improve the travel experience for the flying public."
Duffy also used the crisis to renew his demand for $50 billion in congressional funding to overhaul the nation's air traffic control system, arguing that the aging infrastructure would continue to fail without additional investment.
The administration's shifting account has added to calls for an independent investigation. Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said Friday that investigators had found no evidence of a cyberattack, although he acknowledged that the possibility had not been entirely ruled out.
Rep. James Walkinshaw (D-Va.) was among those who challenged the administration's explanation, suggesting that Duffy had concealed the role of the Trump administration's own modernization efforts.
Walkinshaw cited a September report from the Government Accountability Office warning that precisely this could happen.
“In essence, the FAA is buying delivery speed at the risk of cost uncertainty and potential disruptions," GAO said. "The net effect could be that air traffic controllers are forced to continue to rely on severely outdated systems that are prone to failure leading to more system outages, ground stops, flight delays, or worse—safety incidents.”
The fiasco prompted critics, including Democratic New York Gov. Kathy Hochul, to call for Duffy's resignation.
"From chronic air traffic controller shortages to repeated communications breakdowns, serious lapses in our aviation system have become a pattern on Sean Duffy’s watch," Hochul wrote on Facebook.
"Through it all, he’s been more focused on starring in his own reality show than addressing the crisis at his agency," she added, referring to the former MTV "Real World: Boston" and "Road Rules: All Stars" participant's "The Great American Road Trip" travel series, which features his family, including his wife, Fox News personality Rachel Campos Duffy.
The ground stop was the longest nationwide aviation shutdown in 25 years, with more than 3,500 aircraft already airborne when the FAA ordered the grounding. More than 4,100 flights were canceled Saturday alone, according to FlightAware.
FAA Administrator Bryan Bedford said Saturday that normal operations were expected to resume by Sunday evening.

====================================================================================================

Show HN: A tiny macOS app that turns off the MacBook display [free license]
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
15:37:52 UTC
00:37:52 KST

Blackout turns off your MacBook’s built-in display when you are working on an external monitor. System settings will not show the internal display as a desktop anymore. Unplugging monitors or closing the app will return to normal state again.
Use the switches above to see how your displays appear in macOS.
This works perfectly on Apple Silicon Macs (M-series), but on Intel Macs you'll still see the internal display listed on this system preferences page (macOS limitation).
Individual display switches
Switch individual displays on or off from one window.
Keep the lid open
Use your MacBook’s keyboard and trackpad with its screen dark.
Automatic reset to default
If something goes wrong, the app will automatically reset the screens to default (all screens are on)
Minimal footprint
Reduce the app to a tiny top menu bar icon, without an icon in the dock
Detects monitor disconnects
If monitor cable is unplugged, the app will detect this and act accordingly
TouchID keeps working
Unlike other tools, login or authentication via TouchID will work even with the display turned off.
No, as Apple doesn't allow software that uses the Apple hardware API in the App Store, probably to protect normies from breaking their systems. This app is officially signed by Apple with my valid Apple Developer account, so it's safe to use.
What happens when I close the app, restart the Mac, the app crashes, or I unplug the monitor cable?
In all these cases the internal display will be turned on with 80% brightness instantly. Also when macOS updates or other such events happen.
Is this a permanent change?
No, everything is just software-based, so whatever happens, your display will be back online after a macOS restart anyways.
Compatibility
The community is testing many devices, and this table will get updates.
All devices should work. Intel machines and base M3 MacBook / M3 MacBook Air have a hardware bug that MIGHT prevent turning off the internal display.

Rust Leaves Top 10 in TIOBE Index, Making Room for Go
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
15:34:00 UTC
00:34:00 KST

Rust Leaves Top 10 in TIOBE Index, Making Room for Go (i-programmer.info) 15
			
		 	
				"In November's edition of the TIOBE Index, Rust has dropped out of Top 10," writes I-Programmer, noting that Rust ceded the #10 position to Go. "At the same time, another Google-originated language, Dart, has entered the top 20 ousting Julia after only one month."
TIOBE explains Dart's popularity is mainly being driven by Flutter, Google's framework for building cross-platform mobile applications.
 In fact, Flutter has become one of the major technologies for cross-platform mobile app development. The Dart language itself is not particularly unique, although it incorporates many modern programming language features. Its success is largely driven by the strength of the Flutter ecosystem and the convenience of developing applications for multiple platforms using a single codebase...  
[In November's ranking] Visual Basic has also lost two places, making room for R and SQL in the Top 10. Further down the ranking, Assembly has re-entered the Top 20. 
So TIOBE ranks the top 10 languages in November as Python, C, Java, C++, C#, JavaScript, R, SQL, Visual Basic, and Go. But  I-Programmer points out that "At the top of the table, Java is back in the top 3, after over three years in 4th position," pushing C++ down to #4.   Are some changes more significant than others?
If you think of the ratings as shares of a pie divided into 100 pieces, there is one huge slice —  Python, accounting for a over a fifth of the pie, then comes C with a slice slightly less than half of Python's. Java and C++ then have smaller slices... and C#'s share is around half of their average size. Over half the pie has now been shared between just five languages...
— Next 5 (#6-10): 10.17% (JavaScript, R, SQL, Visual Basic, Go)
— Next 10 (#11-20): 11.12% (Rust, PHP, Fortran, Scratch, Perl, MATLAB, Assembly, Swift, Delphi, Dart)
— Next 30 (#21-50): 12.81%
— Final 50 (#51-100): 12.93%
[So in this month's chart...] Java has overtaken C++, but as you can see from this month's Top 10 chart they are in lock step and the difference between them is insignificant.   More obvious is that Python is way ahead of all the competition and that C has an edge over Java and C++.  For more discussion of this see TIOBE — Two To Rule Them All in which Mike James argued that this:
confirms the standard model of programming -  code is written in Python and C and everything else is just noise. 
Back in July we reported Rust Enters TIOBE Top 10. After four months in #10 Rust is now back at #11, which is still three places above its position, 14, a year ago. Given the way these rankings operate it only takes a very small month-on-month change for a language to lose its position and even, as has happened here it can gain in terms of its percentage ranking while still moving down. So Rust with 1.63% in November is now in #11 when it had been in #10 with 1.44%.

====================================================================================================

Emacs Is My Browser
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
15:21:21 UTC
00:21:21 KST

In my ever-increasing desire to use emacs as my sole computing environment, I have started to take browsing the web inside it far more seriously. Where I previously had thought EWW to be a niceity but far from capable, after using it for a few days it seems to be useable for about 85-90% of use cases - even on the javascript riddled hellhole that is the internet.
Why?#
I have seen in my own use of the modern browser (chromium/firefox) that it is far too easy to get distracted - too easy to get off track and fall down rabbit holes that take over my day. There are infinite suggestions as to how I should spend my time, uncountable shiny objects that take my eye off the prize that is Creativity and Depth. This has been momentously negated with EWW (or any terminal browser, lynx or browsh for the non-emacs users).
In addition to this, my belief is that we should make strides toward leaving behind Layer 8 of the internet - the limiting frontends of social platforms and locked away corners of the net that limit actual discourse (Discord, I’m looking at you). We have given up far too much to big tech platforms, and gotten nothing of value in return, to the point that many now think the internet is dying.
The internet is just a delivery mechanism, and for people that see it for what it really is, the internet has never been more alive. I truly recommend using the internet as if it was 1999.
Even on my phone, I’ve stopped using javascript frontends and embraced using eww in emacs (in termux), only falling back to fennec for about 5-10% of usecases:
I find this way of using the internet far higher in signal than any other method, allowing me to look up information, read documentation, and produce more.
How I am doing this#
For the uninitiated, emacs ships with EWW (Emacs Web Wowser) permitting you to browse the web with image and gif (when did gifs die, I almost never see them these days!) support directly inside Emacs.
I have some sane defaults that permit ease of use such as using B for back, U for yanking url at point, and a few built in functions.
Hitting R will invoke eww readable which is similar to reader mode in firefox, removing headers and footers and focusing in on the text on page.  Useful.
You can send the page to your default browser with &. If a page doesn’t render nicely, this is a good fallback.
D will download images locally, b goes back a page, a will save bookmarks. All of this makes browsing in eww truly enjoyable.
I have also set .pdf to open in emacs, .mp4 and youtube/video hosting links to open in mpv, and gopher/gemini links to open in elpher. Elpher is the EWW for the gopher and gemini protocols, the smolweb that is all signal and no noise.
EWW uses Shr to render html, so you will see some callouts to that in my configuration below:
My browser.el setup:#
Here’s how I have configured EWW to work in my Emacs configuration:
My default search engine is my own Searx instance, a privacy respecting frontend that amalgamates all other search engines.
Limitations#
You will not be watching youtube videos or reading Tweets (x.com is adversarial to non-JS supported browsers). You will not be using social media, nor will you be logging into any platforms. You will not be doing your online banking, filling in government forms, or viewing client portals.
The usecase is quick web searches, documentation, and reading blogs generally.  Once more, hitting & in any web page will bring up your default browser to continue your session in chromium or firefox when you do run into pages that don’t work well in EWW.
Conclusion#
I have reverted all my url functions to default to browsing in EWW, so any web interaction must first go through EWW. This has encouraged me to deeply consider what I am doing online first and foremost, and then only falling back to a modern browser when needed. I have been pleasantly surprised with how much I am able to do inside emacs, and continue to move toward using it as my computing environment in perpetuity.
As always, God bless, and until next time.
If you enjoyed this post, consider Supporting my work, Checking out my book, Working with me, or sending me an Email to tell me what you think.

A Human Reads the Anthropic IPO Filing
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
15:18:06 UTC
00:18:06 KST

Supported by
A Human Reads the Anthropic I.P.O. Filing
The prospectus is unparalleled in its willingness to discuss existential risk. But does honesty bring comfort?
My breath taken, I looked up from my desk to gaze at the red-orange blanket of fallen leaves just outside my window. I needed the pause, or maybe the distraction, from what I was reading.
The thick document before me looked like any other standard business proposal: a prospectus for an initial public offering with boilerplate language about dividends, compensation and Class A stock. But the more I read, the more it morphed into experimental literature, equal parts business plan, philosophical treatise and sci-fi thriller.
This will be an I.P.O. unlike any other, its language both matter-of-fact and unnerving, its ultimate subject our future. Not futures, as in the stock market, but the future, as in what it might look like and whether we have one.
The prospectus comes from Anthropic, the artificial intelligence concern immersed in the technologies rapidly transforming the human experience. It is poised to offer shares to the public for the first time, and this document, recently filed with the Securities and Exchange Commission, lays out its blueprint for potential investors to decide whether to bet on the future of civilization.
I cannot give you odds on this existential gamble. My prior awareness of Anthropic extended little beyond clicking on a button with hundreds of thousands of other writers to join a massive class-action copyright lawsuit. As someone who knows next to nothing about investments, I read the Anthropic prospectus solely out of my vested interest as a human being.
In seeking the considerable capital needed for chips, data centers and electricity, the company — with an estimated valuation well north of $1 trillion — begins its pitch by dangling the possibility that artificial intelligence could “solve problems that have resisted generations of human effort.”

All AI-generated code should be Open Source
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
14:46:58 UTC
23:46:58 KST

All AI-generated code should be Open Source
This may be a hot take, but I strongly believe that all code generated by AI should be Open Source. And here's why.
First of all, AI models were trained on a massive corpus of Open Source code written since RMS announced the GNU Project and the Free Software Movement. That code was simply scraped from the internet and used without permission or proper attribution. The models were also trained on Stack Overflow and Stack Exchange sister projects, which are released under Creative Commons licenses that legally require attribution. Turning this collective knowledge into proprietary, closed-source code feels like license laundering.
The second reason is legal: purely AI-generated code is not written by a human, meaning it should not be copyrightable. The foundation for this principle in the US goes back to the famous Monkey Selfie dispute, which established that non-humans cannot hold copyrights. A precedent later cited by the U.S. Copyright Office regarding AI. Following the same logic, Wikimedia Commons requires all purely AI-generated images to be tagged as Public Domain.
However, copyright laws differ across the globe. For instance, countries like the UK grant limited copyright protection to computer-generated works. To make things fair across all jurisdictions, and to honor the open ecosystem that made AI possible, developers and companies should explicitly release AI-generated code under Open Source licenses or dedicate it to the public domain using the Creative Commons Zero (CC0) license.
Of course, code is not always 100% manual or 100% automated. However, developers more and more rely on AI without even reviewing the generated output, let alone modifying it to fit their specific needs. Code like this, where humans put little effort, except writing prompts, should definitely be Open Source.
If you find this article interesting you may want to follow me on Twitter: @jcubic and on LinkedIn.

The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
14:31:52 UTC
23:31:52 KST

The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
    
draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
        This document is an Internet-Draft (I-D).
        Anyone may submit an I-D to the IETF.
        This I-D is not endorsed by the IETF and has no formal standing in the
        IETF standards process.
    
                    
                        draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
                    
                
Independent Submission                                         R. Bánffy
Internet-Draft                                          15 November 2026
Intended status: Informational                                          
Expires: 19 May 2027
  The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
                 draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
Abstract
   This document defines the Hypertext Transfer Protocol status code 483
   (I'm Not a Freezer).  A server returns this status code when it
   understands a request that requires freezing or refrigeration
   capabilities but is not capable of providing those capabilities.
   This document also clarifies the distinction between a freezer, a
   refrigerator, a server operating at a low temperature, a frozen
   software process, and an online service advertising "cold storage".
Status of This Memo
   This Internet-Draft is submitted in full conformance with the
   provisions of BCP 78 and BCP 79.
   Internet-Drafts are working documents of the Internet Engineering
   Task Force (IETF).  Note that other groups may also distribute
   working documents as Internet-Drafts.  The list of current Internet-
   Drafts is at https://datatracker.ietf.org/drafts/current/.
   Internet-Drafts are draft documents valid for a maximum of six months
   and may be updated, replaced, or obsoleted by other documents at any
   time.  It is inappropriate to use Internet-Drafts as reference
   material or to cite them other than as "work in progress."
   This Internet-Draft will expire on 19 May 2027.
Copyright Notice
   Copyright (c) 2026 IETF Trust and the persons identified as the
   document authors.  All rights reserved.
   This document is subject to BCP 78 and the IETF Trust's Legal
   Provisions Relating to IETF Documents (https://trustee.ietf.org/
   license-info) in effect on the date of publication of this document.
   Please review these documents carefully, as they describe your rights
   and restrictions with respect to this document.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 1]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
Table of Contents
   1.  Introduction  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   2
   2.  Conventions and Definitions . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   2
   3.  The 483 (I'm Not a Freezer) Status Code . . . . . . . . . . .   3
   4.  Client Behavior . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   4
   5.  Applicability . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   4
   6.  Example: Electronic Mail Concerning Frozen Goods  . . . . . .   5
   7.  Relationship to Status Code 418 . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   6
   8.  Distinguishing Refrigerators from Freezers  . . . . . . . . .   7
   9.  Intermediaries and Cold-Chain Proxies . . . . . . . . . . . .   7
   10. Caching Considerations  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   7
   11. Security Considerations . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   8
   12. Privacy Considerations  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   8
   13. IANA Considerations . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   9
   14. Implementation Considerations . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   9
   15. Acknowledgments . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   9
   16. Normative References  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  10
   17. Informative References  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  10
   Author's Address  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  10
1.  Introduction
   HTTP status codes communicate the result of attempting to understand
   and satisfy a request.  Existing status codes cover malformed
   requests, missing resources, unsupported media types, and servers
   that are temporarily unavailable.
   No registered status code clearly indicates that a server understood
   a request but cannot satisfy it because the request requires the
   server to be a freezer.
   This ambiguity can cause clients to repeat requests, select
   inappropriate fallback services, place perishable goods near
   computing equipment, or otherwise confuse information technology
   infrastructure with domestic or industrial refrigeration equipment.
   This document addresses that ambiguity by defining the 483 (I'm Not a
   Freezer) status code.
2.  Conventions and Definitions
   The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT",
   "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and
   "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in BCP
   14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all
   capitals, as shown here.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 2]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   Freezer
      An apparatus capable of maintaining its contents at a temperature
      below the freezing point of water for a sustained period.
      Software containers, frozen processes, cold-storage services, and
      servers located in cold climates are not necessarily freezers.
   Refrigerator
      An apparatus that maintains contents below ambient temperature but
      does not necessarily maintain them below the freezing point of
      water.
   Cold storage
      A term that might refer to refrigerated physical storage,
      infrequently accessed data storage, an offline cryptographic
      wallet, or other facilities that are insufficiently warm.  Use of
      the term does not establish freezer capability.
   Freezer-capable server
      An HTTP server associated with equipment that can perform the
      freezing operation requested by the client.  Merely operating
      inside a freezer does not necessarily make an HTTP server freezer-
      capable.
3.  The 483 (I'm Not a Freezer) Status Code
   This document extends the HTTP status-code semantics defined in
   [HTTP] with the 483 (I'm Not a Freezer) status code.  The status code
   indicates that the server understood the request but cannot satisfy
   it because the requested operation requires freezing or refrigeration
   capabilities that the server does not possess.
   A server MAY return 483 when all of the following conditions apply:
   *  The request can be parsed and understood.
   *  Satisfying the request would require the server to freeze,
      refrigerate, chill, or preserve a physical item by reducing its
      temperature.
   *  The server is not capable of performing the requested temperature-
      control operation.
   A server MUST NOT return 483 merely because:
   *  its operating temperature is below ambient temperature;
   *  it uses liquid, air, immersion, or phase-change cooling;
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 3]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   *  it provides a service described as "cold storage";
   *  one or more software processes have been frozen;
   *  its physical location is cold;
   *  its hostname contains words such as "freezer", "ice", "arctic",
      "frost", or "cold"; or
   *  it is unresponsive in a way informally described as "frozen".
   A 483 response SHOULD contain a representation explaining that the
   selected server lacks the required refrigeration capability.  Where
   appropriate, the representation MAY identify a freezer-capable
   alternative.
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: text/plain
   This server is not a freezer and cannot satisfy the requested operation.
4.  Client Behavior
   A client receiving a 483 response SHOULD NOT automatically repeat the
   same request to the same server unless it has reason to believe that
   the server has subsequently acquired suitable refrigeration
   capabilities.
   A client MAY attempt the request against another origin if that
   origin is known to be freezer-capable.
   A client MUST NOT interpret a 483 response as evidence that the
   server is a refrigerator.  The response communicates a lack of
   freezer capability and makes no claim concerning lesser, adjacent, or
   unrelated cooling capabilities.
   Clients SHOULD avoid automatically redirecting physical goods based
   solely on URI authority components, DNS names, electronic mail
   addresses, service banners, or unverified appliance metadata.
5.  Applicability
   Status code 483 is applicable when a request crosses a boundary
   between digital instructions and physical temperature-control
   operations.
   Examples include:
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 4]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   *  an HTTP-controlled kitchen appliance receiving a request to freeze
      food;
   *  an industrial control gateway receiving a request to preserve a
      sample below a specified temperature;
   *  a logistics service receiving an instruction that requires a cold-
      chain facility that it does not operate; and
   *  a general-purpose HTTP server receiving a request intended for a
      refrigeration appliance.
   Status code 483 is not intended to report CPU thermal throttling,
   data-center cooling failures, insufficient heat dissipation,
   unresponsive software, immutable data, object freezing in programming
   languages, or process suspension.
6.  Example: Electronic Mail Concerning Frozen Goods
   Consider an HTTP service that accepts electronic mail for delivery to
   suitable appliances.  A client might inadvertently attempt to deliver
   a message intended for a freezer:
   POST /mail HTTP/1.1
   Host: mail.example
   Content-Type: message/rfc822
   From: alice@example.com
   To: freezer@example.com
   Date: Tue, 3 Nov 2026 10:00:00 +0000
   Message-ID: <peas-483@example.com>
   Subject: Please freeze these peas
   Dear Freezer,
   Please maintain the attached peas at approximately -18 degrees
   Celsius until further notice.
   Regards,
   Alice
   If the selected service is not a freezer and cannot cause the
   requested freezing operation to occur, it MAY reject the request with
   status code 483:
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 5]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: text/plain
   This server is not a freezer and cannot satisfy the requested operation.
   An implementation MUST NOT infer refrigeration capability solely from
   an electronic mail address containing the strings "freezer",
   "refrigerator", "cold", "ice", or similar terminology.
   The recipient address identifies the intended appliance but does not
   prove that the HTTP service handling the message controls that
   appliance.
   The domain names and electronic mail addresses in this example are
   reserved for documentation as described by [RFC2606] and do not
   identify operational refrigeration infrastructure.
7.  Relationship to Status Code 418
   Status code 418 was introduced by the Hyper Text Coffee Pot Control
   Protocol [RFC2324] and is associated with an apparatus that is a
   teapot and therefore cannot brew coffee.  Status code 483 represents
   a distinct condition.
   A teapot might be neither a freezer nor a general-purpose server.
   Similarly, a freezer might contain an HTTP server without becoming a
   teapot.
   An implementation choosing between 418 and 483 SHOULD consider the
   immediate reason that the request cannot be satisfied:
   *  If the request cannot be satisfied because the responding
      apparatus is a teapot, 418 might be appropriate.
   *  If the request cannot be satisfied because the responding server
      is not a freezer, 483 might be appropriate.
   *  If the apparatus is both a teapot and not a freezer, the
      implementation SHOULD report the condition most directly relevant
      to the requested operation.
   This document does not define response prioritization for combination
   refrigerator-freezer-teapot appliances.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 6]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
8.  Distinguishing Refrigerators from Freezers
   A refrigerator is not necessarily a freezer.  An implementation MUST
   NOT advertise freezer capability merely because it can maintain a
   temperature lower than the surrounding environment.
   If a request specifies a target temperature, the server SHOULD
   evaluate its actual supported temperature range.  A server that can
   maintain 4 degrees Celsius but cannot maintain -18 degrees Celsius
   can return 483 when asked to preserve frozen food at -18 degrees
   Celsius.
   This document does not allocate a separate status code meaning "I'm
   Only a Refrigerator".  Such allocation is left for future work,
   preferably after an appropriate cooling-off period.
9.  Intermediaries and Cold-Chain Proxies
   An intermediary MUST NOT replace a 483 response with a success
   response unless it has arranged for the requested operation to be
   performed by a freezer-capable service.
   An intermediary that forwards a request to a freezer-capable service
   SHOULD preserve sufficient request information to maintain
   traceability through the cold chain.
   A proxy MUST NOT claim that a request has been frozen merely because
   it has been buffered, queued, archived, or stored on media described
   as cold storage.
   Intermediaries SHOULD NOT select a refrigeration service based only
   on the service name.  Capability claims ought to be authenticated
   before perishable or temperature-sensitive goods are redirected.
10.  Caching Considerations
   A 483 response is not heuristically cacheable.
   A 483 response can be cached when explicit freshness information
   permits caching, subject to [HTTP-CACHING].
   Implementations ought to use short freshness lifetimes when
   refrigeration capabilities can change.  A server might acquire
   freezer capability through appliance discovery, configuration,
   repair, attachment of suitable hardware, or relocation.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 7]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   A cache MUST include relevant request-selection information in its
   cache key when freezer capability depends on the requested product,
   target temperature, duration, physical location, or other request
   characteristics.
11.  Security Considerations
   A 483 response can disclose information about the physical
   capabilities of a server, appliance, building, warehouse, or
   logistics network.  Such information might assist an attacker in
   identifying facilities that lack refrigeration capability.
   Servers SHOULD avoid disclosing unnecessary details about appliance
   models, internal temperatures, stored contents, physical locations,
   maintenance schedules, or cold-chain topology.
   A malicious service might falsely claim freezer capability in order
   to receive physical goods, operational instructions, or sensitive
   inventory information.  Clients SHOULD authenticate services before
   relying on claims of refrigeration capability.
   Implementations MUST NOT treat the presence or absence of a 483
   response as proof that a physical item has been frozen, refrigerated,
   preserved, spoiled, thawed, or consumed.
   Request bodies associated with freezing operations might reveal
   dietary preferences, medical requirements, biological sample
   information, commercial inventory, or other sensitive data.
   Implementations SHOULD apply suitable confidentiality and access-
   control protections.
   Operators are advised not to troubleshoot servers by placing them
   inside domestic freezers.  Condensation, restricted airflow, thermal
   shock, and the objections of other freezer occupants can produce
   undesirable results.
12.  Privacy Considerations
   Requests involving refrigeration can expose information about food,
   medicine, scientific samples, commercial shipments, or household
   activity.  Servers SHOULD minimize the information returned in a 483
   response and SHOULD avoid echoing sensitive request content.
   Logs containing 483 responses SHOULD be protected according to the
   sensitivity of associated request metadata and content.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 8]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
13.  IANA Considerations
   This document requests that IANA register the following entry in the
   "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Status Code Registry":
               +=======+===================+===============+
               | Value | Description       | Reference     |
               +=======+===================+===============+
               | 483   | I'm Not a Freezer | This document |
               +-------+-------------------+---------------+
                    Table 1: Requested HTTP Status Code
                                Registration
   If the registration request is not approved, this section can itself
   be considered safely stored for future use, although not necessarily
   below freezing.
14.  Implementation Considerations
   Implementers are encouraged to use the exact reason phrase "I'm Not a
   Freezer" when a reason phrase is supported.  Clients MUST NOT depend
   on the reason phrase because HTTP semantics are identified by the
   numeric status code.
   Protocol versions that do not transmit a reason phrase can use status
   code 483 without modification.
   Implementations MAY include a machine-readable problem detail
   describing the unsupported refrigeration operation, subject to
   [RFC9457].
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: application/problem+json
   Cache-Control: no-store
   {
     "type": "https://example.com/problems/not-a-freezer",
     "title": "I'm Not a Freezer",
     "status": 483,
     "detail": "The requested temperature is outside supported capabilities."
   }
15.  Acknowledgments
   The author thanks the designers of HTTP, refrigeration equipment,
   electronic mail, and combination appliances for creating the
   conditions that made this clarification necessary.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 9]

   The peas used in the example were not harmed during preparation of
   this document, although their final temperature could not be
   independently verified.

The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
14:31:52 UTC
23:31:52 KST

The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
    
draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
        This document is an Internet-Draft (I-D).
        Anyone may submit an I-D to the IETF.
        This I-D is not endorsed by the IETF and has no formal standing in the
        IETF standards process.
    
                    
                        draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
                    
                
Independent Submission                                         R. Bánffy
Internet-Draft                                          15 November 2026
Intended status: Informational                                          
Expires: 19 May 2027
  The Hypertext Transfer Protocol Status Code 483 (I'm Not a Freezer)
                 draft-banffy-httpbis-not-a-freezer-00
Abstract
   This document defines the Hypertext Transfer Protocol status code 483
   (I'm Not a Freezer).  A server returns this status code when it
   understands a request that requires freezing or refrigeration
   capabilities but is not capable of providing those capabilities.
   This document also clarifies the distinction between a freezer, a
   refrigerator, a server operating at a low temperature, a frozen
   software process, and an online service advertising "cold storage".
Status of This Memo
   This Internet-Draft is submitted in full conformance with the
   provisions of BCP 78 and BCP 79.
   Internet-Drafts are working documents of the Internet Engineering
   Task Force (IETF).  Note that other groups may also distribute
   working documents as Internet-Drafts.  The list of current Internet-
   Drafts is at https://datatracker.ietf.org/drafts/current/.
   Internet-Drafts are draft documents valid for a maximum of six months
   and may be updated, replaced, or obsoleted by other documents at any
   time.  It is inappropriate to use Internet-Drafts as reference
   material or to cite them other than as "work in progress."
   This Internet-Draft will expire on 19 May 2027.
Copyright Notice
   Copyright (c) 2026 IETF Trust and the persons identified as the
   document authors.  All rights reserved.
   This document is subject to BCP 78 and the IETF Trust's Legal
   Provisions Relating to IETF Documents (https://trustee.ietf.org/
   license-info) in effect on the date of publication of this document.
   Please review these documents carefully, as they describe your rights
   and restrictions with respect to this document.

   15. Acknowledgments . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .   9

   17. Informative References  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  10
   Author's Address  . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .  10
1.  Introduction
   HTTP status codes communicate the result of attempting to understand
   and satisfy a request.  Existing status codes cover malformed
   requests, missing resources, unsupported media types, and servers
   that are temporarily unavailable.
   No registered status code clearly indicates that a server understood
   a request but cannot satisfy it because the request requires the
   server to be a freezer.
   This ambiguity can cause clients to repeat requests, select
   inappropriate fallback services, place perishable goods near
   computing equipment, or otherwise confuse information technology
   infrastructure with domestic or industrial refrigeration equipment.
   This document addresses that ambiguity by defining the 483 (I'm Not a
   Freezer) status code.
2.  Conventions and Definitions
   The key words "MUST", "MUST NOT", "REQUIRED", "SHALL", "SHALL NOT",
   "SHOULD", "SHOULD NOT", "RECOMMENDED", "NOT RECOMMENDED", "MAY", and
   "OPTIONAL" in this document are to be interpreted as described in BCP
   14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all
   capitals, as shown here.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 2]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   Freezer
      An apparatus capable of maintaining its contents at a temperature
      below the freezing point of water for a sustained period.
      Software containers, frozen processes, cold-storage services, and
      servers located in cold climates are not necessarily freezers.
   Refrigerator
      An apparatus that maintains contents below ambient temperature but
      does not necessarily maintain them below the freezing point of
      water.
   Cold storage
      A term that might refer to refrigerated physical storage,
      infrequently accessed data storage, an offline cryptographic
      wallet, or other facilities that are insufficiently warm.  Use of
      the term does not establish freezer capability.
   Freezer-capable server
      An HTTP server associated with equipment that can perform the
      freezing operation requested by the client.  Merely operating
      inside a freezer does not necessarily make an HTTP server freezer-
      capable.
3.  The 483 (I'm Not a Freezer) Status Code
   This document extends the HTTP status-code semantics defined in
   [HTTP] with the 483 (I'm Not a Freezer) status code.  The status code
   indicates that the server understood the request but cannot satisfy
   it because the requested operation requires freezing or refrigeration
   capabilities that the server does not possess.
   A server MAY return 483 when all of the following conditions apply:
   *  The request can be parsed and understood.
   *  Satisfying the request would require the server to freeze,
      refrigerate, chill, or preserve a physical item by reducing its
      temperature.
   *  The server is not capable of performing the requested temperature-
      control operation.
   A server MUST NOT return 483 merely because:
   *  its operating temperature is below ambient temperature;
   *  it uses liquid, air, immersion, or phase-change cooling;
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 3]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   *  it provides a service described as "cold storage";
   *  one or more software processes have been frozen;
   *  its physical location is cold;
   *  its hostname contains words such as "freezer", "ice", "arctic",
      "frost", or "cold"; or
   *  it is unresponsive in a way informally described as "frozen".
   A 483 response SHOULD contain a representation explaining that the
   selected server lacks the required refrigeration capability.  Where
   appropriate, the representation MAY identify a freezer-capable
   alternative.
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: text/plain
   This server is not a freezer and cannot satisfy the requested operation.
4.  Client Behavior
   A client receiving a 483 response SHOULD NOT automatically repeat the
   same request to the same server unless it has reason to believe that
   the server has subsequently acquired suitable refrigeration
   capabilities.
   A client MAY attempt the request against another origin if that
   origin is known to be freezer-capable.
   A client MUST NOT interpret a 483 response as evidence that the
   server is a refrigerator.  The response communicates a lack of
   freezer capability and makes no claim concerning lesser, adjacent, or
   unrelated cooling capabilities.
   Clients SHOULD avoid automatically redirecting physical goods based
   solely on URI authority components, DNS names, electronic mail
   addresses, service banners, or unverified appliance metadata.
5.  Applicability
   Status code 483 is applicable when a request crosses a boundary
   between digital instructions and physical temperature-control
   operations.
   Examples include:
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 4]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   *  an HTTP-controlled kitchen appliance receiving a request to freeze
      food;
   *  an industrial control gateway receiving a request to preserve a
      sample below a specified temperature;
   *  a logistics service receiving an instruction that requires a cold-
      chain facility that it does not operate; and
   *  a general-purpose HTTP server receiving a request intended for a
      refrigeration appliance.
   Status code 483 is not intended to report CPU thermal throttling,
   data-center cooling failures, insufficient heat dissipation,
   unresponsive software, immutable data, object freezing in programming
   languages, or process suspension.
6.  Example: Electronic Mail Concerning Frozen Goods
   Consider an HTTP service that accepts electronic mail for delivery to
   suitable appliances.  A client might inadvertently attempt to deliver
   a message intended for a freezer:
   POST /mail HTTP/1.1
   Host: mail.example
   Content-Type: message/rfc822
   From: alice@example.com
   To: freezer@example.com
   Date: Tue, 3 Nov 2026 10:00:00 +0000
   Message-ID: <peas-483@example.com>
   Subject: Please freeze these peas
   Dear Freezer,
   Please maintain the attached peas at approximately -18 degrees
   Celsius until further notice.
   Regards,
   Alice
   If the selected service is not a freezer and cannot cause the
   requested freezing operation to occur, it MAY reject the request with
   status code 483:
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 5]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: text/plain
   This server is not a freezer and cannot satisfy the requested operation.
   An implementation MUST NOT infer refrigeration capability solely from
   an electronic mail address containing the strings "freezer",
   "refrigerator", "cold", "ice", or similar terminology.
   The recipient address identifies the intended appliance but does not
   prove that the HTTP service handling the message controls that
   appliance.
   The domain names and electronic mail addresses in this example are
   reserved for documentation as described by [RFC2606] and do not
   identify operational refrigeration infrastructure.
7.  Relationship to Status Code 418
   Status code 418 was introduced by the Hyper Text Coffee Pot Control
   Protocol [RFC2324] and is associated with an apparatus that is a
   teapot and therefore cannot brew coffee.  Status code 483 represents
   a distinct condition.
   A teapot might be neither a freezer nor a general-purpose server.
   Similarly, a freezer might contain an HTTP server without becoming a
   teapot.
   An implementation choosing between 418 and 483 SHOULD consider the
   immediate reason that the request cannot be satisfied:
   *  If the request cannot be satisfied because the responding
      apparatus is a teapot, 418 might be appropriate.
   *  If the request cannot be satisfied because the responding server
      is not a freezer, 483 might be appropriate.
   *  If the apparatus is both a teapot and not a freezer, the
      implementation SHOULD report the condition most directly relevant
      to the requested operation.
   This document does not define response prioritization for combination
   refrigerator-freezer-teapot appliances.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 6]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
8.  Distinguishing Refrigerators from Freezers
   A refrigerator is not necessarily a freezer.  An implementation MUST
   NOT advertise freezer capability merely because it can maintain a
   temperature lower than the surrounding environment.
   If a request specifies a target temperature, the server SHOULD
   evaluate its actual supported temperature range.  A server that can
   maintain 4 degrees Celsius but cannot maintain -18 degrees Celsius
   can return 483 when asked to preserve frozen food at -18 degrees
   Celsius.
   This document does not allocate a separate status code meaning "I'm
   Only a Refrigerator".  Such allocation is left for future work,
   preferably after an appropriate cooling-off period.
9.  Intermediaries and Cold-Chain Proxies
   An intermediary MUST NOT replace a 483 response with a success
   response unless it has arranged for the requested operation to be
   performed by a freezer-capable service.
   An intermediary that forwards a request to a freezer-capable service
   SHOULD preserve sufficient request information to maintain
   traceability through the cold chain.
   A proxy MUST NOT claim that a request has been frozen merely because
   it has been buffered, queued, archived, or stored on media described
   as cold storage.
   Intermediaries SHOULD NOT select a refrigeration service based only
   on the service name.  Capability claims ought to be authenticated
   before perishable or temperature-sensitive goods are redirected.
10.  Caching Considerations
   A 483 response is not heuristically cacheable.
   A 483 response can be cached when explicit freshness information
   permits caching, subject to [HTTP-CACHING].
   Implementations ought to use short freshness lifetimes when
   refrigeration capabilities can change.  A server might acquire
   freezer capability through appliance discovery, configuration,
   repair, attachment of suitable hardware, or relocation.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 7]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   A cache MUST include relevant request-selection information in its
   cache key when freezer capability depends on the requested product,
   target temperature, duration, physical location, or other request
   characteristics.
11.  Security Considerations
   A 483 response can disclose information about the physical
   capabilities of a server, appliance, building, warehouse, or
   logistics network.  Such information might assist an attacker in
   identifying facilities that lack refrigeration capability.
   Servers SHOULD avoid disclosing unnecessary details about appliance
   models, internal temperatures, stored contents, physical locations,
   maintenance schedules, or cold-chain topology.
   A malicious service might falsely claim freezer capability in order
   to receive physical goods, operational instructions, or sensitive
   inventory information.  Clients SHOULD authenticate services before
   relying on claims of refrigeration capability.
   Implementations MUST NOT treat the presence or absence of a 483
   response as proof that a physical item has been frozen, refrigerated,
   preserved, spoiled, thawed, or consumed.
   Request bodies associated with freezing operations might reveal
   dietary preferences, medical requirements, biological sample
   information, commercial inventory, or other sensitive data.
   Implementations SHOULD apply suitable confidentiality and access-
   control protections.
   Operators are advised not to troubleshoot servers by placing them
   inside domestic freezers.  Condensation, restricted airflow, thermal
   shock, and the objections of other freezer occupants can produce
   undesirable results.
12.  Privacy Considerations
   Requests involving refrigeration can expose information about food,
   medicine, scientific samples, commercial shipments, or household
   activity.  Servers SHOULD minimize the information returned in a 483
   response and SHOULD avoid echoing sensitive request content.
   Logs containing 483 responses SHOULD be protected according to the
   sensitivity of associated request metadata and content.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 8]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
13.  IANA Considerations
   This document requests that IANA register the following entry in the
   "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Status Code Registry":
               +=======+===================+===============+
               | Value | Description       | Reference     |
               +=======+===================+===============+
               | 483   | I'm Not a Freezer | This document |
               +-------+-------------------+---------------+
                    Table 1: Requested HTTP Status Code
                                Registration
   If the registration request is not approved, this section can itself
   be considered safely stored for future use, although not necessarily
   below freezing.
14.  Implementation Considerations
   Implementers are encouraged to use the exact reason phrase "I'm Not a
   Freezer" when a reason phrase is supported.  Clients MUST NOT depend
   on the reason phrase because HTTP semantics are identified by the
   numeric status code.
   Protocol versions that do not transmit a reason phrase can use status
   code 483 without modification.
   Implementations MAY include a machine-readable problem detail
   describing the unsupported refrigeration operation, subject to
   [RFC9457].
   HTTP/1.1 483 I'm Not a Freezer
   Content-Type: application/problem+json
   Cache-Control: no-store
   {
     "type": "https://example.com/problems/not-a-freezer",
     "title": "I'm Not a Freezer",
     "status": 483,
     "detail": "The requested temperature is outside supported capabilities."
   }
15.  Acknowledgments
   The author thanks the designers of HTTP, refrigeration equipment,
   electronic mail, and combination appliances for creating the
   conditions that made this clarification necessary.
Banffy                     Expires 19 May 2027                  [Page 9]
Internet-Draft              I'm Not a Freezer              November 2026
   The peas used in the example were not harmed during preparation of
   this document, although their final temperature could not be
   independently verified.

Everyone Always Wants Seconds of These Cheesy "Potato Puffs"
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
14:15:00 UTC
23:15:00 KST

Cheesy “Potato Puffs” Are the Only Way I Eat Leftover Mashed Potatoes
Every Thanksgiving, if you’re lucky, you end up with just the right amount of leftovers for the perfectly stacked turkey sandwich or the loaded leftover lunch plate. But sometimes things get out of wack and you end up with way too much gravy or an extra casserole dish of stuffing. For me, the extra is often mashed potatoes, given my family’s propensity to go big on potatoes (my sister made over 10 pounds of potatoes this year!). But I don’t mind in the least, because these hot, cheesy little puffs are the perfect way to use up those mashed potatoes.
The Best Recipe to Use Up Mashed Potatoes
I first started making these a few years ago when left with an extra Dutch oven full of mashed potatoes and got tired of making mashed potato pancakes. I stirred up the potatoes with some eggs and a heaping helping of cheese, along with some bacon, and dolloped them into a mini muffin pan. (I especially love this pan, which has removable bottoms and is also so good for mini cheesecakes.)
Bake up and bam! Cheesy, tender little morsels of potatoes, with crisp edges and topped with sour cream or gravy (if you like!). They make a fantastic holiday weekend breakfast or appetizer. They are also a great way to use up little bits of leftovers from your holiday cooking: herbs, chopped turkey or ham, extra bacon.
It’s one of the few recipes where I actually like the leftover version better than the original. I eat them straight out of the pan;  just poke a spoon in and get at them. 
Get the recipe: Cheesy Mashed Potato Puffs
Do you have favorite ways of using up Thanksgiving leftovers?

In the 1990s, Sony was absolutely convinced that we were on the precipice of a robot takeover. Pretty soon, the company decided, we’d all be living among countless humanoid robots. Some would be our coworkers; others would be our friends; and at least one would be our pet. And “pet” was exactly what Sony was going for when it created the Aibo. It was supposed to be part of the family.
The Sony Aibo was a robot revolution dressed like a dog
On Version History: How a robot dog became a family member, but not exactly a huge hit.
On this episode of Version History, we go back in time and tell the story of the Aibo. David Pierce is joined by The Verge’s Victoria Song and Jennifer Pattison Tuohy, and together they discuss the origins of this remarkable robot dog, and its impressive success and longevity. They also try to figure out why Aibo had such an effect on its owners, what Sony got right (and wrong) about robots, and whether the idea of a robo-pet will ever be a global phenomenon.
This is the first episode of the sixth season of Version History. We’re back! This season is all about pets: gadgets that are pets, gadgets for your pets, and one gadget called pet. Show HN: db-branch – database branches in seconds for AI agents and CI, via ZFS
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
13:50:51 UTC
22:50:51 KST

Instant database branches for MySQL and PostgreSQL, on your own servers.
Test a migration, let an AI agent loose, or give every pull request its own copy of the database. db-branch creates a copy-on-write branch of a large database in seconds, with nothing copied. Break it, reset it, throw it away. Built on ZFS clones, self-hosted, Apache-2.0.
Status: Beta. db-branch is usable today, but APIs and command-line details may still change before 1.0. It is an independent open-source project, built from scratch.
- In your browser, 0 minutes. A live console demo runs entirely in the page (nothing real is created): https://demo.db-branch.com/
- On a Mac or Windows machine, about 2 minutes. db-branch needs Linux and ZFS, so run it in an Ubuntu VM with Multipass. One command makes the VM, installs db-branch, initialises it on a file-backed ZFS pool and loads a small sample base, shop (MySQL 8.4):
multipass launch 24.04 --name db-branch-eval --memory 4G --disk 30G --timeout 900 \ --cloud-init https://raw.githubusercontent.com/db-branch/db-branch/main/scripts/eval-cloud-init.yaml multipass exec db-branch-eval -- cloud-init status --wait # wait for the setup (about two minutes) multipass exec db-branch-eval -- cat /etc/motd # console URL and sign-in (admin / admin), or what failed multipass shell db-branch-eval # then: sudo db-branch branch create try --base shop --ttl 1h --record On Windows (PowerShell or cmd), put the multipass launch command on one line and drop the \:
multipass launch 24.04 --name db-branch-eval --memory 4G --disk 30G --timeout 900 --cloud-init https://raw.githubusercontent.com/db-branch/db-branch/main/scripts/eval-cloud-init.yaml 
  - The history module is turned on, and the paid modules run for 30 days without a key. --record records a branch so it can be restored to any point.
  - The pool is a file, so it is slower and not meant for real data. The first admin password is admin; the console asks for a new one at the first sign-in.
  - On Apple Silicon (and other arm64 machines) the setup downloads about 800 MB for MySQL, against 80 MB on Intel, because Oracle ships no minimal build for arm64. --timeout 900 keeps launch from giving up on a slow connection.
  - If the setup fails, /etc/motd says so, and sudo tail -n 30 /var/log/cloud-init-output.log in the VM shows why.
  - Delete the VM with multipass delete --purge db-branch-eval.
- On your own Ubuntu server. See Install.
- AI agent sandboxes. The built-in MCP server lets an LLM agent create a branch, run a risky query or migration on it, and delete the branch, so production data is never touched.
- A database per pull request. Each CI run or PR gets its own copy of a large database, ready in seconds, and removed by --ttl when it is no longer needed.
- Version-upgrade checks. Run a major-version upgrade (for example MySQL 8.0 to 8.4) on one branch first, with branch upgrade --check-only to see what it would run into, before touching the base.
- Branches in seconds. On our own 3-node test cluster a PostgreSQL branch is ready in about 0.5 s and a MySQL branch in about 3 s, with 0 bytes copied at creation (copy-on-write).
- Reset and expire. branch reset returns a branch to its creation state; --ttl deletes it automatically.
- Checkpoints. branch checkpoint saves a running branch's state instantly; branch restore puts it back. Restoring replaces the current state, and every checkpoint you already have stays restorable.
- Per-branch upgrades. branch upgrade moves one branch to a newer MySQL or PostgreSQL version while the base and the other branches stay put. A checkpoint is saved first, and a failed upgrade rolls back by itself.
- MySQL and PostgreSQL, several versions at once. Engine runtimes are installed by version and can live side by side.
- One interface everywhere. CLI, REST API, web console and an MCP server (so LLM agents can create and query their own branches) all use the same API.
- Single node or cluster. A Raft cluster of VMs, with optional ProxySQL routing so applications keep one address.
Requirements: Ubuntu 24.04 LTS or 26.04 LTS, and a ZFS pool (the zfsutils-linux package is installed as a dependency).
curl -fsSL https://apt.db-branch.com/db-branch.gpg | sudo tee /usr/share/keyrings/db-branch.gpg >/dev/null
echo "deb [signed-by=/usr/share/keyrings/db-branch.gpg] https://apt.db-branch.com stable main" | sudo tee /etc/apt/sources.list.d/db-branch.list
sudo apt update && sudo apt install db-branch
sudo db-branch init --pool <zpool>
The repository signing key fingerprint is 3F404C4E5D49FA653F8385692C6E89E341D4D7A5. db-branch init prints the initial admin password once.
db-branch runtime install postgres@18.6                                     # a database engine version
db-branch base create prod --runtime postgres@18.6 --dump prod.sql.gz       # a base: your data, loaded once
db-branch branch create feature-x --base prod --ttl 72h                     # a branch: instant, copy-on-write
db-branch branch dsn feature-x                                              # connection URL (includes the password)
db-branch branch checkpoint feature-x --name before-migration               # save the state (instant)
db-branch branch restore feature-x before-migration                         # go back to the saved state
db-branch branch reset feature-x                                            # discard every change
db-branch branch delete feature-x                                           # remove it
MySQL works the same way, for example mysql@8.4.13. List installable versions with db-branch runtime available. To try a version upgrade on one MySQL branch:
db-branch branch upgrade feature-x --runtime mysql@8.4.13 --check-only      # what would the upgrade run into?
db-branch branch upgrade feature-x --runtime mysql@8.4.13                   # move this branch only; a checkpoint is saved first
A base is a ZFS dataset holding a loaded database. A branch is a ZFS clone of a base snapshot, so creating one copies nothing, and each branch runs its own database server on its own port. Optionally, ProxySQL routes client connections to the right branch by user name (or by a /*db_branch='name'*/ query comment). Several nodes form a Raft cluster that shares bases, branches, settings and users.
- Core (this repository), Apache-2.0: branches, bases, runtimes, clustering, CLI, API, web console, MCP, monitoring, notifications, anonymous (no sign-in) branches with limits, scheduled base refresh, backup of cluster state.
- Free modules in the same apt repository: proxysql, greenmask, greenmask-mysql, anon (routing and data masking).
- Paid modules (auth/SSO, audit, history, diff, FSx storage) are separate, closed-source packages, self-hosted and enabled with a license key (monthly or yearly). A governed masking workflow is planned as an opt-in beta and is not part of any plan yet. See docs/modules.md for the extension points they use.
There is no hosted or cloud version of db-branch.
- docs/README.md: index of the design and reference documents (written in Japanese)
- English user documentation: https://db-branch.com/docs/
- Website: https://db-branch.com (English), https://db-branch.com/ja/ (Japanese)
- api/openapi.yaml: machine-readable REST API definition
- CHANGELOG.md
- Contributions are welcome: see CONTRIBUTING.md. Commits need a DCO sign-off (git commit -s).
- Security issues: see SECURITY.md. Please use private vulnerability reporting, not public issues.
- Support: see SUPPORT.md. No support is promised; Issues are read when possible.
- Please follow the Code of Conduct.
Apache-2.0. See LICENSE and NOTICE.
db-branch は、ZFS クローンで MySQL / PostgreSQL のデータベースブランチを発行・リセット・破棄するツールです。現在は Beta で、1.0 までに API が変わる可能性があります。コアは Apache-2.0 で、有償モジュール（認証/SSO・監査・履歴・差分・FSx ストレージ）は別パッケージ（クローズドソース、セルフホスト、ライセンスキー）です。
Mac / Windows では、Multipass のコマンド1つで試せます: https://db-branch.com/ja/docs/getting-started/#multipass-ですぐ試す。日本語のサイトは https://db-branch.com/ja/、設計ドキュメントは docs/ にあります（日本語）。

Things That Turbo Pascal Is Smaller Than (2011)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
13:44:16 UTC
22:44:16 KST

It's not about technology for its own sake. It's about being able to implement your ideas.
Turbo Pascal 3 for MS-DOS was released in September 1986. Being version 3, there were lesser releases prior to it and flashier ones after, but 3 was a solid representation of the Turbo Pascal experience: a full Pascal compiler, including extensions that it made it practical for commercial use, tightly integrated with an editor. And the whole thing was lightning fast, orders of magnitude faster at building projects than Microsoft's compilers.
The entire Turbo Pascal 3.02 executable--the compiler and IDE--was 39,731 bytes. How does that stack up in 2011 terms? Here are some things that Turbo Pascal is smaller than, as of October 30, 2011:
The minified version of jquery 1.6 (90,518 bytes).
The yahoo.com home page (219,583 bytes).
The image of the white iPhone 4S at apple.com (190,157 bytes).
zlib.h in the Mac OS X Lion SDK (80,504 bytes).
The touch command under OS X Lion (44,016 bytes).
Various vim quick reference cards as PDFs. (This one is 47,508 bytes.)
The compiled code for the Erlang R14B02 parser (erl_parse.beam, 286,324 bytes).
The Wikipedia page for C++ (214,251 bytes).
(If you liked this, you might like A Personal History of Compilation Speed.)
permalink October 30, 2011
I'm James Hague, a recovering programmer who has been designing video games since the 1980s. Programming Without Being Obsessed With Programming and Organizational Skills Beat Algorithmic Wizardry are good starting points. For the older stuff, try the 2012 Retrospective.
Where are the comments?

1-Ingredient Upgrades for the Most Flavorful Beef Stew Ever
We independently select these products—if you buy from one of our links, we may earn a commission. All prices were accurate at the time of publishing.
It is officially beef stew season. Right now is the best time of year to braise and slow-cook, and let the warmth from your oven and stovetop fill your home. Here at The Kitchn, we love a good beef stew (like mechado, a Filipino beef stew flavored with soy sauce; Belgian beef stew that uses a beer base; and French onion beef stew that’s extra allium-y and cheesy).
The foundations of beef stew are pretty consistent — beef, slow-cooked with aromatics and vegetables until it’s fork-tender and seasoned to perfection — but there are seemingly endless ways to add extra umami and flavor, and we’ve tried a lot of them over the years. Here are a few of our favorite ways to spice up and deepen your beef stew with simple, one-ingredient upgrades.
