Using surveillance camera footage, police identified the make, model, and color of the robber’s car, but they did not know its license plate number. After running an open-ended search of two ALPR databases, Flock and ELSAG, officers learned a car belonging to the defendant had been tracked near sites of the robberies, eventually leading to his arrest. Faced with a motion to suppress the ALPR evidence, the district court held that Mr. Sturdivant had no reasonable expectation of privacy in his license plate number because it could be observed by members of the public, and that therefore he had no Fourth Amendment interest in not having his car tracked in public places.
But ALPRs are simply not comparable to observing an individual license plate on a passing car. As we’ve explained in many amicus briefs and in our lawsuits directly challenging ALPR use in San Jose, California and Wisconsin, ALPRs give law enforcement “a virtual time machine to view suspects’, or anyone else’s, past movements (and much more) with just the click of a button.” Just last month, a federal district court agreed and held that ALPRs are a “form of indiscriminate mass surveillance” that can violate the Fourth Amendment.
We hope the Sixth Circuit will reverse the lower court in this case and hold that ALPR searches require a warrant.

====================================================================================================

Court denies motion to dismiss DRAM price fixing case
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
01:18:36 UTC
10:18:36 KST

Key Points
- Judge Noël Wise denied in full Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron's joint motion to dismiss a proposed class action alleging they conspired to cut supply of conventional DRAM, such as DDR5, and shift capacity to HBM.
- The court held the case was not a rerun of DRAM II, the Ninth Circuit's 2022 affirmance of dismissal of an earlier DRAM conspiracy suit against the same companies.
- The judge found that long-term HBM lock-ups, refusals of long-term conventional DRAM contracts, Micron's Crucial closure, fab delays, the failure to expand despite record prices, near-identical customer vetting and the companies' prior cartel history supported a plausible inference of conspiracy.
- The court gave no weight to three of the plaintiffs' arguments: investor statements about "supply discipline," which it found too similar to statements the Ninth Circuit's DRAM II decision had already held insufficient; executives' meetings at industry conferences, since DRAM II all but forecloses relying on them unless plaintiffs identify what was actually exchanged or agreed there; and the three-company market structure, which it called neutral.
- Answers are due Dec. 7, 2026, and the initial case management conference was reset for Jan. 12, 2027.
A federal judge on Nov. 14 refused to dismiss a proposed class action accusing Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron of conspiring to restrict the supply of conventional dynamic random-access memory, or DRAM, the standardized memory chips used in computers, phones and servers.
U.S. District Judge Noël Wise of the Northern District of California denied the companies' joint motion to dismiss in full, finding the complaint plausibly alleged an agreement in violation of Section 1 of the Sherman Act. The allegations, accepted as true at this stage, have not been proven.
The companies argued the case was a rerun of DRAM II, in which the Ninth Circuit in 2022 affirmed dismissal of claims against the same three companies over a 2016–2018 supply restriction. "The Court finds that it is not," Judge Wise wrote.
The suit was filed June 25, 2026, by 17 consumers and small businesses from five states. It concerns conventional DRAM, which the complaint describes as "standardized memory built to industry specifications and sold for ordinary purposes," including DDR5 generation modules built for computers.
According to the complaint, the three companies controlled more than 91% of global DRAM revenue as of the third quarter of 2025. They allegedly cut production starting in late 2022 and, beginning in 2023, shifted capacity to high bandwidth memory, or HBM, a specialized DRAM product for artificial intelligence accelerators that "requires approximately three to four times the silicon wafer area per gigabyte compared to conventional DDR5 DRAM."
By the third quarter of 2025, conventional DRAM prices had risen 171.8% in one year, according to the complaint, which alleges a 697% increase over 18 months. Yet "not one of the three Defendants expanded or announced plans to meaningfully expand commodity DRAM production capacity," the complaint alleges.
Without direct evidence of an agreement, the plaintiffs needed plus factors, additional facts suggesting a preceding agreement rather than conscious parallelism, in which competitors independently make similar moves.
Judge Wise rejected the companies' argument that timing gaps undercut parallel action. SK Hynix and Micron announced cuts three weeks apart in late 2022, she noted, and Samsung's lag of about four and a half months, until April 7, 2023, was "not such an unreasonably long delay that it defeats an allegation of parallel conduct on a motion to dismiss."
She found the complaint plausibly alleged that the three companies acted in parallel when they cut supply, shifted production from conventional DRAM such as DDR5 to HBM, and wound down DDR4.
Under Ninth Circuit precedent, action against self-interest must be "so perilous in the absence of advance agreement that no reasonable firm would make the challenged move without such an agreement." Judge Wise wrote that a pivot to HBM by itself "might not be sufficient," but that "the related conduct tips this factor over the edge to weigh in Plaintiffs' favor."
According to the complaint, all three signed long-term contracts locking in HBM supply, Samsung and SK Hynix reportedly refused long-term agreements with major conventional DRAM customers, and Micron announced on Dec. 3, 2025, that it would close Crucial, its 30-year-old consumer brand. Those moves, which she called "difficult if not impossible to unwind," plus the failure to expand despite record prices, "are the sort that at the pleading stage, it appears no reasonable firm would make without a prior agreement," she wrote.
She applied similar reasoning to allegations that Samsung and Micron delayed or halted new DRAM plants, citing the Ninth Circuit's 1990 Petroleum Products decision that "Changes in refinery capacity are not readily reversible." "The Court views the commitment to build or not build manufacturing capacity as analogous," she wrote. She also counted the companies' alleged same-quarter 2025 decisions to wind down DDR4 as a plus factor, citing the complaint's allegation that "older, slower DDR4 came to cost more per gigabyte than newer, faster DDR5."
Judge Wise found that near-identical customer-vetting practices all three companies allegedly adopted in January 2026 "strongly support a plausible inference of conspiracy." She rejected the companies' "obvious and non-conspiratorial explanation" of booming AI demand, noting that the vetting began after the AI boom and the HBM pivot were underway.
The court gave no weight to three other arguments. Judge Wise held that investor statements, including Micron's 2022 reference to "supply discipline," did not distinguish the case from DRAM II. Though the conference-meeting allegations went beyond those in DRAM II, she wrote that DRAM II "seemingly all but forecloses a trial court from giving any weight to trade conference meetings" absent specifics, and she called the market's oligopolistic structure "neutral in the analysis."
According to the complaint, the companies and two other manufacturers formed a criminal cartel to fix DRAM prices from 1998 to 2002; Samsung paid a $300 million fine, Hynix paid $185 million, and Micron received immunity for cooperating as a whistleblower. Judge Wise, like the DRAM II court, rejected the argument that the now-20-year-old history should not count.
At the hearing, the companies quoted the late Judge Posner's line that competitors in concentrated markets "watch each other like hawks." Judge Wise wrote: "But Plaintiffs' allegations go beyond hawks watching each other; their contentions are more akin to hawks flying in formation – an activity that hawks ordinarily do not do."
The plaintiffs are represented by Bathaee Dunne LLP. Samsung is represented by O'Melveny & Myers LLP, SK Hynix by Cleary Gottlieb Steen & Hamilton LLP, and Micron by Gibson, Dunn & Crutcher LLP.
Judge Wise ordered the companies to answer by Dec. 7, 2026. Because the court had reset argument by about three weeks, she also granted the companies' unopposed administrative motion to continue early case deadlines and reset the initial case management conference for Jan. 12, 2027, at 9 a.m.

Bitcoin Plummets 24% in 46 Days as Crypto Falls on Rising Inflation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
00:52:00 UTC
09:52:00 KST

Bitcoin Plummets 24% in 46 Days as Crypto Falls on Rising Inflation (fastcompany.com) 53
			
		 	
				October 3:  $84,680
Today:  $64,108
Bitcoin dropped 24.2% in 46 days.  And it "fell more than 4% on Friday to $68,500," reports Fortune, "wiping out gains built over the past three months and casting doubt on whether its bear market was ever really over."
The asset also faces weaker investor appetite, according to crypto analytics provider CryptoQuant. The firm's demand indicator recorded its weakest reading since December 2023, while Bitcoin exchange-traded funds became net sellers again.   The recent downturn has also extended to other parts of the crypto market. That includes Tether's USDT, the largest cryptocurrency pegged to the U.S. dollar, whose total value shrank by $7.7 billion over the past two months, according to CryptoQuant, a sign that less cash is available in crypto markets.
This time around, inflation has been the main culprit. Tuesday's Consumer Price Index report showed prices rose 0.4% in October, more than economists expected. Even stripping out food and energy, inflation ran hotter than forecast. Traders now overwhelmingly expect the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates by a quarter percentage point in December, with some betting on an even bigger increase.   "Because these are non-yielding assets, and we expect inflation to rise... people are chucking stuff like gold and Bitcoin to find... government bonds that are going up, providing you [with] income," Chris Beauchamp, chief market analyst at financial technology company IG Group, told Fortune...
Heavy borrowing and spending on AI have created what 21Shares calls an "AI black hole," drawing money toward the sector and away from assets like Bitcoin and gold. But Bitcoin remains "joined at the hip" with tech stocks, according to Beauchamp. That leaves it in an awkward position: It faces competition for investment dollars when enthusiasm for AI runs high, yet can get dragged down when that enthusiasm fades.  
Fast Company points out Bitcoin "remains down more than 20% since the beginning of 2026."
[Nvidia] has seen its shares lose more than 27% of their value over the past month.  The tech sell-off seems to have dragged down the crypto markets, too, as the two sectors are, on some levels, intermingled... On top of that, outflows from Bitcoin funds may also be contributing to the slide. More than $1 billion in leveraged crypto positions were closed in recent days, which may have added to declining demand, affecting BTC prices.

US loses measles-free status: Pan American Health Organization (2025)
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
00:48:11 UTC
09:48:11 KST

Washington, D.C., 10 November 2025 (PAHO) –The Pan American Health Organization (PAHO) announced today that the Region of the Americas has lost its verification as free from endemic measles transmission.
The decision follows the conclusion of the PAHO Measles, Rubella, and Congenital Rubella Syndrome Elimination Regional Monitoring and Re-Verification Commission, which met in Mexico City from 4 to 7 November 2025 to review the epidemiological situation across the region. The Director of PAHO, Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, received and agreed with the Commission’s report indicating that endemic transmission of measles has been reestablished in Canada, where the virus has circulated for at least 12 months.
As a result, the Americas, which was the first region in the world to eliminate measles twice, has now lost its measles-free status. All other countries continue to maintain their measles elimination status.
“This loss represents a setback—but it is also reversible,” said Dr. Barbosa, “Until measles is eliminated worldwide, our Region will continue to face the risk of reintroduction and spread of the virus among unvaccinated or under-vaccinated populations. However, as we have demonstrated before, with political commitment, regional cooperation, and sustained vaccination, the Region can once again interrupt transmission and reclaim this collective achievement.”
Current situation
As of 7 November 2025, 12,596 confirmed measles cases have been reported across ten countries (approximately 95% of the Region’s cases in Canada, Mexico and US), a 30-fold increase compared to 2024. Twenty-eight deaths have been recorded: 23 in Mexico, 3 in the United States, and 2 in Canada.
Active outbreaks are ongoing in Canada, Mexico, the United States, Bolivia, Brazil, Paraguay, and Belize, mostly triggered by imported cases. Transmission has primarily affected under-vaccinated communities, with 89% of cases occurring in unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccination status. Children under 1 year of age are the most affected, followed by those aged one to four years.
Canada’s measles outbreak began in October 2024 in the province of New Brunswick and has since spread nationwide, with more than 5,000 confirmed cases reported nationwide. Although declining, transmission persists in Alberta, British Columbia, Manitoba, and Saskatchewan.
Measles is highly contagious. A single infected person can transmit it to up to 18 others. Severe complications can include pneumonia, encephalitis, blindness, and death. Outbreaks also disrupt daily life and place additional pressure on health systems.
Vaccination remains the most effective means of protection. Over the past 25 years, the measles vaccine has prevented more than six million deaths across the Americas —and an estimated 15 million deaths over the last 50 years. Nevertheless, in 2024, regional coverage for the second dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine (MMR2) averaged 79%—well below the 95% needed to prevent outbreaks. In addition, only 31% of countries reached 95% or more coverage for the first dose, and just 20% achieved that level for the second dose.
PAHO’s response and recommendations
PAHO continues to provide technical support to countries to strengthen surveillance, laboratory diagnosis, outbreak response, and vaccination campaigns. Experts have been deployed to Mexico, Argentina, and Bolivia, and the organization is monitoring risks in Belize, Brazil and Paraguay.
Dr. Barbosa emphasized: “Every case we prevent, every outbreak we stop saves lives, protects families, and makes communities healthier. Today, rather than lamenting the loss of a regional status, we call on all countries to redouble their efforts to strengthen vaccination rates, surveillance, and timely response to suspected cases—reaching every corner of the Americas. As a Region, we have eliminated measles twice. We can do it a third time.”
RVC strongly encourages countries to:
- Continue strengthening surveillance, immunization and outbreak rapid response activities for every suspect case.
- Expand and consolidate electronic immunization registries across all countries, following PAHO’s established guidelines.
- Maintain sustainable laboratory surveillance by allocating sufficient financial and human resources.
- For suspected cases with clinical and epidemiological risk factors and a negative measles IgM result (with specimens collected within 0–3 days after rash onset), perform PCR testing to confirm or rule out infection.
- Implement all recommendations down to the local level.
- Document outbreak closure following the Regional Framework guidelines.
Looking forward
The Americas have previously faced setbacks, including temporary loss of measles elimination status in Venezuela (2018) and Brazil (2019). The region regained elimination status in 2024 through coordinated regional action.
To regain measles elimination status, a country must demonstrate interruption of endemic transmission for at least 12 consecutive months, supported by comprehensive vaccination, surveillance, and outbreak-response data.
Canada will now present and implement an action plan under PAHO’s regional framework, focused on boosting immunization coverage, reinforcing surveillance systems, and ensuring rapid outbreak response to stop endemic transmission and regaining measles elimination status.
“Cooperation, solidarity, and science are stronger than any virus—because when we work together, we protect the health and lives of all people in the Americas,” Dr. Barbosa concluded.

====================================================================================================

An overview of Jensen Huang’s messy days
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
00:09:10 UTC
09:09:10 KST

This is The Stepback, a weekly newsletter breaking down one essential story from the tech world. For more on Jensen Huang’s resignation and the fallout from Nvidia’s leadership crisis, follow Terrence O’Brien. The Stepback arrives in our subscribers’ inboxes on Sunday at 8AM ET. Opt in for The Stepback here.
An overview of Jensen Huang’s messy days
Things moved fast for the now-former CEO of Nvidia.
An overview of Jensen Huang’s messy days
Things moved fast for the now-former CEO of Nvidia.
How it started
On October 29th, The New York Times reported that Nvidia co-founder and showman CEO Jensen Huang appeared to be the target of a Chinese spy plot. Any major tech CEO potentially being compromised by the Chinese government is concerning — especially Jensen Huang. “If you could draw the network diagram of the entire US AI ecosystem, Nvidia would be at the center of that,” Kyle Chan, a foreign policy fellow at the Brookings Institution’s John L. Thornton China Center, previously told The Verge. “If you could draw the diagram of the entire Nvidia ecosystem, a single man is standing at the heart of all of that.”
The Times reported that Jensen Huang had developed a “close relationship” with Alice Huang (no relation), an executive at Singapore-based data center company Megaspeed. Alice Huang had been on the American government’s radar for some time, perhaps as early as 2024. Her company was investigated by the US Commerce Department over suspicions it was illegally smuggling Nvidia’s AI chips into China in violation of US government trade restrictions. Singapore authorities confiscated her passport back in June of 2025, eventually barring her from the country, after she reportedly gave “inaccurate information” to police. And perhaps more concerningly, some US officials believed she was passing information, possibly about Nvidia’s designs or the White House’s deliberations over trade restrictions, directly back to Beijing.
This made her relationship with Jensen Huang concerning for multiple reasons. Nvidia’s chips are considered crucial to America’s AI superiority and a matter of national security by the White House. The government has restricted the sale of Nvidia’s most advanced chips to China since the Biden administration. But Jensen Huang has routinely pushed to loosen those trade restrictions. Meanwhile, he had gotten quite cozy with President Trump, once even putting him on speakerphone on-stage at the All-In Summit.
Then last week, things escalated quickly.
On Friday, November 6th, the Times reported that Alice Huang was specifically recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security to spy on Nvidia’s CEO. Though, there is some disagreement among US intelligence officials over the strength of evidence tying Alice Huang to Beijing. While the paper did not confirm it at the time, rumors suggested the “close relationship” between Jensen Huang and Alice Huang was romantic in nature.
On Sunday, November 8th, Jensen Huang admitted to having an extramarital affair, but did not name the Megaspeed exec and alleged Chinese spy specifically. Nvidia’s board announced that it was launching an investigation into its CEO shortly after.
Many believed that Jensen Huang would survive the scandal. His company’s importance to America’s booming AI industry and his crucial role at the company seemed to make him nearly untouchable. He was, after all, the only CEO the company had ever known with no clear and obvious succession plan in place, and he sat atop an unusual corporate structure with around 60 direct reports. (These numbers are slightly outdated, but according to the Harvard Business Review, 10 to 20 is far more common among Fortune 500 companies, and Tim Cook reportedly had 17 in 2015, which was deemed “a lot” by Business Insider.)
Then on Wednesday, November 11th, The New York Times reported that Jensen Huang had a second affair — this one with one of his direct reports, Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice president of health care. Whether that was the last straw or if they were afraid it would be merely the tip of the iceberg, the board was ready to push Jensen Huang out, according to reports circulating that evening. According to Bloomberg, he had accepted his fate Wednesday evening reportedly looking “broken and shattered” and telling people, “I’m a piece of shit.”
On Thursday, November 12th, Nvidia announced that Jensen Huang had resigned as CEO of Nvidia.
How it’s going
CFO Colette Kress was immediately named interim CEO, and acknowledged to employees that this has been an “extraordinarily difficult period.” Harvey Jones was also named the first board chair in the company’s history, already signaling there would be some changes to the corporate structure. In a memo to employees, Jones said the company would “search for a permanent successor looking at both internal and external candidates.”
But the destabilizing impact was immediate. Nvidia’s stock closed at an all-time high of $239.24 on October 6th. It closed at $162.85 on November 13th. It’s hard to overstate the importance of Nvidia to the US’s AI arms race with China, and the industry in general. The company has invested $99 billion in various AI labs, startups, and neo clouds. Plus has announced plans to mobilize over $500 billion in financing for building out AI data centers and other infrastructure.
Even before Jensen Huang had resigned, Nvidia was already facing a shareholder lawsuit claiming the company had deceived investors. Jensen Huang and the company’s spokespeople insisted that the relationship with Alice Huang and Megaspeed was “completely normal and not unusual.” Only days later, Jensen Huang admitted to an extramarital affair that raised “national security concerns” without naming anyone specifically. According to Reuters, Jensen Huang misled several Nvidia leaders, “including general counsel Tim Teter and chief marketing officer Alison Wagonfeld” about the nature of his relationship with Alice Huang.
Many in the AI industry and Silicon Valley jumped to Jensen Huang’s defense. Epic Games CEO Tim Sweeney called it a “sad day for NVIDIA, developers, America, and all humanity,” in a particularly hyperbolic post. Elon Musk said on X that it would “be an honor” to hire Jensen Huang. Many of Nvidia’s employees even voiced their support to “bring back THE boss,” on an internal post.
Some even speculated that Jensen Huang was the victim of a vast conspiracy, whether that was a coup by some faction within Nvidia, or perhaps the goal of China all along had simply been to chase him from the CEO chair.
By Thursday night, Reuters reported a third affair, this one also with an Nvidia employee, though no further information has emerged.
What happens next
First and foremost for Nvidia, it must find a new CEO. It’s possible that Kress eventually ceases to be “interim” CEO, but other names, including Apple’s hardware chief Johny Srouji, have also been rumored. (Donald Trump believes they should hire Tim Cook, but to call that unlikely would be an understatement.)
It’s also possible that Nvidia will undergo a dramatic reorganization. Whoever takes over as CEO is unlikely to keep the 60 direct reports of Jensen Huang. With a first-ever board chair in Jones, it’s also possible that the board of directors will play a larger role in the company going forward.
The board’s investigation into Jensen Huang that began on November 8th is ongoing. The scandal is likely to drag on for some time. With the possibility that Jensen Huang will have to testify before Congress, the company may choose to be proactive about disclosing any additional information publicly.
The incoming Democratic majority is expected to come down harder on Silicon Valley than the current Republican Congress. While it’s unclear just how far the new House and Senate will go in trying to rein in the tech industry and its billionaire leaders, it’s possible it will try to make an example of Nvidia as an early act of strength. If Nvidia is found to have been turning a blind eye to companies like Megaspeed, which are suspected of funneling advanced chips to China, it could face civil or criminal penalties. And it seems increasingly likely that stricter trade restrictions will be implemented on Nvidia’s chips, which could increase tensions with China in an already costly trade war.
There is a possibility that Jensen Huang could face criminal charges. In May, he claimed that Nvidia had “zero percent” market share in China due to stringent export restrictions. But shortly after, Culper Research published a report claiming that in reality over 20 percent of the company’s 2026 compute revenue was from China, though that number has not been independently verified. The report cited “illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries” as the primary source of those revenues. Among the largest of those intermediaries, according to Culper Research, was Megaspeed, which Nvidia repeatedly vouched for to the US government.
If proof is established that the CEO was knowingly helping Megaspeed or any other companies to illegally divert restricted chips to China, he could face criminal charges. And, as The Verge’s Elizabeth Lopatto put it, “under a functioning legal system, [he] could actually go to jail.”
There is the chance that members of the current administration are also tied up in this scandal, as it’s unclear how much the White House knew and when.
Considering Nvidia’s sizable investments, borrowing to build data centers, and the importance of its hardware, there are tons of open questions about how this impacts not just the AI industry, but the American economy as a whole. Nvidia is essentially propping up a large part of the tech industry and, if money stops flowing out of its coffers, it could drag a lot of other companies down with it.
There are also open questions about how this impacts the American public’s perception of the AI industry. The new Democratic majority was elected in part by campaigning against AI companies and the explosion of data center buildout across the country. That one of its more prominent CEOs is now embroiled in a spying scandal and multiple extramarital affairs probably isn’t going to endear the industry to the public.
By the way
- Megaspeed was the largest buyer of Nvidia chips in Southeast Asia, but when Singaporean newspaper The Straits Times visited its office in October of 2025, it found “no signs of activity.”
- Megaspeed is just one of multiple companies that Nvidia has been approved to sell chips with suspicious offices. The New York Times described New Matrix’s headquarters as “a hollow gray shell with wires dangling from the ceiling where lights should be.”
- Jensen Huang’s connections to Super Micro are also under scrutiny. Earlier this year, one of its founders, an employee, and a contractor for the company were indicted by the Justice Department for conspiring to violate American export controls and divert Nvidia chips to China.
Read this
- While a lot has changed, it’s still worth reading the Times’ first big report from October 29th that lays the groundwork for the eventual deluge of international intrigue and infidelity that ultimately led to Jensen Huang’s downfall.
- The New York Times paints a fascinating portrait of Alice Huang who now finds herself at the center of an international espionage scandal.
- The Decoder interview with Kyle Chan, a foreign policy fellow at the Brookings Institution’s John L. Thornton China Center, digs into what China hoped to get out of Jensen Huang.

Where was Nvidia’s security team?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-15
00:08:07 UTC
09:08:07 KST

Outsiders had little reason to notice when Nvidia’s longtime chief security officer and head of product security stepped down in August. Just two and a half months later, CEO Jensen Huang himself left the company over growing questions about his extramarital relationships — starting with an executive at Megaspeed, a Singapore data center company that U.S. investigators suspect of funneling Nvidia chips to China, then widening to reports about two separate affairs with Nvidia employees.
At the time, David Reber, who spent four years as CSO and seven at Nvidia overall, said he was leaving for health reasons. “This wasn’t a pivot I had planned. But I know what needs to come first: family and health,” he wrote on LinkedIn in late August, disclosing that he’d been diagnosed with young-onset Parkinson’s more than two years ago.
Reber’s replacement, Saša Zdjelar, previously spent 17 years at ExxonMobil in Houston, leaving as a senior IT manager before spending one year at Salesforce and several years at the cybersecurity company ReversingLabs. Zdjelar didn’t respond to questions Thursday about the transition or what the security team knew.
Nothing public connects Reber’s or Zdjelar’s role changes to the Megaspeed matter, but it seems fair to wonder what Nvidia’s security team knew about the government’s scrutiny of Megaspeed and about Huang’s relationships, and whether anyone raised concerns. Reber didn’t respond to questions Thursday. Nvidia also has not answered our related questions.
Relationships between a CEO and his employees typically fall to HR, legal and the board, not security. But security specialists say any affair — even one inside the office — can give a foreign intelligence service leverage over an exec who wants it kept quiet.
Still, spotting the clues isn’t easy. The way intelligence services collect information, much of it through everyday conversation rather than hacking, makes it especially difficult for security teams to identify risks, according to retired senior intelligence officer Nicholas Eftimiades, who says he has trained more than 1,000 corporate security professionals.
Some people inside Nvidia clearly knew parts of the picture. Nvidia’s top lawyer was engaged with the government’s Megaspeed inquiry more than a year ago, The New York Times reported earlier this week. (Nvidia has said it regularly contacts officials.) Reports about Huang’s relationships with employees suggest colleagues also knew of them, even if the behavior is not expressly forbidden by Nvidia’s code of conduct. The Times cited five Nvidia employees who said he was involved with a vice president who reported directly to him; Reuters, citing two people, later reported a second relationship. What’s unclear is whether any of that reached security.
Even if it had, Eftimiades, who wrote Chinese Espionage Operations and Tactics in 2020, told TechCrunch that a security team’s resources are often limited. For one thing, they’re often underfunded because, unlike some other cost centers, they don’t drive revenue. It’s astonishingly common for CEOs to answer a security team’s warnings with “not a nickel more than compliance,” he said.
Many companies also define security overly narrowly, specialists say. In an opinion piece in The Hill, Don Aviv, CEO of the corporate investigations firm Interfor International, and author Jeremy Hurewitz, wrote, “Too many companies still approach security primarily as a matter of protecting facilities, preventing cyberattacks, and responding to physical threats.” The two argued that “[s]ecurity teams need to pay greater attention to insider threats, including the cultivation of executives and employees by foreign intelligence services, suspicious business relationships, conflicts of interest and attempts to obtain sensitive information through trusted personal contacts.”
It would be surprising if Nvidia’s security team didn’t have a more expansive remit than most companies given its scale and business partners, but it’s possible.
In fairness, some of it is simply beyond what any team can do. Speaking generally, not about Huang, Eftimiades offered a hypothetical in which a CEO complains that he can’t do something because “I’m fighting a board who doesn’t want to do this.” That tells an intelligence asset who the obstacle is. The service then asks, “Who’s the sticking point here?” and looks for ways into that person’s email and phone.
What a rival intelligence service wants is often less technology than policy insight, including who’s pushing to change export rules and who the biggest blocker is. “Intel world tends to be like cancer,” he said. “Once it gets into the body, it looks to spread and looks to reach out.”
The playbook isn’t new, Eftimiades added, “particularly for China.” Any executive dealing with the country “will tell you that they’ve been approached.” He said there are also “tons of diplomat stories” of U.S. diplomats walking into a hotel room to find a woman on the bed. He said what’s newer compared with Soviet era spying is the relationships these services cultivate now often last years, not weeks.
Technology can augment that human intelligence, Eftimiades said, pointing to IMSI catchers as one example. The devices mimic a cell tower so that nearby phones connect to them. A basic version can cost about $1,000 and fit in a suitcase, he said, though government-grade models cost far more, and there were a couple of cases involving Chinese espionage where these factored in last year.
Eftimiades says no one knows yet if the devices were used against Huang, whose phone was presumably hardened such that compromises could be detected, if not stopped altogether. But what Nvidia’s security team knew, and whether it could have acted on it, will only become clearer as the board’s independent investigation and federal inquiries proceed.
In the meantime, Eftimiades said he hopes the whole episode is “a wake-up call” for U.S. industry, which he said is mostly “clueless” about China’s tactics. “We have lost trillions [of dollars] in intellectual property,” is his guess. “Whatever we think we’re doing isn’t working.”

America Officially Loses Its Measles-elimination Status After Continued Spread of the Disease
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-14
23:37:00 UTC
08:37:00 KST

America Officially Loses Its Measles-elimination Status After Continued Spread of the Disease (msn.com) 134
			
		 	
				Reuters reports:
The Pan American Health Organization has revoked the United States' measles-elimination status, ending a 26-year run after determining the country failed to halt continuous transmission of the highly contagious virus for more than a year, the Wall Street Journal reported on Friday...
The reported decision by the Americas branch of the World Health Organization comes as the US battles the highest number of measles cases it has seen in more than three decades. Some 7,500 people in the US have been infected with measles and 13 have died since 2025. That period coincides with falling immunization rates and rising vaccine hesitancy under US Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has for years spread doubts, without scientific evidence, about the safety of measles vaccines...
Canada, Spain and the UK recently lost measles-elimination status after review by WHO experts....
"It is a fully preventable disease. It is unconscionable that we have this amount of measles," said Noel Brewer, a public health professor at the University of North Carolina...  William Moss, an epidemiology professor at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, said loss of measles elimination was a hit to the United States' reputation as a public health powerhouse... For the average American, he added, it means that the United States will continue to have large outbreaks, putting people at risk of disease, hospitalization and death.
